ANONYMOUS GIFT

FURNISHED ROOM BY JAMES NOBLE GIFFORD 

TR. GIFFORD has undertaken for the first time in modern fiction to treat a theme which is delicate, yet an important motive in the lives of thousands of men and women.

Tom Smith comes to New York with great ambitions but very little money. He takes a room in a cheap boarding house, off Broadway. The house displays a large sign: 

THIS IS A RESPECTABLE HOUSE. 

NO LADIES ALLOWED IN GENTLEMEN'S ROOMS. 

NO GENTLEMEN ALLOWED IN LADIES' ROOMS. 

But this does not deter Mrs. Crowley, in search of booze and excitement, from knocking on Tom's door. 

The first time nothing comes of it. But there is another time which leaves its physical consequences on Tom's life in no uncertain fashion. 

Young and unsophisticated, Tom is bewildered. He wanders around for days afraid even to tell his only friend, Alfred. Finally, Alfred worms the truth out of him and brings him to a doctor.

On a bench in Bryant Park, Tom meets Anne Dale, a girl of good family and breeding, who invites him to visit her home. More in a spirit of resentment than friendship, he goes, and a strong attachment springs up between them. Because of his condition, Tom breaks off the relationship without informing Anne of the truth. 

For a long time he is almost entirely alone, discouraged and anti-social, but finally he finds a compromise within himself of his resentment at his fate, and there is a reconciliation with Anne.

The author has succeeded admirably in presenting his story without deference to Mrs. Grundy, but at the same time without shocking the sensibilities of the reader willing to face a common fact in a straightforward way.

CHAPTER ONE 

E followed her carefully down the dark, narrow hall until she reached the end where she twisted open a door. "Four dollars." There was a take it or leave it tone to the voice.

He walked into the room and she followed him, jangling a heavy bunch of keys in her pocket and looking at him with the direct, level glance of her type. He caught a glimpse of her expression in the bleary mirror over the wash basin, but he didn't need to see it in order to feel it. They always thrust you into a room like that and then followed and stared at the back of your head until you couldn't keep silent any longer and had to turn around and face them. It was the most annoying part of the whole business. 

You always rang a bell hopefully, with a wild dream that it would be something different; that a pretty girl would come to the door, or a young fellow would pop out with a wide smile of welcome, or else, perhaps, a little old lady with white hair and a cap, leaning on a cane. That was the special type for moving pictures and plays, but how little they really showed about life. 

In reality landladies were always short and puffy and blowsy, with a mushy mouth and hard eyes, and always wore a bungalow apron, or whatever one called the shapeless one-piece dress, seemingly just in order to jangle keys in the front pocket. That noise was the barometer of the women's feelings. 

A renewed rattle of the keys made him turn. The

FURNISHED ROOM 

woman spoke sharply as if he was annoying her and keeping her from something more important. "Well? This is the only one I have and there are very few around here at the price. And on the parlor floor, too. And running water. May God strike me dead if the hot water wouldn't scald you even in the middle of the night."

There was a pause while she caught his wandering eye and managed to hold it an instant before he could draw away. Then her tongue ran on more determinedly. "You won't find anything around here like it. It's quiet, and right across the street from Roxy's, too. One subway at the end of the street, one around the corner, the El at the other end, and busses in front of the door. And the Broadway and Seventh Avenue cars, too. I don't know what more you could want." There was a savage bite to the last remark.

He gave another glance around the room. The eye doesn't travel far in New York hall bedrooms. At the near end some hooks with a shabby curtain over them; that was the clothes closet. Then a narrow iron bed with a patched not-quite-clean counterpane, and a bureau which touched the bed on one side and the window sill on the other. Facing the bureau the wash basin with just enough room between them so that when you bent over the basin to wash your face you sat on the edge of the bureau. The window was tall to match the high ceiling. 

Across it hung a tattered shade which looked the inspiration of the original designer of Swiss cheese. It obviously wouldn't go up and probably only came down when it fell down. On the ceiling were the crumbling remains of an ornamental plaster cornice, a sad reminder of the old days of the house when this had been part of an elaborate "back parlor," that cherished room of old New York which will soon be as extinct as the

FURNISHED ROOM 

3 "atrium" of the Romans. But the wall-paper encouraged no such speculative dreaming. It was a ghastly red with a nondescript design except where sickly bits of an earlier green showed through slits. And that was all.

He sensed the next interrogative "Well" before it came and drawing a bill fold from his pocket paid the woman four dollars, a two and two ones. She tried to look into his wallet as he did so, but he was prepared for that and turned his broad back so she couldn't see. They always tried to look, probably as a method of gauging the financial rating of the prospective tenant. It was really very simple. What you didn't have in your wallet you didn't have at all. People who looked for hall rooms didn't have bank accounts. Knowing the psychology, the man didn't care to have her see that a single dollar bill remained when he had paid her. 

She took the money, asking his name dully. "Thomas Smith."

He watched her as he spoke. He knew there would be a sudden grin as if they were now sharers of a secret. He always looked for it and he was never disappointed. At first it had bothered him but now he had come almost to expect it as an end to the sordid comedy of room renting. Usually there was some remark by the landlady. 

This time she merely turned away. "A rather common name, isn't it?" he mocked. "Especially in this part of the city. But very likely you won't be having much mail." Her voice was like acid.

She spoke without turning her head, and was gone. 

With a smile he realized that she probably lived in the room next to him and certainly expected the worst of him. The casualness with which his name was accepted as false was amusing but, with reservations, she was right. He wouldn't be having much mail unless you

FURNISHED ROOM 

counted rejected manuscripts as such. Even then one of the "Confession" magazines had added a line to a rejection slip, saying, "We call your special attention to the fact that while we do not use the names of authors in our magazines it is essential that real names be used in correspondence with us." He had had a good laugh that day, and had managed to refrain from writing them that his father had been John Smith and had always been called "Captain."

He took off his coat, sat on the edge of the bed, and looked around. So this time it was a red room. The last one had been a blue room and equally ghastly. There was little to choose between blue painted walls and red papered ones. The other room had had a lower ceiling and been up three flights of stairs. Also the floor had slanted so that anything you dropped rolled promptly under the bed. But on the other hand there had been more sunlight and a good steam radiator, while here only an exposed steam pipe ran through the room. He shivered a little. The room was cold now in the middle of a mild day. Luckily he didn't need or even like a really warm room.

He lay back on the bed after slipping off his shoes, doing it carefully to avoid seeing the hole in the sock which he knew was there. When you got rather used to holes in socks they only bothered when you allowed yourself to see them. He had learned that long ago.

He turned on his side and pulled the blankets at the foot of the bed up over him. He could sleep until late in the afternoon when he must remember to make some phone calls. At any rate this bed was longer than the other had been, even if it was narrower. And what if he did feel hungry? Was there not a French proverb which said, "He who sleeps eats?" A wise race, the

FURNISHED ROOM 

French. They knew how to kill two birds with one stone. Maybe that was a French proverb too and if it wasn't-well, it didn't matter. He breathed slowly, regularly, like a trained nurse taking gas or ether, and there was no other sound.

It was perhaps an hour later when a loud banging on the door shattered the quietness of the room and Tom sat up with startled eyes. The banging came again and this time he realized a name was being shouted. By the time he had disengaged himself from the blanket, which clung to his legs in the stupid way of blankets, and reached the door crumpled and disheveled, the banging had begun a third time. He pulled the door open, and as it came it dragged the shouter who stood clutching the knob. Before he could say anything, a woman had fallen upon him. Her weight thrust him back and his arms closed instinctively around her to keep her from falling. At the same instant she flung both arms convulsively around his neck. "John-what the hell took you so long to open the door? Gimme a drink." 

With a grin he looked at what he held. Her face was buried in his neck but her hair was darkly reddish, very obviously a result of choice rather than any caprice of nature. Her arms were white and smooth as they clasped him, her body softly yielding in his arms, and scantily encased in an old black silk kimono which set off the whiteness of her flesh. Her breasts were pressed firmly against him. The legs were bare and the feet thrust into ragged pink slippers that had been decorated with feathers at some vague time in the past.

His grin widened as he turned his mind to the social rather than the physical aspect of the moment. It was an amusing thing to happen within an hour of his arrival and while his bag still reposed in the check-room

then flung back over her shoulder, "You'd better read the sign in the hall. It was meant for you."

A head popped back. "You needn't ask. Maybe you're his brother. He was John Smith, of course." There was the bang of a door as she entered the next room. 

Tom threw back his head and laughed. It was quite easy to understand Mrs. Tillinghast's feelings about the name Smith. And strangely enough for her feelings toward Mrs. Crowley were clearly more of protection than accusation. She had warned him away from her with the threat of a pugnacious husband. With the name 

Jim Crowley he might well be that, even though his wife had demanded, "What's in a name?" Think of 

Roseanna. What a lovely name for one who fell into your room half nude demanding gin and reeking of it.

The lines of drink were set upon her face even while her body still held up white, and firm, and alluring. 

With a deep sigh he began pulling on his shoes, twisting the sock so the hole in the toe came in a slightly less uncomfortable position. Then he shook his clothes into position much as a dog might shake his collar, and putting on his coat started out. In the hall he stopped to read the sign which had been recommended to him. It was about twelve by eighteen inches in size, with white letters on a blue enamel base. It read: 

This is a Respectable house.

No Ladies allowed in Gentlemen's rooms. 

No Gentlemen allowed in Ladies' rooms. 

If Rent is not Paid on day specified 

Rooms will be rented next day to first applicant.

Signed-The Owner

With a laugh he ran down the front brownstone steps. Outside Roxy's the crowd was lined up waiting to get into the afternoon show. Many people turned to stare at him as he stood laughing, then glanced disapprovingly up at the house from which he had come. 

Their glances proclaimed to the world their absolute doubt of the first statement proclaimed so dogmatically by the blue and white sign.

II Wally poured out his mournful saga of Madge's desertion, ending with, "And I still miss her-the lousy bitch." It was boring to Tom but the whiskey was good and filled his hungry body with a warm glow. When the first bottle was empty Wally brought out a second and it was clear he had money for whiskey if nothing else.

Tom lay back in the bed with his coat and shoes off while Wally's voice ran droningly on. Wally didn't require any answer; he just wanted someone to talk to. 

Tom was really hungry but when you had enough whiskey that didn't matter and your body got all warm as if flooded with sunshine. He smiled calmly as he wriggled the big toe thrust through the hole in his sock. 

When your body was all glowing inside what did that matter? A quarter would buy socks but it wouldn't buy that warm rosy feeling. 

Wally suddenly stopped talking and stared at Tom's foot. "Hey-those the only socks you got?" 

Tom shrugged. "It doesn't matter. Go on, tell me some more about Madge." "The hell with Madge!" He sat glumly a moment. "Say, where you stayin' now?” "On 50th Street. I have a room there-red wall paper.

FURNISHED ROOM 

woke up with a little start, his mind strangely alert, his senses keen as they sometimes were after drinking. 

He was vaguely conscious that the door was opening, then it closed swiftly and there was a faint sound of heavy breathing. He lay quite still, not quite willing to trust his eyes and ears on the first night in a strange dark room. Then he caught a cloying breath of perfume and knew he was right. He raised himself a little on his arm and spoke softly. "Aren't you a rather late visitor?"

The shadowy figure approached the bed. In the eerie darkness it seemed to be a long time coming. And then a strange hushed whisper reached him as if from far away: "Ss-sh! The cops are after me!"

It was a woman, a young one by the sound of her voice. She bent over him and he could hardly resist an insane desire to burst out laughing. It was all too absolutely mad. A side glance at Wally assured him that that worthy still slept as deeply as his regular breathing indicated. No strange, husky-voiced females pursued by cops interrupted his slumbers. Or, perhaps, he was quite used to them. He smiled at the idea. It was too ridiculous. "Don't laugh. They'll be here in a minute now."

The husky voice interrupted his speculation with its soft penetrating insistency. "I gotta get away. Listen! 

They're coming."

It was all nonsensical and he was about to inquire what sort of stuff she drank when there were quick footsteps and the door of the room was flung open with a bang. A vivid finger of light flooded him from the doorway. It was done so quickly he caught his breath, but a side glance assured him the figure was gone from the bed-side.

TOM CHAPTER THREE 

OM SMITH lay stretched out naked on his bed cooling off after a bath. He had had a long day tramping to the theatrical offices and tutoring agencies without any result and, as he expressed it to an acquaintance he met outside the Earl Carroll theatre, "All 

I have to do now is live until Monday."

The week-end was never a thing to look forward to, but something to get through without mishap, like the period of time you spent under water in a deep dive. 

Through the week you went here or there and tried this or that. You talked to some people and argued with others, and somehow the hours went by. Over the weekends it was different. People all seemed to have something definite to do, somewhere definite to go. They didn't casually invite you to a meal, or say, "Drop up to the room this evening and have a drink,” or any such welcome pleasantry. On Saturdays Tom felt that he lived in a district that was too gay; that presumed you wanted to be gay too, and, moreover, took it for granted that you had the price to be gay. He would walk along 

Broadway on a Saturday afternoon or evening and feel sure that everyone on that densely crowded thoroughfare was going to the theatre. Of course it wasn't true and you knew it wasn't, but you couldn't escape the feeling no matter how you reasoned with yourself. 

There were always so many plays you wanted to see. 

You read about them and you passed the entrances to the theatres, glittering entrances that seemed to lure you like so many magic dream caverns. 17

FURNISHED ROOM 

Tom often wondered how many times he had gone without food to go to the theatre. Indeed he had seen very few plays that had not entailed some privation. 

Maybe that was why plays were so dear to him. He remembered the first time he had seen "He Who Gets 

Slapped" and had almost fainted away from hunger.

And then there was always his book experience in 

Florida. He hadn't eaten in four days and was prostrated with a slight touch of fever. He had read Knut 

Hamsun's "Hunger" between spells of delirium alone in his room to which no one came for days at a time. He could never forget the play or the book. Maybe you had to buy your priceless memories like that, make your body pay.

Tom stretched his legs out wider on the bed and looked critically down his powerfully muscled body, bare and smooth, glowing in the glare of the single unshaded electric bulb. Maybe it would be cooler if he put on a pair of drawers at least and pulled up the shade that covered the open window. That was one bad feature of parlor floor room. You gained in escaping stairs and in having a high ceiling, but it always seemed as if the whole world looked in at your windows. Outside of his window was the roof over the restaurant below and the landlady's son played ball there every afternoon. If the window was closed, the ball would bang up against it with a crash that brought him up like a shot, and if it were open the ball flew in and probably knocked something down or hit him. In either case the boy was sure to be overheard by his mother, who would start over the roof in pursuit, whereupon the youngster would dart through the room as the only immediate avenue of escape. So far he had only found it amusing but in time that sport would probably pale. 

But tonight he was so lonely lying there that he

FURNISHED ROOM 

19 wished the boy, Urban, would come dashing through the room. He wished he had to give a lesson to Cynthia 

Bobbs. She was a sweet child and her strange half-tamed theatrical family always cheered him up. Mrs. Bobbs, a veritable mountain of a woman, made him forget his own troubles with her quaint good humor and a philosophy of life as ponderous as herself. She buoyed him up when he needed it, when his own theory of "simple happiness simply sought" seemed to be the dream of a madman. But there was no lesson.

He turned on his side restlessly. It was so easy to be happy, then why must sheer loneliness strike such terror to his heart? He was so gay, so sure of himself with other people. Why was it he could not beguile himself with madcap gaieties? Why could he always make others laugh and laugh with them, too, until the tears rolled down his cheeks, and yet not laugh when he was alone, unless with a touch of acrid bitterness at his own folly and in scorn of his own way of life? He could sit in a restaurant with two or three others and retail anecdotes of his own experiences in search of food until a hundred people within earshot were rocking with laughter. Once in a cafeteria the manager had come over and taken the four punched checks of himself and his friends and cancelled them, saying, "I wouldn't have the heart to charge anyone who has gone hungry and still can make a whole room full of people laugh about it."

But all alone lying on the bed because there was no comfortable chair to sit in, it wasn't funny any more. 

Even the restaurant affair became more a matter of pity than of admiration for his clever way of telling a story. 

Today someone had taken him to lunch and he had been in a mad mood that had had half the place in a roar. He had said, "I always know when I tell a good story. If someone three tables away laughs I'm satisfied."

FURNISHED ROOM 

A gaily dressed chorus girl exactly three tables away had called out, "Then do talk a little louder, please. I almost didn't hear that last one.' 

It had been so funny then, but now it had lost its zest, and the remembrance merely served to remind him he hadn't had any supper; he didn't have any money; and he wouldn't get his ten dollars from Mrs. Bobbs until tomorrow night. He would pay Mrs. Tillinghast four dollars, two dollars would go for laundry, and three dollars he owed Alfred Murray. That would leave him exactly a dollar to live on for a week.

He sat up and laughed his loudest roar. That at least was funny. It left so few avenues of thought open to him. The only tenable solution was to buy some crackers and cheese-a great deal of crackers and cheese could be bought for a dollar. But he must remember to spend only eighty cents. With the remaining twenty he could buy a big bottle of citrate of magnesia and so survive. 

What a happy thought it was. 

Or he might use the dollar for a haircut and a shave and then spend the week visiting with everyone with whom he could scrape acquaintance exactly at meal time. He brushed the tears of laughter away from his eyes with the back of his hand. There were really so many nice things one could do with a dollar if only one didn't have to eat. That was the source of all the trouble. 

All science and art should therefore be devoted to finding a substitute for eating. He chuckled softly. And if they did it would probably be more expensive than food. 

He reached for a sock and had just thrust a foot into it when there was a loud bang, bang, bang on his door. "Come in," he cried in complete disregard of his nakedness, and then jumped up gaily. "Al, you old sinner! 

God I'm glad to see you."

FURNISHED ROOM 

21 "Profanely glad?" The newcomer was well dressed in a quiet untheatrical way. ee 

And more. I've been so low I couldn't even jump up to reach bottom. I took a bath and just now managed to laugh myself into strength enough to start to dress." 

Alfred Murray thrust his hands deep into his trousers pockets and looked at Tom, scanning him from the top of his brown wavy hair, down the powerful chest and arms, along the swelling thighs and calves down to the wide spread sturdy feet. "You certainly do look terribly weak. How do you manage to get about without a wheelchair and a pair of crutches?"

Tom laughed happily. All his gloomy introspection was gone now as if it had never been. "You just wait till you see Siegfried. He's a head shorter than I am but good Lord, what a body! He just picked me up and swung me around like a baby the other night. He's the first man ever made me believe in Hercules. Compared to him I'm just fat and sloppy.” "Who's Siegfried, outside of the opera?" "Oh, he's Mrs. Bobbs' private boy friend. He's a German strong man, and I mean strong." "Well, get some clothes on and we'll take a bus ride.

I need the air and badly too. I've been having a scene at home." "The usual," Tom smiled as he pulled on his trousers. 

Alfred faced him seriously. "Just a little more usual than usual, I should say. By the way, will you come to dinner Sunday? It's a very official invitation and I can promise you worlds of food at least." "Will I come? Why I'm perfectly willing to call tomorrow Sunday if you say so. We'll just give Saturday away to whoever wants it. It's never any good to me. In fact, I don't see why anyone wants it.”

"No, that won't do. It will have to be Sunday according to the New York Times and the St. James Version. 

And don't spring any such ideas on mother and father. 

You'll be a strain on them at best. I'm taking a chance, as it is." "Oh, I promise to be very good. I'll wear a clean shirt and a clean collar and wave a clean handkerchief. What else is there I can do? Oh, yes. I'll tell the story of the little boy who was chased by the Newfoundland dog and ran to tell his mother he was being chased by a lion. 

It's a good story, and so proper. Even Quakers like it." "I've never heard that." "Be not alarmed, brave youth. It smacks not of my usual breed of stories. It's simon pure and virginal and has annoyed at least three clergymen by being so innocuous they just couldn't enjoy it. As you'll have to hear it Sunday I'm sparing you the agony today." He plunged into his coat and flung open the door at the same instant. "Hurrah, we're off. We'll thumb our noses at all the millionaire's houses on the Avenue."

They clattered together down the brownstone steps and turned toward Sixth Avenue. The spires of St. Patrick's Cathedral rose coldly against the dark heavy sky. 

The little Waldorf Theatre looked very lonely standing in the midst of the long row of garages. Men stood outside of these in grimy overalls shouting at cars as they passed, trying to make the owners park them during the theatre hour. Taxis hustled along going nowhere with avid intent. The air reeked of burnt gasoline. 

Tom laughed. "Do you wonder why I call this street 

Gasoline Alley? But look. There is one of the alley roses, born of that impossible combination of alcohol and water which they used to say wouldn't mix. They're having a hard time since Repeal." 

Alfred looked at the girl just ahead of them, and as

FURNISHED ROOM 

23 he did so she turned under an awning stretched over the sidewalk from a narrow doorway leading to the space above a particularly villainous-looking garage. She was very slender and rather tall, with a long "bob" passionately henna-ed. She was pretty in a bold dissipated sort of way. As they came abreast of her she called out, "Hello, Tom Smith."

Tom stopped short in surprise, then held out his hand cordially. "Why if it isn't my playmate, Linda Larkin. I must introduce my friend Alfred Murray." They shook hands, Linda with coy demureness. "How goes the little game, Linda?"

She shook her head to signify not so good even as she faintly reproved the question. Then she smiled suddenly. "I'm going in here for a while. Sometimes I do a little specialty dance. Won't you come in?" "Sorry, dear girl. We're flatter than flounders tonight." "All right. I'll buy the drinks. Now how about it?" "I guess we couldn't impose on you, Linda. Wait till we get rich. You never can tell, you know." 

She sighed and held out her hand impulsively. "You just won't be nice, will you? I guess you want to stay up on the hero pedestal where I've put you. You might like it if you fell off, you know." 

There was a flash of white teeth and she was gone.

As they turned again toward Fifth Avenue Alfred grinned. "Who's your playmate?" "Just a poor little tart I helped once. She has an idea. she must repay me, somehow. It was just fun. I'll tell you some day. Now I just want to ride and forget such things."

It was after midnight when Tom unlocked his door and entered his room again. After the gay hopeful ride and conversation with Alfred the room seemed so small

FURNISHED ROOM 

and narrow. Alfred made him feel that it was worth while fighting on and on against a sordid commercial world. Alfred worked in a bank, and lived with his father and mother who were the bourgeois type of couple, but he always wanted to escape. Tom always felt new strength to go on after an evening with Alfred.

Then he came back to the furnished room and it closed prison-like about him.

No room to pace up and down; no room to stretch one's arms without hitting the walls. You could only walk between the bed and the wall if you were very careful, and if you bent down to pick up something under the bed you had to bend with the length of the room. There was only space to sit on the edge of the bed in the time-honoured pose of the furnished-roomer. 

First you took off your shirt and collar and then in your undershirt you sat on the edge of the bed while you took off your shoes. There is always something meditative about taking off shoes. How many men ever take them off with great precision, one after the other? Strange how men will sit with one shoe off for minutes before attacking the other, or even sit holding a shoe while they dream. After that there are the socks to be managed and somehow that, too, is cause for delay. 

Tom smiled as he sat with one bare foot caught up on his knee. If you lived in a furnished room, and a hall room at that, you simply had to conform to the standards. They were dictated by sheer exigencies of space and would not be denied. When your room contained only a bed and a hard, uncompromising chair it was only natural you should sit on the edge of a bed. Then if you wanted to stretch you could do so, since your position utilized the long way of the room. And one might carry the idea further, Tom mused. Of the many beds he had known, so few of them had been comfort-

FURNISHED ROOM 

25 able to sleep in, and yet most of them had been good enough to sit on. Therefore one sat. 

Yes, probably that was why when you rode on the elevated trains you could see into thousands of rooms where men sat on the edges of beds in their undershirts and just stared into space. All young men, too. That was the strangeness of it, and the horror of it. What were all these young men thinking about as they sat evening after evening on the edge of the bed, just staring? What were their thoughts as they undressed when they weren't tired, and didn't want to go to sleep? What were their dreams, and aspirations, and hopes as they wasted the evening with desultory newspaper reading and undressed very slowly in order to kill time? All the young men who flock into the city from throughout the whole country and for most of whom this is Fate; what do they yearn for instead of their soul-destroying loneliness?

With a deep sigh Tom stepped out of his underwear after switching off the light. He must manage to get some sort of shade for the bulb at the ten cent store. 

They always had such things there, and common as they were, they were better than the naked bulb. Now the light was off and the shade pulled up, a faint illumination sifted in at the dirty window, a sort of "dim cathedral effect," Tom always jokingly called it. It was one of the things one always expected and found in furnished rooms where windows are seldom, if ever, cleaned. 

When he lived on 29th Street he had an amusing view one Christmas morning. A young man across the street had suddenly decided his window must be clean for the Holy Day and so got to work at it immediately upon rising. He was dressed in a B.V.D. union suit on which most of the buttons were missing, but he climbed onto the window sill and worked away heartily in full view

FURNISHED ROOM 

of the entire street, either oblivious to or not bothering about the fact that he might just as well have been nude. 

Indeed absolute nudity would have been far more modest. Then inspired by the success of the inside cleaning, he had climbed outside and clinging to the framework of the window worked just as vigorously until it was fairly gleaming. The wind had fluttered his tattered union suit and caressed his bare feet and legs icily but he had paid no attention. Then he had climbed in and was seen no more. Tom chuckled at the remembrance as he stretched out on the bed.

But that was just a funny remembrance. Now the air pouring in at the open window felt good on his naked body as he lay outside the covers, and he ran his hands over the flesh, revelling in its soft coolness. Who could doubt how good it was just to be alive and lie stretched out like this with the cool night air caressing your body and kissing away the memory of the harshness of your clothes? With his hands resting on his thighs he fell into a soft sleep; "slid down the glorious, velvet hill of sleep into the perfection of oblivion," to use his own phrase so clearly indicative of his sensuous enjoyment of the rapture of the approach of slumber.

He was barely asleep when there was a blurred knocking at the door. At the first tap he was wide awake, thinking Alfred must have returned to tell him something he had forgotten. He jumped to his feet and caught up the bathrobe he always kept flung over the foot of the bed. "Is that you, Al?" he called. "Yes.” The answer was muffled. 

He flung open the door with a gesture. "Then come on in. The later the hour the more welcome.' "2 An instant later he realized his mistake, but the figure

FURNISHED ROOM 

27 was already inside the room and had closed the door. For an instant his mind swung here and there, then he placed the shadowy figure. It was the woman from upstairs. 

Before he could speak she said softly: "Aren't you going to go on making that speech you started?" She bowed clumsily. "The later the more welcome." "I'm sorry; I thought my friend had come back about something. I didn't know it was you.' 

"Well, can't I be your friend, too?" She slipped up beside him and caught his arm. "After all, we live in the same house, don't we? And I haven't bothered you any more for gin, have I?" She sighed deeply. "And I like you too. I thought you'd get that and come see me. 

I'm always home.' 

Tom didn't answer. He felt he couldn't trust himself to speak. His throat felt dry and he could hear the thumping of his heart. He knew he must try to pull himself together and get her to go, but no words came to his lips. He closed his eyes but he could only recall her body as he had held it in his arms the first day of his arrival.

Suddenly she was clinging to him with upturned face murmuring very softly words which he couldn't catch, but whose intent was clear.

With an effort he held her away from him and reached for the door. "Please go, Mrs. Crowley. It's late and I'm tired." He threw every effort into his words. "I thought I was Roseanna to you." "Roseanna, then." "I thought you'd ask me to stay a little. I'm so cold all alone." She shook a little. 

His hand touched the door knob but at the sound she shook her shoulders and with a gesture dropped her robe at her feet and stood goldenly naked in the dim light.

FURNISHED ROOM 

Tom could just discern the soft contour of her form and hear her quick breathing. Her voice reached him, warm and throaty, in a single word. "Tom."

His hand dropped from the door and he extended it, whether in appeal or excess of emotion we can never know. At the gesture his bathrobe fell open and in an instant she had slipped inside its gaping folds and her tingling body was pressed against his. With a cry his arms closed convulsively about her and as she gasped under the pressure her very flesh seemed to melt into his. He felt her creeping velvet fingers as they slipped the robe from his shoulders. It slipped down his back until caught by the muscles of his arms locked about her soft slimness. Her breath was on his naked chest. 

His wild racing mind flung back to the endless rows of young men sitting on the edge of the bed wanting what-? He flung wide his arms with wide spread trembling fingers. "You must go, quickly." 

But as the words fell, the robe, finally released, slipped down his arms over his clenched hands and lay in a tumbled heap behind him. Her arms were clasped feverishly about his neck now, and her hands pressed his head down to meet her upturned face. Her eyes were closed and her breath touched his cheek in quick flame-like gasps. Her body seemed to cling to his by the very power of its desire, and he was madly conscious of her moulded breasts against his ribs, the vague glow of the flesh on her back in the dim light sifting through the window. 

From somewhere outside came a burst of high-pitched idiotic laughter. A convulsive shiver ran through his frame and her body vibrated in response to it. Her voice was a little frightened now as she clung to him and his clenched fists still caught the empty air.

29 "Tom, what's the matter?"

A sound gushed from his lips as it tore from the depths of his chest. His arms suddenly swung her lightly up and onto the bed. His lips sank against hers and his body came over her frailness. She caught a hand in his thick hair gasping, "Tom-be good to me!" 

Her arms entwined him and her mouth was wet against his. "Roseanna—oh—oh God.”

TOM CHAPTER FOUR 

M sat kicking his legs on the big tin box at the rear end of the 72nd Street subway station, uptown side. It was used to stuff newspapers into, to await the little train that came along about two o'clock in the morning collecting trash. But now at about eleven thirty on Sunday morning it was empty and Tom found it made a good seat while waiting for a Van Cortlandt 

Park train. On Sunday morning the wait always seems interminable on a platform but it is easier if you sit on something and swing your feet and pretend you are very young and very gay. It also helps you to think, if you need to do that strange thing, and Tom felt he needed to think. He somehow hoped that during the long ride to Dyckman Street he would be able to think more clearly than he had in his room on 50th Street for the last thirty-six hours.

The mad delirium of the first few hours of Roseanna's stay in his room was all well and good, but the thing had to end; so much was clear. His hands shook a little with suppressed emotion as he closed his eyes and again felt the velvet fire of her body against his and again the uncontrollable convulsive wave of desire passed through him. For those first few hours as she lay in his enfolding arms vibrant with desire or languid with repletion, with half closed eyes and faintly smiling lips she was all that was womanhood and he was just man. Then in the grey haze of dawn their renewed, rejuvenated ardours had sprung to fuller life, and when she had left him, creeping softly from the room with a light finger at her kiss- 30

FURNISHED ROOM 

31 crushed lips, he had lain exhausted and exalted on his bed watching with happy unaccustomed eyes while the daylight filled the sordid red room and vainly pointed out its dullness. Passion and desire had flung their crimson banners full to the breeze and in the quiet calmness of the morning it was beautiful in retrospect. 

At this point in his recollections the train rolled in and he boarded it and stood on the platform at the end of the last car. There were seats available but he felt he had rather stand and let his thoughts run on until they had reached their inevitable conclusion. There was no hope of crushing them now and he was not sure he even wanted to. But also it was doubtful if other people in the car might not read his haggard expression and make some guess as to the turmoil raging within him. 

For turmoil there was. His satisfied calmness of Saturday morning had disappeared in the afternoon. He had felt that Roseanna's madness had been a thing of a night during her husband's absence, but she had come to him and quietly explained that the man she lived with was simply the man she lived with and nothing more. She had waved aside his attempts at any explanation of his feeling that even if she was just living with somebody there was still an obligation to be considered. "Don't be a fool. I told Jake the other night I was crazy about you and he said he knew it. Jake's like that. 

He's a real sport and got good sense too. He bet me a bottle of gin that I couldn't get you and now he's gotta pay. Boy, I had to come after you and you took a lot of convincing but you're worth it. When you handle me, lover, I know I been through it and then some. Say, anybody as good as you oughta give lessons. Wait'll I tell 

Jake some of the things he don't know and ain't got.' 

She patted his hand and then went on. "Anyway 

Jake's crazy about some dizzy blonde he keeps on 49th

FURNISHED ROOM 

Street. Gee, don't get any idea he keeps me cause he don't. I'm my own boss and when I say I like you, hell couldn't stand in the way. Now how about that? And you could lick Jake anyway. He's not so tough. All these taxi drivers talk tough but he ain't got muscles like you."

She smiled as she finished and snuggled up close to him as they both sat on the edge of the bed. In the gaudy daylight she looked hard and coarse and there was something about her that repulsed him. After their wonderful night together, why couldn't she just keep away from him as if it had never been but in a dream. It would be perfect if they could never have seen each other again, but even living in the same house she might have treated him with cool aloofness as if they had never lain side by side in exhausted ecstasy. It would have stung him a little, of course, but it would have been perfect. 

Now she came to him in the hard revealing daylight and told him she had forced her way into his room and bed to win a bet with her taxi-driver lover who kept another woman on 49th Street. How revolting it all was in this new light. How fitting this sordid, gruesome, red room, so miserably furnished that they had to sit side by side on the bed because there was no other place to sit, even while she smacked her lips at the thought of the bottle of gin she had won through his passion and ecstasy.

He shuddered at the thought and she nestled closer to him. "What's the matter, lover? Don't you like me any more? Hold me tight like last night."

He put his arm around her and she caught it and cupped his hand over her breast murmuring, "Now crush me like the big bear you are. God, I never been with a man as strong as you and you're just on fire all the time, too. Sweet answer to a poor girl's prayer.”

FURNISHED ROOM 

33 

And as he held her there the fire of her flesh ran through his veins and he could have cried aloud with the agony of it. In an instant their lips were crushed together and his hands had found their way to the sweet flesh under the coarseness of her clothes. Suddenly she had sprung away laughing. "Now that's a little more like it." She stood between his knees now and her hand tousled his thick hair. "You are just a regular boy, aren't you, just gasoline ready to be set on fire all the time." She laughed softly. "To see you struttin' around so high and mighty with your head in the air a girl'd think you wore frigidaire drawers or something. Boy, but you're all there. What the girls wouldn't give if they knew, but you're all mine now, lover." She caught his face in her hands and kissed it. "Must run along now to see my sweet sister who has cash in plenty and whose mind is far from sin. Goodbye." And she was gone before he could speak.

He stood, shaken by the memory, looking out on the gloomy receding subway tracks. The lights of the station they had just left swung out of view and then there were only vague incomprehensible signal lights. Life was like that, too; a long narrow tunnel down which you plunged and usually only guided by lights which were signals you didn't understand. Yet you had to understand in order to stay on the track and live.

His thoughts returned to himself again. After she had left him yesterday he had realized fully his own weakness. He must break off with her. His mind was disgusted and yet she had merely to touch him to turn him into a flaming sensualist. She had sensed it at once and called him her "boy." Yes, he was acting like a crude inexperienced boy overwhelmed by the first intoxicating realization of sex. At seventeen he had not been such a fool, nor at twenty. Now at thirty he was in a

FURNISHED ROOM 

fair way to become the pet of a taxi-driver's second best woman. That was what rankled. On Saturday night he had stayed out late and only returned when he was dog tired and sure to sleep. He had carefully locked his door and then gotten into bed as quickly as possible without even washing his face lest it wake him ever so little. He had fallen asleep right away but suddenly he was startled by a light knock and voice. "It's me, Tom."

He lay very still although he felt sure she must hear the dull thudding of his heart. He tried to recall the bet, the bottle of gin, the drunkard's face and eyes and instead all he had was the fierce memory of his searching hands on the swelling roundness of her hips and cupping the firm delicacy of her breasts. A tremor passed over him and the bed rattled faintly. "Tom! Are you there?"

The voice was still low but it seemed to ring in his ears. He counted the thudding heartbeats as he lay clutching the sides of the narrow bed. Then there was a creak of a board in the silent hall and he knew she had gone. For a long time he lay there trembling in the pale darkness of the room. It was as if a heavy weight 

Îay on his chest and he couldn't breathe freely. With a sob he had turned over to crush his agony and buried his face in the pillow. As he turned he flung the bedclothes from him so the air poured down upon his burning naked body. Clutching the pillows with both arms he lay with his face sunk into them and slowly pulled his mind back from the abyss. After a long time he had fallen into a broken sleep made horrible by sensuous, revolting dreams. Daylight had found him again staring at the ceiling as the room slowly lightened. But now there was no ecstasy, no exultation. He had won a struggle but there was no triumph.

35 The train swung out of the 191st Street station and Tom pulled himself together. Viewed clearly, he had won the fight last night, and if it happened again surely it would be easier. And yet even now his legs were quivering as he walked through the car. It was empty except for the conductor, who sat glumly in a corner. 

Tom shook himself as he stood on the platform of the next car waiting for the door to open at Dyckman Street. There the subway suddenly swoops into the open air as if tired of the noisy, stuffy darkness. Maybe a good hearty dinner was what he really needed. Last night he had collected his ten dollars from Mrs.

Bobbs but he had only eaten a little, though it had been his only meal of the day. He had been too worried to eat. But now- He ran down the steps to the street. 

Food was such a fine antidote for his particular poison. 

Food and a real touch of family life to make him forget two nights of insanity, both in their different ways devastating to mind and body.

Meanwhile the two friends walked down the street and then stood irresolute on the corner. Alfred then suggested they take the ferry across the Hudson River and sit on the pier over there. Tom agreed it was certainly too early to go to bed and so they walked toward the river. Both were strangely silent, each full of his own thoughts. Once on the ferry there were three or four cars but no foot passengers. As the ferry pushed its foaming way through the water they stood looking up at the stars which blazed clearly down now one was out of the area of lighted streets. Tom spoke very quietly. "Aĺ, I certainly do like your father and mother. It has been a fine day for me.' 99 "Thanks." His voice was replete with thinly veiled sarcasm. "I knew you had the makings of an actor but

day, now be yourself. Tell me what you really think of a Sunday at home with the morons?" "That's not fair." "No, of course it's not fair. But good God, haven't we had enough platitudes and insincerities for one day?

The ferry banged into the piles and then slowly groped into the slip. High above, the Palisades reared dark and grim, shutting out half the splendor of the sky. CHAPTER FIVE 

Snore knockings at his door and Tom slept peace UNDAY and Monday nights passed off without any fully. On Tuesday morning he woke up feeling out of sorts but at the same time happy in the conviction that his madness was over. He went out at about eleven to make a call at a theatrical office where he often went to see if an opening might have occurred which he could fill. With thousands of actors walking the streets it was undue optimism to hope for anything to turn up for him, a man without theatrical experience, but a number of the offices told him to keep calling and at least he felt he sang better than the others, or most of the others. FURNISHED ROOM

The taxi screamed to a halt.

He ran heavily down the shallow, slanting steps leading to Riverside Drive and then across the roadway, plunging blindly between cars and busses. A taxi-driver screamed some profanity at him but he paid no heed and sank on a bench, almost as if his legs had crumpled under him.

He had to force his mind back now to recapture his own optimism. His cry had been: "Merely to breathe consciously is to be happy." According to Betty's crisp, practical philosophy of life, these days when money was so hard to get there was no time to be wasted on punks. With quiet assurance 

Betty divided all men into "gentlemen" who went with her and "punks," who didn't. It was a simple classification, easily made and endlessly reassuring to a young girl struggling to get along. Tom stared after her dumbly.

I asked her if I couldn't give her a lesson just as well as you and she said absolutely no. Her mother had told her that only you could make a lady out of her. I almost confided in her that there were several girls you might make an honest woman out of, but as for making anyone a lady-" he spread his hands in exaggerated Hebraic deprecation and then laughed mockingly.

Tom invited him into the house and soon they were in the dingy red room. Alfred took a long look at his friend as he took off his coat languidly and flung himself on the bed.

FURNISHED ROOM 

"What's the matter, Tom? You look all in."

Alfred handed back the paper gravely. "I'm sorry you feel you can't tell me, Tom, but I dare say there is some reason. Anyway, is there anything I can do?"

61 "Whatever they please. Now quiet down and I'll be back as soon as I can." He was gone.

Tom leaned his head against the wall, his hands above him lying flat on the dingy stained red paper. 

Heavy sobs shook his frame. "He didn't turn away. 

He didn't even turn away."

CHAPTER SEVEN 

NE of the most amusing scenes left in a dull, Nork-a-day world is a chorus call by one of the big theatrical producers. Its animation is false, if you please, and pretentious, but it is none the less animation. for all that. The girls may not be the simple, child-like creatures their mothers hoped they would be, and the boys may have absorbed most of the feminine qualities the girls have lost, but in spite of, or perhaps because of that fact, it is a strange, startling scene.

The girls in their extremely abbreviated rehearsal costumes seem to have hardly any clothes on-no more than a broad belt, and the boys have added a bewilderingly gay array of rainbow-hued shirts and ties and fluttering handkerchiefs. There are ecstatic cries of greeting among those who have not met in at least twenty-four hours, and impeccably impersonal kisses are exchanged blithely. Everyone chatters wildly in utter disregard of the absence of listeners and groups form and reform at minute intervals. Everyone is "dearie" or "my dear," that supremely impersonal form of address of the theatre, and everyone is overwhelmed with joy to see everyone else, even though each newcomer limits his own chance of getting a job.

It is one of the strangest things about those in the chorus, especially the type that seems doomed never to get any farther. Let them be out of work ever so long and be ever so hard up, when they hear of a "call" they dash about telling everyone they can meet, and thus quixotically obliterate their own chance of success. But 62

FURNISHED ROOM 

63 it is their code and they cling to it. One sometimes is forced to wonder if they don't really enjoy the bustle and turmoil of getting a job far more than being in one.

When Tom entered the stage door of Meyers' Theatre, a few days later, the stage was packed. The footlights and standlights were lighted, but the rest of the house was in darkness. A line of boys (all men in the theatrical world except those playing parts are known as boys) were standing right over the footlights and a voice from the knot of men seated in the darkened orchestra was calling certain ones out of line and telling them to report to Mr. Brannigan. There was a tense silence about the whole thing, then a high-pitched squeaky voice cried: "That's all, boys. Sorry we can't use a million dancers, but we can't. Goodbye. Clear the stage please. 

Pandemonium broke loose at once and there was pushing and shoving, some trying to get out the stage door, and others refusing to budge. "Lucky boy." "Sorry, Bill. See you tonight."

The cries of commiseration or congratulation were bandied back and forth while one or two stood forlornly at the edge of the stage still trying to catch the producer's attention. But Meyers, once he dismissed a group, was adamant, and had now deliberately turned his back on the stage and was talking to his assistants. The forlorn ones gradually departed.

Then, as the stage cleared, it was rapidly filled up again by those who had been sitting at the back of the orchestra. A man rose up in the manager's group crying, "Singers! Men singers only. Girls please stay back in the orchestra and off the stage. We want you all later but not now."

FURNISHED ROOM 

Several of the men came out of the orchestra and surrounded a baby grand piano which was suddenly pushed out. The man who had called out before now cried "first tenors only," and there was a tremendous pushing to get to the footlights. Tom found himself at the very end of the line and pressed against the proscenium arch. The unfamiliar glare of lights blinded him, but the others stared off into space with superb nonchalance and posed their heads to let the upward flood of lights show their faces to best advantage. The strange hush fell on the theatre. There was an almost ghastly expectancy about it and then suddenly a voice came out of the darkness. "You on the end there, what are you playing in now? 

No, not you, Jones, the left end.”

Tom wasn't sure if he was being called so he remained silent. The man next to him nudged him and whispered, "He means you. Talk up." "Not in any show now," Tom called out obediently. "What were you in last? I don't seem to remember seeing you before." "I've not been on the stage before," he cried.

He was conscious of the hundreds of eyes fixed on him. Then suddenly he remembered what he had been told to say and added, "Not on the stage in New York." 

The man next to him muttered, "You fool, never tell them that." There was a faint murmur. 

The voice out of the orchestra snarled, "Well what the hell do you think this is, an amateur performance? 

What do you do, sing?" 

Tom flared up. "Certainly. I wouldn't be here otherwise."

A titter ran through the crowd. He knew he had made himself popular with the crowd but probably not so with the producing group in the orchestra. His

FURNISHED ROOM 

65 eyes were getting better accustomed to the glare now and he saw someone talking rapidly to the man with the high-pitched voice who was probably Meyers himself, judging from the descriptions of him he had heard. 

Perhaps the shadowy figure talking was Harold; he hoped it was. Suddenly a husky voice from the group cried, "Moe, let this chap sing now.'

Willing hands fairly pushed him to the piano and he pulled the song he always carried for auditions out of his pocket. The pianist was a crushed-looking Jew with glasses which magnified his eyes so much they startled you when he looked up. "For You Alone," he read with a grunt and then struck a loud chord for silence. Everyone stood breathless in the peculiar way one does at auditions while waiting for the singer to begin. "Take thou this rose, this little tender rose,

The fairest flow'r in all God's garden rare, And let it be, while yet its crimson glows, An emblem of the love I proudly bear."

As he began to sing Tom felt his throat tighten in that way singers know so well and fear as nothing else on earth. But after the first few words, fear suddenly was gone and his voice flowed from him in the round easy tone which made him so happy, always. Was not his slogan, "When I can sing I am happy?" He forgot everything in the sheer joy of sound.

As he swung into the second verse he knew he was being followed intently. Anyone who sings knows that, and at auditions you are only allowed to finish a song if you are pleasing the auditors. Finally he came to the last line and the top note rang out in the silent house. 

The pianist had caught the idea now and Tom held the last note round and full while the piano swept its theme

FURNISHED ROOM 

to conclusion. For one instant there was silence and then applause broke out from all sides. It stopped as swiftly and unexpectedly as it had begun and Tom heard the deep husky voice shouting: "There'll be no more of that. We're not here to be amused, but to work. As for you, Tom Smith, give your name and address to Mr. Breen. Report here a week from today. Now once more, tenors, line up again. Moe! Will you begin when you are ready?"

Tom received several claps on the back and there was a loud buzz of congratulation as he threaded his way toward the stage door. Just inside of it Harold suddenly popped out of the door that led into the main body of the theatre. He rushed forward with both hands outstretched. As soon as he saw him Tom knew how the man had known his name. "You were simply marvellous. Meyers is crazy about your voice, though he'd never acknowledge it. I'm sure

I haven't seen an audition crowd applaud like that more than two or three times before. He didn't want to hear you at first because you said you hadn't been on the stage but I told him to be careful or he'd miss a find. 

I'm so glad you didn't fail me.”

His face clouded over. "It's too darn bad about that other thing but we'll keep it dark. For Heaven's sakes don't breathe a word of it around here. Don't even let anyone know you don't drink. They're so suspicious, always. And now I must run back.'

As Tom walked along Broadway to 50th Street, his mind was a jumble of confused thoughts. Who would not be elated at having survived the test he had just been through? At last he had been told to report, perhaps for rehearsal, perhaps only for a second audition, but at any rate the first step in the theatre was gained. 

And just when he had forgotten it for the first time

FURNISHED ROOM 

67 

Harold had to remind him of the "other thing." He surely needed no warning as to how quickly such news would bar him from any theatrical undertaking, or from anything else, as far as that was concerned. That was the worst horror of it all. It drove one out of social life as leprosy might, or anything equally loathsome. 

He almost felt as if he should walk through the streets crying, "Unclean, unclean," as they did in ancient Jerusalem. 

Only the day before Mrs. Tillinghast had come into his room to straighten it while he was lying on the bed. She had been curt as she polished the wash-basin and then had turned suddenly. "Mr. Smith, I don't see how you get these awful yellow stains on your basin, but do be careful as I can't seem to get rid of them." 

She rubbed with ostentatious vigor for a few seconds and then: "I suppose it's something you drink. I'm sure I wouldn't put into my stomach anything that stained like that. But then, I suppose I'm not a man," and she marched out with her head in the air as if Tom had somehow disagreed with her and subtly accused her of being the very thing she said she was not.

It was things like that that made it all so terrible. 

Perhaps the woman knew what his trouble was. After running a rooming house for a great many years one was apt to know about most everything in life, especially everything unpleasant. Her insinuating manner implied that she knew about everything, of course, but then that was only the usual manner of her class and might indicate nothing. It was almost a temptation to tell her and have all doubts settled at once. 

It was a temptation to do that with everyone one knew and so find out once and for all who would remain friends and who would not; who would be sympathetic and helpful, and who would only be shocked

FURNISHED ROOM 

and horrified. But it was a temptation one always resisted, even as everyone similarly placed always resisted it, except, perhaps, those who belonged to the "no worse than a bad cold" school of thought, and they usually moved in a class of society whose company one did not go out of one's way to seek or to retain. 

Yet after all was it a fair test? That thought always followed close on the heels of the rest of it. Would he himself have continued to chum with anyone similarly placed? Above all now that he knew more of the dangers, would he be willing to do so, or would he too not make some excuse and get away either gracefully or crudely according to his confidant's cultural standards and training. To be sure Alfred was exactly the same as always, a little more kind and solicitous, perhaps, a little more anxious to help him financially to the limit of his very small means, but in other ways no whit different, no matter how one studied him. But then Alfred was, in so many ways, the unusual person who seemed in no way governed by the usual rules. 

Such response could certainly not be expected from the average person.

He climbed the brownstone steps of the house with dragging feet. He was tired with the utter exhaustion that seemed to creep over him now. Doctor Krassin had said, "It is very important for you to keep off your feet as much as possible. Never stand or walk when you can possibly sit down, and if you can lie down, so much the better."

Once inside the hall he fairly leaned against the wall for support when a voice hailed him from up the stairs. "Mr. Smith, could you come up here for a minute? I want you to help me." 

He glanced up and saw Roseanna Crowley's head leaning over the banister looking down. A sudden shiver

FURNISHED ROOM 

69 of remembrance shook his body and his jaw tightened spasmodically. He had not seen her since he realized his condition and its origin. She had been on a prolonged drunk, he knew that, but that was all. He had hoped he would never see her again even as he had cursed her at every moment of pain that unnerved him by day and tortured him by night. The first night when he had found Alfred awaiting him, his plan had been to seek her out and tell her what he didn't know, he couldn't know. What words were there to express his feelings that night before Alfred's splendid calmness had quieted him?

And now she was calling him just when the stimulus of the audition was gone and only remained the sickening dog-tiredness of being on his feet so long. Clenching his fists he cried, "Coming right up," and began to mount the stairs. He followed the woman into the room and closed the door after him carefully. With quiet deliberation he turned the key in the lock and then faced her squarely. "Now what do you want?” 

She stretched her arms wide with a full smile of welcome. She had only a kimono and slippers on and her manner somehow subtly suggested even the temporary nature of that. She started to hum "Lover Come Back to Me" in her coarse, throaty, uneven voice. For a few seconds they stood thus, then he lunged forward swiftly, caught her wrist and jerked her to him. Her face blanched a little as she swung off balance and then caught herself, but she only smiled again and looked straight into his eyes. "Tom, boy, you're so strong. But why be in such a hurry after staying away so long? And why lock the door? He won't be home and to hell with him if he does come. Now give me a real bear hug."

He caught her other hand in a vise-like grip and

FURNISHED ROOM 

held her firmly. As he crushed her hand the laughter died out of her eyes and a tight smothered cry fell from her lips, but he paid no attention now. His teeth were clenched until the muscles stood rigidly out in his jaws and his eyes were blazing.

Suddenly he dropped her wrists and his brutal grip fastened on her arms just above the elbows. His fingers sank heavily into the smooth soft flesh and as they did so, he felt all sense leave him, and nothing remained but fire flowing through his veins. It was a far different delirium from what this same woman had brought on him before and the feel of her flesh under his fingers was maddening. "Now what do you want of me? You've ruined my life, now what more do you want? Have I some vague shred of decency left that you can strip from me?” "Tom," she sobbed, "you're hurting me. What's the matter with you? What have I done?" 

He burst into a crazy gust of laughter, then stopped dead suddenly. His voice was low and bitter. "Maybe you're right. Maybe you're so used to it that it's just part of the day's work. I suppose it's some sort of hellish round-robin and it's your turn to pass it on. Some sort of religious service in praise of Venus. I hope you're well enough up in your Latin to appreciate that." 

Roseanna's eyes were popping now in amazement. 

She gasped. "You're crazy, mad. What are you talking about?" 

He laughed insanely. "Listen, bitch! Now you'll understand. You've dosed me. Do you understand that? You filthy scum, you've got me down to your level. Bah-you slut."

With a quick gesture he flung her away from him and he fell heavily on the bed. For an instant she lay

71 there panting, looking up at him as he stood over her with clenched fists. I was cured a year ago. It was someone else. Ask Jake. Before God, Tom, it wasn't me.' 

"No! Of course you're innocent. Of course I imagine everything. I imagine my sufferings now, my treatments, and consultations. And there was no one else, no one else," he muttered half to himself. 

She stood up facing him. "Tom, I'm cured, I tell you. I was sick but I'm cured. You poor boy. I know what you are suffering. It's terrible." 

Tom with a cry caught both hands around her throat and squeezed her neck. "So you're a liar as well as everything else. Now I'm going to beat you so you'll never take your rotten carcass near a man again. Do you hear me? Do you hear me?" The words seemed to tear themselves from his gasping throat.

He lifted her slight body clear of the bed but no sound came from her beyond a single gurgling gasp.

Then suddenly he felt her body go limp under his hands and she sank to the floor. As her legs wilted he loosed his grasp and in an instant she lay in a silent shapeless heap on the floor. He stared at her, then pushed her with his foot. "Get up."

A second time he pushed her but she lay silent like an empty potato sack. A wave of realization swept over him and gathering her up roughly into his arms he dumped her on the middle of the bed. He turned her over on her back as she lay sprawled there and then bent over to listen if she was breathing. It would only be following the general trend of things if she made him a murderer as well as an outcast. 1

FURNISHED ROOM 

A hollow smile twisted his lips as he felt her breath and heard her faint heart-throbs. For one instant his head lay again on her breast and as his mind swung back an overwhelming nausea almost overcame him. He got to his feet slowly and swayed to the door. The key seemed to stick but finally he flung out of the door and banged it shut after him. As he staggered down the stairs he saw Mrs. Tillinghast standing with hands on hips watching him but he brushed her aside and entered his room. There was no sound from above.

CHAPTER EIGHT 

R. and Mrs. Dale after twenty-five years of New 

Jersey suburban life still made something of a ceremony out of Sunday morning breakfast, lingering over the meal and discussing plans for the day. It was a day they believed in making much of, and the house usually was full of company. They both believed in entertaining their friends and enjoyed doing it. Anne, their daughter, always fell in with their plans and as far as possible kept her Sundays free from outside engagements. Even as a little girl she had had the privilege of inviting as many friends for Sunday dinner as she cared to. Now she had just returned from a year of music study and travel in Europe and had once more fallen easily into the general scheme. 

Mrs. Dale was very plump and jolly, with a vivacious bounce in her carriage which kept every other woman she knew vainly trying to keep up with her. She ran her household joyously on a cook and maid and chauffeur. The latter was a kindly old soul who really loved to potter about the garden far better than to drive and who was the ceaseless object of Mrs. Dale's motherly care, for he had been her father's coachman in what she always called "the old days." The maid and cook, two sisters, were very happy too, and so extremely Irish in their brogue that one knew immediately they hadn't seen the country of their birth in at least a quarter of a century.

In truth they had been with Mrs. Dale that long, she having gotten them just after they had come to the 73

FURNISHED ROOM 

country and were sitting forlornly in their first employment agency. The first day they had blurted out their story, of their childhood in County Kildaire and of their parents' death within a few weeks of each other. They had then decided to take the little money they had inherited and try their fortune in the new country. So Mrs. Dale had found them in the agency waiting with folded hands for someone to hire them who would be kind to them and give them a good home. "For sure," they explained, "we don't intend to be gallivanting from place to place. Faith, we were brought up to find a job and then stick to it." 

Mrs. Dale in return had told them that she was just married and had never hired anyone before and didn't know just what questions she was supposed to ask, or what answers to expect. So they decided to do away with all questioning and just see how everything went. 

At the end of twenty-five years it was still going perfectly and Nora and Maggie had been completely baffled by questions as to why they were spending their whole life in one place. "Sure it's our home. Where else should we be going?"

Through the years Mrs. Dale's brow showed no wrinkles of domestic care and now, nearing her fiftieth year, she carried her plumpness with the energy and grace of youth and her skin was the envy of everyone. 

When she was asked what she would do if Tim and 

Nora and Maggie left her she always laughed. "I'm sure one of our funerals will precede that and the rest really wouldn't matter." 

Now, after breakfast, Mrs. Dale laid her plump white hand across her daughter's long powerful brown one. 

The small prettiness of the one lay almost dove-like on the clean developed power of the other. Mrs. Dale's jolly quick voice was exactly what one would expect after

FURNISHED ROOM 

75 studying her. Her whole face seemed to light up as she spoke. "Anne, dear, are you glad to be back with us again?” "Yes, mother, very glad. I've loved it in Europe, but it was lonely. I suppose it always is, a little. I just couldn't find a mother anywhere." 

The plump hand patted softly. "You are a dear, sweet girl even after Paris. And no mother can say more. 

Anne laughed softly. "Why be so hard on poor

Paris? Everyone reads so much about the gaiety, and night life, and nothing about the real student life of work and privation. We all used to say someone should write a new 'Vie de Boheme,' and tell the truth this time." ee Lean, elegant, with precise manner and tone, her father put down his coffee cup with the quiet easy grace which marked every move he made. Anne, do you mind so very much about your finger? We have wanted to know, your mother and I, but we didn't want to write you about it. Now you're home mother has rather left it to me." 

Anne smiled until the quiet, intellectual face seemed illuminated from within. She was blessed with her father's quiet intellectuality combined with her mother's vital energy and rich variety of temperament. Now as she held out her free hand to her father and grasped his firmly, no one could escape the realization of how fully she was the perfect offspring of these two divergent personalities and how beautifully she embodied the best of each. She tightened her grip a little on the two hands clasped within her own. "Now please believe me even as you didn't when I wrote you. I'm afraid I was almost relieved when the surgeon said that finger would never regain its dexterity.

FURNISHED ROOM 

I'm afraid I was getting just far enough ahead with the piano to realize that a concert career meant more of drudgery than applause and flowers and all the things 

I had dreamed about. Le Boeuf says I shall always play beautifully; being a Frenchman he says that is because

I have a beautiful soul, or something to that effect, but then it would be a poor Frenchman, especially an artist like Le Boeuf, who could not think of something sweet to say to a pretty woman in distress." 

She laughed lightly. "Did you ever hear me calmly and coolly call myself a pretty woman. You see how inordinately vain living in Paris has made me. Any woman in Paris who doesn't squint or have a hare-lip is pretty. You must know that. It is one of the principal reasons why women from all over the world flock there. 

No woman is so unlucky as not to have at least one improper proposal within the first week.” cc 'Anne, how can you talk lightly of such a thing?" "Don't look horrified, mother. It's such a long way from the thought to the deed even in Paris. In spite of the good puritanical guide books, it really is. And, besides, I always have my operation to protect me now. 

Even the boldest Frenchman shrinks and quavers when you start on your operation and threaten to show the scar. It isn't necessary to explain, of course, that only the little finger on the right hand is involved." She finished with her gayest smile. 

Her father looked anxiously at the hand he held tenderly. "Does it hurt at all now?” "Not the least, daddy, and I really play well in spite of it. Let's just forget about it and make believe it never even happened. You know how I do hate commiseration.
