Anne went to the Brick Church station in her car to get Tom and was a little shocked at sight of him as he stepped out of the crowd. She knew he was going to be studied carefully, and there was something almost purposeful in his deviation from Sunday correctness. 

His shoes were not shined, his clothes were not pressed, and she noticed his trousers were patched and his coat threadbare at the elbows. His shirt cuffs were reversed and his soft collar as well, obviously to cover the dirty right side of them. His tie was a crumpled crudity and frayed untidily at the knot. In Bryant Park he had had a sort of romantic, brave look, but out in the tidy propriety of East Orange he just looked poor and neglected.

Even the erect bravery of his carriage seemed more like bravado out here and she thought of a quaint expression of her grandmother about "a beggar flaunting his rags."

No sooner was he in the car than she had turned her face aside so he might not see her flush and read her inner thoughts. It was shameful to think about a man's clothes when she knew he was poor and even had had to accept carfare with his invitation. But it hadn't occurred to her he might not have clean linen or the price to have clothes pressed and shoes shined. In a way wasn't it an unkindness to bring him out to her parents in this way? How must he feel about it?

FURNISHED ROOM 

81 So it was with rather an air of defiance that Anne led her guest into the drawing room where her father and mother were waiting. But Anne, after a year away, had lost something of her old confidence in her mother or she would not have been alarmed. Mrs. Dale welcomed him with outstretched hands and her brightest smile.

Her manner could not have been more gracious for the Prince of Wales. Then she turned him over to her husband with a smiling admonition not to linger over his "brushing up." "We always assure our guests that when they come by the new electric trains there is no excuse for having very dirty hands." 

Anne laughed, intensely relieved. "Or maybe you carry a little bottle of alcohol and some cotton to rub the dirt off as the French ladies do on trains?” 

Mr. Dale joined in happily, "You see how broadening travel is, Mr. Smith. In this country we'd probably drink the alcohol and go dirty, or use Lifebuoy." He led him off into the hall and up the stairs. 

Mother and daughter were left facing each other. 

Finally Anne cried, "Well-?" and waited. "He seems a fine big chap," her mother answered slowly, "but he has such sad tired eyes for a young man. 

I wonder how old he is?” "Thirty. He told me that right away." She wandered to the piano and began to play very softly. The first fence was cleared and it had been far easier than she had supposed. It made her heartily ashamed of her own thoughts and also of her distrust of her own parents. Once in the house one was more conscious of the open, sturdy look of him and less conscious of his clothes than one had been at the crowded railway station. Her mother began to arrange the flowers about the bright sunny room and then called over her shoulder.

FURNISHED ROOM 

"Anne, darling, do sing some little sweet song for me before the others come. Being a fond doting mother I like to hear you sing as well as play." 

Anne began a soft throbbing accompaniment and then sang softly— 

Ah si'l est dans votre village 

Un berger sensible et charmant- 

Her quiet simple voice suited the song and when she came to the dramatic "C'est mon ami, rendre le moi, c'est mon amour, il a ma foi," her voice rang clearly and with fine emotion. When she had finished the second verse she swung round and found her mother standing watching her. "That's a sweet song, very lovely indeed." "Yes. I like it. Goddard wrote it." "Isn't that the one about the girl who falls in love with the shepherd boy?" "Yes, mother."

Mrs. Dale smiled again and continued around the room touching an object here and there. "Yes, I still think you sing well. It is feeling and emotion that always impresses me more than sheer voice. I know it's very ignorant to say that, because the inherent quality of the voice should come first, but you know how I am. 

You sang that as if you meant every word you said.” "I do, mother, every word of it."

Before she could reply the two men entered the room again. Mrs. Dale went to them. "Mr. Smith, did you hear the lovely song?" "Only so faintly, Mrs. Dale. Were you singing?" "Thank you, but I was not. It was Anne. Darling will you sing it again?" 

The girl rose quietly. "No, mother. All encores are superfluous and Mr. Smith will sing this afternoon."

FURNISHED ROOM 

83 She sat down with the others and began to talk lightly of people of note she had met in Paris. Mrs. Dale smiled softly to herself. So at any rate she was not ready for him to hear her song, yet. The girl had not lost her head entirely.

After dinner Anne took Tom out to see the garden. at the rear of the house. They strolled about leisurely as she pointed out the various flowers which he confessed he didn't know. "I think you're doing it just to test me.

It isn't human to know the names of so few flowers.' "You're right. It isn't human. It's just New York-ish. 

The New Yorker is never quite human. It isn't possible for him to be." 

They sat on a stone bench together. She pushed a pebble about thoughtfully with the point of her shoe, then remarked quietly, "You seem to be human enough." "Don't deceive yourself. Out here I can fit myself a little into your lovely surroundings, but it is only a pose. Instead of a wooden bench in a public park I have moved up to a stone bench in your private garden. So 

I try to live up to it as an actor does to his painted scenery. But still I'm not human.' 

"That doesn't seem very clear to me?" "Or to me," he smiled sadly. "I apologize for it. It was crude and inept." 

She studied his face for a moment. "Do you think you'll like us out here?" "What a mad question. It is more a matter for me to be grateful for being here at all.” "Have we impressed you that way?" 

He swung toward her. "Lord no! You have almost succeeded in blinding me to myself, to my relation to life-to your life-to any life. But gardens are so useful in bringing a man to his senses. Flowers are so

FURNISHED ROOM 

gloriously impersonal, they belong where they are so inexorably. They make one think about things.' 

She laughed shortly. "As you go on you become less clear. Or maybe I simply don't want to understand. 

I'm afraid I like the idea of beautiful flowers better than philosophical thoughts sprung from them.” "Have I been guilty of such an idea? Forgive me.” "You have been very quiet today." "I have been penitent for talking too much and too stupidly last night. I was overwrought and you too generously let me take myself out on you. You see I had just done the lowest meanest act of a singularly low and mean life. I wanted to explain myself to someone. 

I couldn't tell you what I had done-believe me, it was too wholly horrible-but in talking to you I could try to justify myself to God. I was sure I would never see you again and so I was safe as long as you would listen. 

You might have been bored and walked away, or been angry and called a policeman, but you did neither of these very sensible and very obvious things. Instead you foolishly sat and listened and even encouraged me to go on. May I plead that now in extenuation of the incredible length of my tirade? "You see, I never stand bravely and face the consequences of what I do. I thrust my common sense aside with a rhetorical question. But last night I began to talk to you because you were from a world in which I had no place. You had stepped into my world as a bather may cautiously touch a toe to water testing whether it is warm enough to plunge in. Therefore it would be safe to talk to you as long as you would listen. 

You would soon go away and even if I saw you again, on the streets, perhaps, it would not matter because we were as hopelessly far apart as two concentric circles. "Even when you broke all rules and expectations and

FURNISHED ROOM 

85 asked me to eat with you I clung to my analysis because I had already said too much. But when you asked me to come here today, even I could no longer keep up the romantic pretence of the confessional. It could no longer be called romantic interest in the 'other half.' One may listen with interest to Gorky characters but one doesn't invite them home to dinner. Will you agree it was a little hard on me?" "Very hard, but what was I to do?" She spoke simply. "That's it. It is always so little when one does too much. I came today prepared to resent being patronized.

For it I summoned my last resources of pride. And then my defences fell down stupidly. Your family refused to see my clothes and what can you do with a hostess who simply won't see a dirty, turned soft collar?" "Was that exactly fair to me, to us?" "No it wasn't. But having seen your face when you met me at the station I felt some explanation was necessary. I have nothing else to wear. Some explanations are pitifully short, aren't they. Would you care to explain that to your parents or should I?" "That's not worthy of you or kind to us." 

He spread his hands in mock deprecation. "I'm awfully sorry about it all. I know it doesn't happen in your world. According to the 'code' I should have sent my regrets and stayed home. But there already was the matter of having accepted carfare, wasn't there? Confess you wouldn't have been too much surprised if you hadn't seen me again. 'Only another bum who needed a dollar and took that way to get it.' Would you have said it? I wonder. "But," he continued swiftly as she tried to speak, "aside from all that there was the primitive fact that I was hungry and I knew I could stuff myself here like a bear about to hibernate. Did I live up to your ex-

FURNISHED ROOM 

pectations in that direction? I hope so. Surely four people were never before served so much food in your house. And with a hostess like your mother-what could a tramp do. I do believe she looked happier every time 

I accepted more food." 

He stopped with a strange laugh and sat staring down between his feet. Anne felt as if he were forcing her eyes down to his broken shoes. Her hands were clasped loosely before her. When she finally spoke her voice was very low and quiet. "Why are you so bitter? You talk as if life had dealt you some cruel blow from which you feared you would never recover." "Perhaps it's even worse than that. Surely it's much worse if you deal the blow to yourself." He got up quickly and stood with his hands thrust heavily into his coat pockets.

She rose and by common consent they moved toward the house. "Shall we have some music now? I'm sure I have many things you will like to sing. I have been collecting music for years. I'm rather vain of it.” "May I sing 'Ich Grolle Nicht' for penance?" 

She smiled slowly. "Do you escape through your music, too? It is certainly my refuge when I don't understand why things must be." "For example your finger?" he queried. "Yes," she nodded. "Yes, to you; and no to all the rest of the world." They entered the house.

CHAPTER NINE 

OM entered his room at about one o'clock in the morn- 

TOM 

ing with a happy smile on his face for the first time since his illness. The day had been a fine one for him, and his singing had quite washed away the gloomy thoughts he had spoken to Anne in the garden. How gentle and kind she had been with him, not at all like any woman he had ever known. He chuckled aloud at his own thoughts. How utterly commonplace to think the last girl he met was like no other girl he had known. Surely he was beyond the silly calf moonings of youth, especially in his present condition. That had to be faced, too. With an actual physical gesture he thrust the ugly thought aside and his mind returned again to Anne. 

She certainly was different than any girl he had known. That was only his misfortune, of course, and his loss, but it was not the less true for all that. He should have known such a girl long, long ago. All of his life would have been different, but now there was nothing to be gained by reasoning in that way. For all his hardships life had not been so very hard on him up to now. 

He had taken things as he found them and enjoyed himself immensely in the process. He had revelled in food when he had it, eating with the fine gusto of those who know what it is to go hungry. When there was nothing to eat he had tried to laugh it off and be happy just the same. Of course he hadn't succeeded; one doesn't succeed, quite, in doing anything like that, but in retrospect it had always been jolly. 

For example his winter in West Palm Beach when he 87

FURNISHED ROOM 

had chummed about with the two Englishmen. Tom sat back on his bed and clasped a knee meditatively. He almost wished he smoked a pipe that he might wreathe himself with smoke as an aid to memory as men did in pictures. The three of them had been very gay in spite of all their difficulties. They had stayed at home evenings to play poker with the landlady so she wouldn't be too insistent on collecting the room rent. It was the one vulnerable point in the old lady's armor against the blandishments of young men who wanted a place to sleep and had no money to pay for it. All Florida was full of such young men that winter, but with stony visage and her tight, hard smile she had resisted them all. It was pay in advance or stay out. She had a way of folding her arms for emphasis as if to protect her house against the entrance of such miscreants. 

But the three of them had discovered her passion for giving "penny-ante" poker parties at which she yearned for attentive young men. Then her stony face creased into wreathing smiles; she patted her faded hair with a bony hand on which she shoved as many rings as possible; and for the first time one realized that the probable reason for her tight-lipped smile was the necessity for holding in her glittering false teeth. At her parties they were in danger of slipping most of the time, but, since she was the soul of precaution, she kept a dainty lace handkerchief on her lap as she played, and she was expert at snatching this up swiftly when necessary and covering her mouth as if to smother a ladylike laugh, but in reality to catch the unmentionables e'er they quite fell out.

Tom grinned and rolled back on the bed at the remembrance. What a game it always had been. The old lady invariably gave each of them a quarter to play with and at the beginning of the game they were supposed

FURNISHED ROOM 

89 to buy a quarter's worth of chips with reckless abandon. 

All the other guests would be elderly people who were only too delighted to make the stakes as small as possible.

The hostess would turn to Tom with a winning smile. "How many will you buy at first, Mr. Smith?" And

Tom would hesitate a moment in the manner of one about to corner the wheat market, and say, "I think a quarter's worth will make a good start," and everyone would agree eagerly, the chorus being artfully led by the two Englishmen. Whereupon the hostess would graciously consent and dole out chips with a sweet smile for each of the three protegés who played their respective parts so well. And so everything swung along gaily and the game began.

But there had been further difficulties to face in the game. After all, no matter how carefully one plays it is not difficult to lose twenty-five cents. And so there was a prearranged signal of distress upon seeing which the hostess would suddenly decide to serve refreshments and take the stranded one as her assistant. Looking back how funny it all was. And how eagerly they awaited the refreshments because they had had nothing to eat all day and often nothing to eat for two days. One had to agree that if the refreshments served left much to be desired in the way of daintiness, at least they were substantial. 

The other guests always ate as if they had too come chiefly for that purpose. As a meal it was worth a quarter.

Sometimes they had won and after it was all over the three of them would go arm in arm down the street to a little counter restaurant run by an unspeakably dirty Greek, but who seemed as gracious as Epicurus himself since he always served them black coffee and then considerately turned his back while they emptied the bowls of crackers lying about. It must be confessed that plenty

୨୦ 

FURNISHED ROOM 

of them went into coat pockets as reserve amunition against the necessities of the next day. After the first time they had been almost afraid to go back but the proprietor always welcomed them as if they were his prize customers. When there was a little change of fortune in the spring and they were to go their various ways they went to him, explained what had happened, and offered to pay for the damages. He waved them aside with a sweeping gesture worthy of old Athens. "You tink mebbe I don't know. I know. Every time 

I put doughnuts where you can reach 'em and I hope you take 'em. I tink mebbe if I give you get insult. Why don't you take 'em?"

Yes, that was the sort of memory that was precious as the years went by. And the old Jew who used to save stale cake and sandwiches from his soda fountain and give them to him. There was always something, and how often the bread was moist on sandwiches that were supposedly too stale to sell. What did the cruelty of having to accept such favors amount to in comparison with the joy of finding such splendid kindness and consideration. 

In some people's eyes it had all been very sordid, of course, but to him it had brought the happiest of experiences.

Tom's hands dropped from clasping his caught-up knee, and he slumped into his limp sitting posture. All that was gone now, was past in a cruel, irremedial way. 

Now he couldn't even accept such things were they offered to him. He was a sick man now. He was on a diet now. Even today he had drunk a cup of coffee, which was rigidly forbidden to him. Good God, how could he have gotten through his thirty years of life had it not been for coffee. How often almost for weeks at a time had he not lived on coffee and doughnuts, that horrible combination keeping so many people alive in our large

FURNISHED ROOM 

91 cities, and often enough there were no doughnuts. How many times had he loaded his coffee with four or five heaping spoonfuls of sugar because, even though he hated it so, it was sustaining that way. Now he must regret a tiny cup of after-dinner coffee. 

And there had been the matter of cocktails too. How foamy and tempting the drink looked when handed to him and he had taken an appreciative sip before he realized what he was doing. The doctor's words rang inside his head. "Remember, every drop of alcohol is so much poison added to your system, and you will suffer for it."

With almost a shudder he had replaced the tiny frosted glass on the tray. There had been jokes then about his not liking Mr. Dale's special, and offers of other tempting things equally hard to explain away. How lame his excuse had been that it was better for him not to touch drinks that would make him want what he could not get. Anne had looked at him with quiet wondering eyes.

It was clear his action had surprised her, and not fallen in with her preconceived notions formed so rapidly the night before. He had been expected to be very thirsty. 

How wide a circle his thoughts had swung, but now they were back with Anne. Was there something cruelly ironical about his meeting such a girl at this time, or was it possible that only now could he appreciate her quiet intellectual dignity? This was clearly not the sort of girl who climbed into bed with you either willingly or unwillingly; or for a moment toyed with the idea. 

She was not the sort to get hilarious with you over a bottle of gin, or maudlinly sentimental over a few bottles of beer in a shabby, stupid restaurant. Even her piano playing was so superior to anything he had ever known that it made it difficult for him to sing his best. 

For the first time he sensed the full richness and equal beauty of accompaniments that he had merely felt to be

FURNISHED ROOM 

framework on which to hang his voice. He was almost tempted to stop singing and drink in the hitherto only half-conceived beauty of it all. 

In the Schumann "Widmung" she had driven him along at breakneck speed until at the end he was breathless but strangely triumphant. They had tried it over three times until finally Mrs. Dale had clapped her hands softly, crying, "Now you have it. What a piece of ecstasy it is!" 

And he had been very happy. It had all been glorious and he had stood singing until his knees almost gave away under him; but at the same time it had been hard. 

It was more like work than the easy happy-go-lucky singing in which he usually indulged. But at the end 

Anne had turned and said, "Mr. Smith, you do sing well.

We must try everything I have. I think you have the makings of a real Lieder singer and mother will tell you 

I don't know any higher praise." Strangely enough it had been worth while.

On Monday night Tom went to the Bobbs' apartment to give the lesson which was his sole source of income. 

Cynthia, the child, was out but her mother was bubbling over with good humor and dying for a chance to talk.

Mrs. Bobbs was colossally, monumentally, incredibly fat. Her short arms seemed almost as wide as they were long, as she stretched them and the folds of fat hung down. Her tiny, elegantly shaped hands only accentuated the lack of proportion. As she sat sunk in her special chair she crossed her tiny feet which swung a good three inches above the floor, and the masses of flesh wrinkled about her ankles.

But she was always full of energy and struggled bravely to keep her mad theatrical family going, including Siegfried, a German strong man, out of a job. Just now she was full of preposterously grand plans, all of

FURNISHED ROOM 

93 which included Tom as she had taken a fancy to him, a fancy, however, which in no way interfered with her more marked fancy for Siegfried's bulging muscles.

Tom, in turn, told her of his audition with Meyers and his hopes for a small part. She had just begun to express her enthusiasm when the door flung open and Bobby, Mrs. Bobbs' dancing son, Siegfried and Cynthia, came dashing in with paper bags in their arms crying, “Hurrah! Hurrah!" They dumped a roast chicken, a bag of French fried potatoes, lettuce, tomatoes, etc., into 

Mrs. Bobbs' mountainous lap, then rushed joyously over to Tom, as if they hadn't seen him in a year, and, catching hands, began to dance wildly around his chair, shouting all the good news he had just heard at great length from the mother.

Mrs. Bobbs managed to hoist herself out of her chair, thereby spilling all the packages onto the floor while the chicken slipped from its bag altogether and rolled under the table leaving a trail of salt behind it on the carpet. Cynthia broke from the dancing circle and dove under the table after the fowl while Siegfried reaching down, caught her lightly by an ankle, and pulled child and chicken up into the air. Meanwhile, Mrs. Bobbs had gotten her balance and from the so-called kitchenette was wildly calling for the chicken, and for Bobby to clear the table, and for Mr. Smith to stay for supper, and for Siegfried to behave himself.

There was reason enough for that last, as the strong man had by now caught Cynthia by both feet and was whirling her about over his head in a great circle while the child clung desperately to the chicken and squealed her delight. Bobby being left with nothing to do suddenly began a quick cartwheel just to show sympathy with the general trend of proceedings. Pandemonium reigned.

FURNISHED ROOM 

Glancing up Tom saw Gwendolyn, the daughter, in the doorway with a shocked expression. It always seemed as if she timed her entrance for a scene of some sort. "Well I never," she cried, but her voice was lost in the general din. Tom, slunk down in his seat to keep out of range of the various acrobatics, grinned at her as she tried to come into the room and then ducked to avoid Cynthia as she flew squealing past still clutching her prize.

It all ceased as quickly as it had begun. The chicken flew from Cynthia's grip and whizzed into the bathroom, splashing gaily into a tub of water. As if by magic everyone stopped performing in order to shriek with laughter, after which there was a concerted rush for the bathroom to rescue the poor roast chicken. Mrs. Bobbs reached it first by the simple expedient of blocking off the others and then held up the dripping battered bird. 

There was another gale of laughter. "Well, one should always wash things before eating them," Tom cried as he wiped the tears from his eyes.

He felt somehow as if they were plotting against the sallow young waster who leaned against the side of the elevator with tired closed eyes. Soon they were packed in the front seat of the Rolls-Royce roadster and headed up Sixth Avenue.

All three sat silent, immersed in their own thoughts, as they swung into the park. The powerful car seemed to skim over the road as if the distance flowed toward and under it. To Tom it seemed to be whispering softly,

97 "Just see how easy money makes things. See how easily life flows by." He smiled at the vague disturbing thought and his mind ran ahead to the girl he was to meet and back to the one he had met a few nights before. Surely

TOM CHAPTER TEN 

'OM lay back on the hard couch in the doctor's office, limp and ragged. The doctor stood at his wash basin in the corner, quietly washing his hands. "Doctor." "Yes, Mr. Smith?" "How much longer is this to go on?" "The treatment is over for today. I know it is uncomfortable, but it has to be." "I don't mean today," Tom cried half hysterically. "I mean the whole thing; everything. I can't stand this any more. I'm trying to keep up, but I can't do it. Every time I fool myself into forgetting for a few minutes I have to torture myself again. I can't stand the pain. I'd sooner die than keep on suffering so every time I come here."

The doctor turned about with the towel still in hand. 

A cynical smile played about his lips. "There is nothing to gain by making a scene. Too many have been made in this office for a new one to impress me at all. There is pain, of course, but you magnify your sufferings by your imaginative temperament. I wish you would try to see that. And, of course, you make my work harder too." "My God, do you think I want to suffer more? Can 

I help the way I am? Ugh! It is all too horrible." "Now see here. You act as if you were alone in all this.

I am treating over fifty cases myself and many doctors are handling more. It is the commonest disease in Amer- 98

FURNISHED ROOM 

99 ica today. They all suffer too, maybe some not so much, but others probably more. Your financial situation makes it worse, your temperament makes it worse. Now brace up and let's not talk about it any more. That doesn't help any."

Tom pulled on his coat in silence, then turned to face the doctor. "And there's another thing," he said slowly as if in continuation of his previous thought. "I'm sorry but I can't pay you today. I know I said last time I'd pay you today, but I can't. I haven't anything but a nickel to get home on."

The doctor hesitated a moment and then spoke slowly. "All right. But you will remember I made you a very low price in consideration of cash payments." 

Tom laughed grimly. "I'm not likely to forget that, or anything connected with it. Sometimes I wish I could. 

But I hope things are going to be better soon. Tomorrow morning I report to Meyers, the theatrical producer, for his new musical comedy. I sang for him last week." 

The doctor faced him abruptly. "You are in no condition to go on the stage. You mustn't stand too much, far less dance or whatever would be required of you. 

It is absolute madness." "Perhaps, but you see it has to be done. I can't go on this way. It's a question of doing what I have to do." "But I forbid it," cried the physician. "It will upset all I'm trying to do. You may develop a chronic condition if you are not careful, and then you will suffer.” "I'll have to chance that." He walked swiftly up to to the doctor. "Do you know what it means to be hungry in this condition? Do you know it takes all I earn to pay my room rent and you? I borrow for medicine, I borrow for food and laundry; and whom have I to borrow from? Do you realize that situation? Anything I do in order to live is because I must. I'm blaming nobody for

FURNISHED ROOM 

it but myself but that doesn't make it any easier." He ended in a low tone as if talking to himself. "You will do as you please, but I have told you,” and he raised his shoulder and turned up his hands in the classic ages-old gesture of the Jew. For an instant the two men faced each other and then both relaxed into a smile. With a brief handshake Tom walked out, shoulders thrust back rigidly and head in the air. 

Next morning Tom saw Sylvia Blake walking ahead of him toward the stage door of the theatre and slowed his pace a little so as not to catch up with her. He would probably meet her on the stage but just now he would rather think about her than talk to her. The other night she had been a complete surprise and he had not known just how to take her. The strange thing was that Gwendolyn's description of her had been so accurate and yet so incomplete. She was certainly short and very animated, with brown hair, eyes, and complexion. But somehow that didn't picture the vibrant dynamic person she was.

At the same time there was a strange virginal quality about her that defied analysis. It had nothing in common with the quiet intellectual purity of Anne Dale.

There was something about Anne of the quality of stained glass that glowed warmly under the light but still remains supremely itself and aloof from the glorious colors with which it stains the common objects of life. Sylvia had no such glamour about her. Her virginal quality was clearly sheer innocence and rendered the more childlike by her bubbling gaiety of manner. Her spontaneity was the fine overflowing of effervescent youth. 

And there was no doubt Bob Landers was completely fascinated by her, as a man of his type might well be. 

It was clear what he thought of the usual girl who enter-

FURNISHED ROOM 

ΙΟΙ tained him in her apartment at midnight, but here was something bewilderingly, fascinatingly new. Tom had looked at Gwendolyn and sighed. How hopeless was her liking for Bob. Pretty she was, with a lithe, sinuous, dancer's body, but were there not several thousand on Broadway enough like her to pair in a sister act at any time. And they were all just "desire" to Bob Landers.

How many of them had he toyed with and thrown by as one would a scooped-out melon rind. They had streamed in and out of his bed and had meant nothing permanently but weakness, drunkenness, delusion, boredom-all the things which women come to mean to a man they try to hold against his will. So much for 

Gwendolyn. And on her part it seemed the careless, mercenary description of her liking for Landers scarcely covered the case. Life was not quite as easy as all that and Tom was sorry it wasn't. The true gold-digger is at least free from emotional entanglements. 

And then Sylvia. Tom was not such a fool as to be blind when a girl fairly flung herself at him. She was positively radiant when Gwendolyn introduced them, in fact, she cried out that she knew who he was the minute he walked in the door. They all walked into the living room and a single glance about would convince anyone that pressing need of money scarcely bothered Sylvia. 

There was fine, easy comfort about the living room that touched Tom's heart. From all his vaunting a scorn of the common amenities of life they had always meant more to him than he cared to acknowledge. There was a sheer joy in Sylvia's tasteful room for example. The eye rested gently on a dozen beautiful objects, even in a single cursory glance about. Sylvia's gay chatter gave him a chance to glance about before she suddenly realized they were all standing. She pushed everyone into deep easy chairs and then produced a tray with a variety

FURNISHED ROOM 

of bottles and glasses. Gwendolyn and Landers accepted 

White Rock high-balls. She turned to Tom with a ludicrous imitation of the barman gesture. "I know what you'll have. I'm sure I can guess, Scotch and soda." She held up a bottle gaily. "Look, I have

Schweppe's soda, straight from London. And even if the

McPherson so what more can a poor girl do?" "I'm sorry but I'm not allowed to drink anything. 

Doctor's orders." 

They all stared and then Bob grunted "Oh hell,” and took a deep draught. It was clear he already resented 

Sylvia's reception of Tom. As for the girl she looked blank for a moment and then cried, "I know now. How about some Cointreau, the opera singer's special? Right off the boat this time. I carried it off a French boat with my own lily-white hands so there can be no mistake unless some gay bootlegger carried it on, which I doubt." 

Tom shook his head slowly. "I really can't. My throat is terribly sensitive. Couldn't I just have some of the soda and join you that way?" 

Gwendolyn laughed. "I know it's because I'm here.

He feels he must be proper before the big sister of his prize pupil. He's so proper anyway. Bobby calls him Sir 

Galahad. My kid sister looked up at the gent and agreed with him."

I went right out and bought a copy the other day after you sang it. So there are no excuses.'

FURNISHED ROOM 

103 of the hour but she thrust the idea aside as being unworthy of any consideration. She played fairly well in the slap-dash musical comedy manner and it was clear at once that she was very partial to sentimental ballads and theme songs in languorous waltz time. Takt" and made him go through it again from the beginning.

Meanwhile Landers was obviously annoyed and not much appeased when Gwendolyn moved alongside him on the heavy sofa and began to talk with great animation and rolling of her eyes. "Don't you like music?" he cried rudely. "Why of course, Bob," she pouted. "Then listen and concentrate. Maybe you can imitate and learn to stay on key a little better in your opening chorus." He deliberately swung his body the other way and watched Sylvia with his head cupped in a lean hand.

His other hand slowly and steadily kept raising the glass to his lips and replenishing it from the bottles on the tray. Gwendolyn picked up a magazine from a small table at the other end of the couch and began to read ostentatiously. From the corner of his eye Tom watched the comedy. There had been no doubt Landers was boiling over. 

But all that was last night. Now he must be careful.

His reminiscence carried him to the stage door where a group was loitering about as is customary at first rehearsals. One man nodded to him and he said "Hello," even though he had no idea who it was. Inside there was a restless throng but this time there was a kind of purposefulness about it which had been lacking before. 

These people had all been chosen during the last week or two and now it was necessary to justify that choice. 

Everyone knew there were dozens ready to step into his

FURNISHED ROOM 

shoes and so the careless gaiety of the tryouts had turned into the vital determinaton of the first rehearsal. Tom had often heard of it, but even so the sight was sufficiently startling.

Before he really had time to accustom his eyes to the combination of darkness and glaring lights, there was a stir at the footlights, then a swift pulsing melody from the piano on the stage, and a light figure flung itself into the glare of lights and began to dance. With a start Tom recognized Sylvia in tiny black velvet pants and a fluttering white silk blouse. She was dancing exceedingly well, he saw that at once, and moreover everyone was liking it. The high thin voice of Meyers called out: "Very good, Miss Blake. Will you find out where Mr. 

Random is rehearsing tomorrow and meet him there. 

And you might drop into my office about five this evening. Thank you, my dear."

A murmur ran through the crowd. In the profession there is no mistaking "the sound a good contract makes in advance," as someone near Tom whispered. He stood back to watch the stage door, hoping to be able to congratulate Sylvia, but when she did come out she was the center of an animated group among whom Bob Landers was very prominent. Tom did not know that it never takes the satellites longer than five minutes to discover and swarm about the rising star. The doorkeeper bowed obsequiously to Landers and Tom smiled a little. There was evidently Landers money in the show or the theatre, perhaps in both, and with equally little doubt it had been used in Sylvia's favor. The next instant he hated himself for the thought as he remembered how well she had just danced, but the thought would not die, curse it as he might. It annoyed him that he was a little piqued. 

He turned his attention back to the footlights where a roll call was in progress. It dragged on slowly and at

FURNISHED ROOM 

105 last the caller bawled, "Everyone whose name was not called please leave the sage."

For a moment he stood still as several people pushed past him, then he began to squeeze toward the man who still stood holding the long sheet of names. His name had not been called, of that he was sure. "My name was not called," he cried finally as the man was turning away. "Who are you?" "Tom Smith." He couldn't keep a little catch out of his voice. The man ran his eye down the sheet. "Smith?

Your name's not here. I thought it was. I remember you well." He paused a second in thought. "I guess you got scratched somehow. Sorry. Better luck next time.”

Tom turned away with his head reeling. He knew they were all looking at him and feeling sorry for him, as actors do. They all know so well what such blows mean and how one must smile it off as if it were of no consequence. Then he gritted his teeth and turned toward the orchestra. He knew Meyers was out there somewhere and so looked earnestly into the darkness. 

Then he spotted him, standing near the other corner of the stage. He crossed swiftly. "Mr. Meyers, didn't you tell me to report today?"

There was a belligerent tone in his voice. "Yes. I did. What is the matter?" "My name is not on the list."

A man walked swiftly out of the shadows of the orchestra and whispered into Meyers' ear. Tom waited numbly. Then Meyers' suave tones reached him. "Yes, I remember your case now, Mr. Smith. You said you didn't dance and we needed a dancer where we hoped to use you. Mr. Random was most particular about it. Come around again in about a month or so. I'm putting on something then where I'll need your voice. 

Sorry about this. Good day."

FURNISHED ROOM 

"Thank you, Mr. Meyers." He turned quickly into the shadows at the back of the stage. He was afraid there might be tears in his eyes and he didn't dare lift a hand to brush them away. It was clear enough that someone had blocked him and it took no great powers to know who. Even Meyers himself hadn't known of his being dropped. He walked slowly out past the aged doorkeeper who paid no attention to his passing. 

Outside on the street the sun was bright with glorious

October brilliance and the air clean and invigorating. 

He walked east to Broadway and then down toward 

42nd Street. It shouldn't bother him as it did but he couldn't laugh it off as he knew he should. All theatrical promises were so cheap at best and, in truth, he had not been given any promise. But this time the hurt went deeper. He had been tricked out of something he needed and needed desperately. Meyers' manner had settled all doubts. Meyers had really wanted him. His first chance in years had been snatched from his grasping hands.

Now where was he to turn. He hadn't eaten since last night and he hadn't a cent in his pocket. He felt tired too, dead, dog-tired, and there was no place to go.

But even he did not quite realize what he was facing as he walked numbly down Broadway that bright 

October morning. The next month was a frantic struggle that kept him in a panic of fear all day long. After the loss of his hope with Meyers he tried a number of the offices and agencies but there was nothing for him and he was not surprised. Without friends or experience nothing but a lucky break could give him a start, and having had that break he had lost out. That was the awful part of it all he felt as he wandered about hungrily or lay suffering on the bed. Even his ghastly red room was no longer a haven of refuge as he had run be-

107 hind in his rent in order to eat and there seemed to be no way of catching up. There were times when it seemed Alfred suffered everything his friend suffered.

Tom had turned eagerly toward the Bobbs on the hope that some of their wild plans might easily come to fruition, but as was to be expected their dreams remained dreams. Siegfried talked louder than ever of what he intended to do, but even he had his serious moments as he saw Mrs. Bobbs was turning against him too, and telling him it was about time he did something to support himself.

Only Gwendolyn kept on working steadily but then she left during the second week to go on her trip west.

The poor thing, weighted down with a mountain of flesh, `had troubles enough of her own, as she sat day by day in her chair, scarcely ever setting foot out of doors, waiting for the great and marvellous things that were to happen to her children. It was only Wednesday and the end of the week seemed far away to Tom. He was so hungry he felt exhausted as he turned down 47th Street to Broadway. 

He stood a moment at the Palace Theatre reading the posters there announcing the coming attractions. He wished he were able to go there this afternoon and forget his troubles watching the fairly feeble spectacle, but cheap as the Palace was, it was far too expensive for a man who hadn't even ten cents for doughnuts and coffee.

How he would have relished even these forbidden foods which his stomach clamored for. 

He heard his name called and turned slowly to face 

Bob Landers who was holding out his hand cordially. 

Tom took it, dumbly wondering why he did so. "What are you doing, Smith?" "Reading the posters, that's all." Tom noticed that the man was quite drunk and swayed as he stood. "Aw, don't be like that, you know what I mean. Got a job?" A wave of the arms imperilled his precarious balance.

IIO FURNISHED ROOM 

"No! In fact I have just lost the one I did have teaching Gwendolyn's sister." "Ain't it tough! You poor kid." He swayed until Tom reached out a hand to steady him. "Gee, but didn't they have one Hell of a nerve to have a tutor for the kid when they're broke? Just crazy. I told Gwendolyn so before she went away."

Tom pushed a crazy thought back as it tried to force its way into his mind. It was absurd to let himself think, even for a moment, that he had lost his tutoring work also because of Landers. The fact was that it really had been ridiculous for people in the position of the Bobbs family to engage a tutor, and certainly at the moment they could not afford it. Landers swayed again and this time Tom had to use two hands to support him. "Got an idea. Let's go to lunch at the Tavern. I'll treat you to a good lunch. Come on."

For a second Tom hesitated, but the thought of a good meal at a good restaurant made him accept. Landers took hold of his arm confidentially as if they were lifelong friends and Tom was amazed at himself to find he didn't even resent it. In a story he would have drawn himself up proudly and cried, "How dare you touch me. And do you flatter yourself that I will accept your invitation to lunch after the way you have acted. I scorn your rich fancy food." But this wasn't a story. It was life. 

Once in the restaurant and seated Landers waved a hand spaciously and told him to go the whole hog. Tom did. He ordered Lynnhaven Oysters, Green Turtle Soup, English Mutton Chops Broiled, Baked Idaho Potatoes, Brussels Sprouts, Tomatoes stuffed with Chicken Salad, and Camembert cheese. Landers opened his eyes and said "the same," then continued with a grin, "You may not drink but you do eat, don't you?"

FURNISHED ROOM III 

"Whenever I get a chance," Tom answered quietly.

The food braced Landers up a bit and toward the end he took Tom's address. "Say, maybe I can find something for you. Can you go to Chicago?" "Why not?" 

There was no answer but Tom was not surprised.

Before they parted outside the restaurant the man reiterated his intentions of placing Tom in a show, and then insisted on lending him ten dollars. Tom accepted with very little persuasion. He had no faith in Landers' promises of help and the money would keep a roof over his head and pay for one visit to the doctor whom he now owed for several treatments, and whose manner reflected the fact clearly.

When he entered the hall of his house he found himself face to face with Roseanna whom he had not seen since he had walked off leaving her limp body on the bed with his finger marks red and livid on the whiteness of her throat. He had left her with all the loathing which would be felt toward a snake that had struck one viciously.

Time had moderated his feelings a little but he had hoped he would never meet her. His memories of her were too conflicting, too horrible. And now he stood face to face with her in the hall. He tried to push past her without a word but she blocked him with a quickly outstretched arm. "Aren't you going to speak to me?" "I don't see there is anything we could have to say to each other." He eyed her coldly. "You might apologize for the other night," she volunteered with a sly smile. He shook his head slowly. "I don't feel in the least apologetic about it. I'm glad 

I didn't kill you because that would only cause me more

FURNISHED ROOM 

trouble. Otherwise I have no feelings about the matter in any way different from what I had then. Now I would like to go past, if you please.”

She dropped her arms to her sides slowly and her words moved slowly too. "You are very hard. You must be suffering terribly to make you change so. Can't I help you in some way? I want to make amends." "It will help if we never speak to each other again." "I don't mean that. Why are you so hard? I know how poor you are. Can't I lend you a little money?" "Mrs. Crowley," he cried bitterly, "I have just had lunch with a man who made me lose the first decent chance I've had in years. I accepted his food because hunger strips a man of pride. I also took a loan of ten dollars to pay my rent because poverty strips a man of decency. But I've not yet sunk low enough to accept help from you.' دو She started back a little at the biting cruelty of the words, but plunged desperately a last time. "Don't you see I want to help? Move up to my room and I'll take care of you. You don't hate me enough not to want to get well. I could nurse you and be good to you. Please let me. I won't touch a drink. I promise it."

He looked squarely into her eyes for a second, and then walked past down the hall and into his room. A horrible desire to seize the woman by the throat clutched at his heart and made him mistrust the spontaneous actions of his hands if he stood near Roseanna for another minute. He couldn't believe his own ears. After what had happened between them how was it possible for her to suggest they should now live together. It was unbelievable. He closed his door carefully as if to shut out remembrance. 

A note was pinned to his mirror, "I must have some money today," and signed "Mrs. T-." He tore it up

FURNISHED ROOM 

113 and flung the pieces toward the basket, which they missed, and lay in white splotches on the dirty red carpet. He sank down on the bed with a crushed feeling of weariness. In a few minutes he would go pay Mrs. Tillinghast six dollars and keep her quiet.

An hour later he got up and going into the hall knocked on the landlady's door. She greeted him frozenly. "Well!" "I want to pay a week's rent." "I suppose you know you owe me two back and one in advance." She nodded her head savagely. "Yes, I know, I'll give you the rest in a day or two." 

He smiled wearily. "I hope so," she sneered and the door slammed.

He turned back into his own room. He was safe for at least a few days.

THE CHAPTER ELEVEN 

HE weeks dragged on slowly. Three or four times 

Tom had dinner at the Murray's who were maddeningly concerned about his diet and Mrs. Murray especially seemed anxious to compare symptoms and ailments. Tom responded so badly that they told Alfred they thought Tom's sickness had not done him much good. "I don't quite see how any sickness could do anybody any good," retorted that loyal friend. "It might teach him the wickedness of his way of life," cried his mother with some heat. "When anyone's on a severe diet like that it's probably because they've sinned in some way. I feel sure of that."

Alfred made no reply to this. A dozen retorts were easy enough, varying from the mildly cynical to the openly rude, but he felt she had blundered too close to the truth to be encouraged to probe further into the reasons for Tom's diet. And the crux of the matter lay in the inconveniences it caused. The Murrays were triumphant "Sunday roast-beefers" as they themselves phrased it and they found leg of lamb a bad substitute. 

Chicken, in Mr. Murray's phrase, was "just a lot of work and not much to eat" and the tradition of it meant nothing to him. They explained all this to Alfred in the course of a long discussion one evening. "Then why do you ask him here if his coming puts you out so much? We always have fish then and he eats that."

I'm sure all men do unless they catch the fish themselves and then they'd eat it if it was poison." "Then why do we always have fish on Fridays?" "Because everyone does. Now don't say another word.

Everyone has fish on Friday and that's all there is to it.' وو So Alfred was placed in a dilemma of not wanting Tom to come and disrupt his parents' schedule, and of wanting him to come because it meant two splendid Sunday meals to a hungry man. the train for East Orange. As she shook hands she said earnestly: "Then we shall expect you Sunday at noon?" "Thank you, yes. I shan't fail you any more."

The first mail Saturday morning brought a note from Anne containing the railroad ticket he had returned before. No reference was made to it; it was just there. 

He appreciated the blunt delicacy of it. At 1:30 he was at Sardi's hoping he would not have to wait long in his shabby clothes. He was sure the doorman under the awning entrance hesitated about letting him in, but Sylvia arrived an instant later and it was clear her prettiness and elegance of appearance overwhelmed the doorman, and her greeting of Tom set all that functionary's possible doubts at rest.

Sylvia was in a gay mood and rattled on about her rehearsals and how she loved it all. She was also full of ideas for her party and at least three times asked Tom if he wouldn't change his mind and come. For his part he found each refusal a little easier than the former one. 

There was no doubt that Sylvia was not showing up at her best in comparison with Anne's superior mind and poise. If she was trying to impress him it was a mistake to eat again in the same restaurant when the memories of his luncheon with Anne was still poignant. And if such an interpretation was mere masculine vanity what other meaning could there be. If she was merely feeling sorry for him and wanting to give him a meal Sardi's was scarcely the place to do it. No one ever went there just to eat. There was always an ulterior reason. 

Over the coffee he watched Sylvia. Even her vivid prettiness was no match for Anne's quiet beauty, and the prospects of her party sounded almost childish in their madcap humor. He was rather relieved when they parted, but Sylvia's good humor was undiminished. He

FURNISHED ROOM 

121 put her into a taxi to take her to her apartment and she waved goodbye from the window crying, "If you change your mind tonight, come along." He bowed and waved his hat in reply. Frankly he was glad he was not going.

It was pleasanter to go home and lie down and think about next day. He looked up the train in the time table and then laughed at his folly. He knew well enough when it went. What was he looking for in the long columns of figures.

CHAPTER TWELVE 

DECEMBER Clm. He overcoat

ECEMBER came in cold and disagreeable and Tom felt it severely. He had neither overcoat nor hat and his shoes barely held together on his feet, as there were gaping holes in both soles and uppers. Day after day he plowed about in the cold dreary rain looking for work, any kind of work, but there was nothing he could do. 

Tutoring agencies, theatrical agencies, ordinary employment agencies alike produced no tangible results. It seemed there was no need for an unskilled person of education. Indeed there never had been, but in his need he felt that his very desperation must produce tangible results. His eyes began to look haggard and the gay laugh was as dead as if it never had been. A gaunt look of despair was now his chief characteristic and he knew it, which only made matters worse, since the cruellest part of being in dire straits is to look beaten. This drags one down until it becomes useless to make any effort since all effort is foredoomed to failure, and one crawls about hopelessly in a lethargic stupor. 

Even when the insidious gnawing of hunger was eased for a moment it was done in bitter ironical style. One 

Sunday he would gorge himself with forbidden roast beef at the Murray's and the next Sunday with more elaborate dishes at the Dale's. In between stretched a horrible waste space. Alfred would be able to buy him some sandwiches about twice a week, but generally whatever money he could lend had to go for medicine or a visit to the doctor. About once a week he met Anne and she would take him to luncheon, always a splendid meal and the only happy one for him. For one hour or 122

FURNISHED ROOM 

123 two he forgot the horror of his predicament. Then Anne came to realize his passion for the theatre and after that timed her lunches for Wednesday or Thursday so they could attend a matinée together. 

Once he met Bob Landers and that worthy, drunk as usual, took him to lunch again at the Tavern, where he ate until he felt quite sick. He did often at this time when he tried to pack away food in the futile hope that he might stave off hunger for a longer period of time.

He knew it was merely the natural revolt of a stomach which had been treated to alternate fast and famine for too long and was finally putting up protest. It was particularly annoying that it should happen when he was with Landers who again talked all through the meal about what he wanted to do for him. 

Once out in the street his sudden indigestion became so painful that he was forced to ask Landers to buy him a bromo-seltzer. His host arose to the occasion and became very solicitous about him. After leaving the drug store he pushed a ten dollar bill into Tom's hand. Tom forced himself to demur even as his fingers closed tightly about the bit of paper he wanted so badly. "But I still owe you ten dollars." "To Hell with it. Pay me when you get rich." With a wave of the hand Landers ambled off. 

So again he could pay a week's rent which was now more in arrears than ever and also pay Dr. Krassin for one visit. His landlady had taken to waylaying him in the hall and having bitter scenes, for which her other roomers cheerfully opened their doors so as to miss none of the choicely bitter invectives. Even when he paid her something on account of back-rent, it was the same, though in a minor key, and for a day or two he was not waylaid. That was something to be thankful for, no matter how ignominiously the respite was gained.

FURNISHED ROOM 

He called on Mrs. Bobbs several times and the lady seemed glad to see him, but he had not been able to collect the six dollars she owed him. Once she insisted on his staying to eat with her and Cynthia, and they had revelled in pork chops and fried potatoes. How little the doctor's orders not to eat pork meant when he hadn't eaten a morsel of food in three days. Another time she had with equal cordiality insisted on his staying to eat graham crackers and milk with them. How good the simple food had been and with a smile he told himself that this time, at least, he was well within the limits of his narrow diet. 

And once he met Linda Larkin on 50th Street not far from his house. He felt he really did not want to talk to anyone that day, much less anyone like Linda, but she gave him no choice in the matter. As a rule they merely smiled or nodded to each other with the briefest of salutations, but today she held out her hand in greeting and after the handshake retained her grip. "I'm glad I met you. I want to have a long talk with you. I must talk to someone.' 

He smiled feebly. "I hope you're not in trouble.” 

She looked up into his eyes earnestly. "No, not like you mean, but in another way I am. Where can we talk? There are too many people here." "I don't know."

He was not helpful and his tone showed he did not intend to be. But Linda was blind to such minor considerations as inclinations. "I thought Bobby told me you had a room on this street. Are you ashamed to take me there?" 

This time she touched him to the quick. "Yes, Linda, I am ashamed," he cried. "I live in a filthy little pig sty that I am ashamed to take a lady into." 

She flushed a little under her badly applied rouge.

The words "Big Boy" made him wince. They were bitter reminders of Roseanna and all that he now inevitably associated with her name. As he led Linda into the hall he half expected her to be waiting there so powerful were the two words to evoke her image. But no one was there and they passed into the dingy red room in silence. The girl looked around her carefully from where she stood, then sighed deeply. "Poor kid. I thought it was something like this. I wish I could help you." "You do help me, Linda. Just to see you braces a man up this weather." "Get out," she cried raucously, but she looked pleased just the same. She shivered a little. "Boy, it's cold in here. Don't you have no heat?"

She would be very understanding, of course, but her very understanding would make her shrink from him and possible contamination. After all it was her business to be well and one couldn't blame her.

But aside from that he did feel worse than he ever had felt, with a horrible ache in his head and eyes accompanied by a ghastly feeling of exhaustion. Yet he managed to grin. "It's nothing, Linda, just low spirits, I guess."

"I still do want to talk with you but I'll come in tomorrow. Maybe we'll both feel better. Bye bye." She sidled out of the door and was gone.

With a sigh that was half groan he pulled off some of his clothes and lay down, pulling the stained torn comforter up to his chin. He had better rest until Alfred came at eight o'clock. Alfred said he was going to take him to a play and he would certainly not enjoy it if he felt so horribly weak. But a play would change his current of thoughts and he must go. His eyes closed and he fell into a heavy snoring doze.

He awoke about six-thirty and sat up drearily. He had never felt quite so bad before and he began to be frightened about his condition. He counted his change and found that he had forty-three cents. It took every ounce of strength to drag on his clothes. Perhaps he had better use the forty-three cents to eat, he reasoned. It might make him feel better.

He almost staggered as he walked up the street to Sixth Avenue and turned down to the cafeteria where he went whenever he had a little money. It was cold on the street and there was a faint drizzle of rain in the air. He shivered a little as he ran across 49th Street just ahead of the traffic and then almost stumbled into the cafeteria. It was not crowded now as it invariably was

FURNISHED ROOM 

127 in the middle of the day. A few people were scattered about and there were only three men behind the counters. The girl at the cashier's desk smiled as she handed him his ticket. Her glasses flashed. "I haven't seen you in quite a while. Have you been out of town?"

He ran his hand through his heavy wet hair. It was beginning to be a little hard to joke about going about hatless and claiming that the rain was good for his hair. 

With an effort he pulled his mind back to answer the question he had heard vaguely. "No, I'm still in town. I've been sick." "You don't look well," she said earnestly. "You must take care of yourself, especially in this weather." "Yes. It certainly is nasty tonight." 

She leaned on her little counter. "I'm reading a fine book. 'Anthony Adverse.' Have you read it?" "No, I haven't had time to read much lately."

With an apologetic smile he wandered down to get a bowl of soup. The girl settled back to her book with grave concentration. Tom took the soup to a table where he could be alone and started to eat slowly. He had been sure he was very hungry but now he realized he couldn't force the soup down his throat. His stomach simply refused to accept food and an overwhelming feeling of nausea was creeping over him. He pushed the bowl away and went to the door leading down to the men's room. 

Once there he tried to vomit but there was only a horrible retching and no further results. It couldn't be anything he had eaten because he hadn't eaten for twentyfour hours. He caught sight of his face, haggard and drawn, in the little mirror over the washstand.

and as he swung back held on grimly. He realized that the girl was looking at him anxiously and heard her voice as from far away. "You must go home quickly. Is there anything I can do?" 

He realized he was shaking his head as he paid fifteen cents for his soup, and then he staggered blindly out into the night. His knees shook under him and he leaned on a wet mailbox for support before going further. 

With slow quavering steps he crept down 50th Street with the gay theatre-bound crowds pushing past him in both directions. When he reached his house Alfred was standing in the vestibule waiting. After a single glance he ran down the steps anxiously. "What's the matter, Tom?" "Have you any money?" Tom gasped. "All right. 

Get a taxi quick. We're going to Dr. Krassin! I'm—I'm going to die this time!”

Less than an hour later Tom sat on the hard couch in the doctor's office stripped to the waist. For ten minutes the doctor had poked and probed and used the stethoscope over every inch of his torso. The heavy asthmatical breathing held Tom in terror, especially when it lingered behind him and there was nothing save it and the delicate, cool touch of the metal disk on his hot skin and the hoarse whispered command. "Breathe deeply and exhale slowly." Finally the doctor put the instrument away meticulously and crossing to the door leading to the waiting room called Alfred. "Mr. Smith is a very sick man. Now tell me, are his living conditions much as he has told me?" "I don't know what he's told you, but they couldn't be much worse.

129 become a little acquainted with Mr. Smith's temperamental outbursts. Has he any place to go, anyone to care for him for a while?" "I'm sure there is no one. I wish I could help but—” he smiled pitifully.

I think you had better understand that and go to the hospital. I'll call up Mount Sinai now and make arrangements for you."

In an instant Tom was galvanized into action. He jumped from the couch. "No! I won't go to the hospital! 

I won't! I won't!" His voice ended in a sob.

The doctor swiftly caught his arms again in his characteristic gesture. "Do you realize your condition? You have a fever of almost 105 degrees. You have a real attack of influenza and I don't mean a fancy name for a bad cold. I mean the real thing. You need the best of care, real hospital care.' 

Tom flung back his head wildly. "I don't care. I can't go to a hospital." "But I will arrange for you. There will be no charge.” "It isn't that. It's this other rotten thing. I can't do it. 

I won't go to a hospital." "You are quite mad," cried the doctor. "You are endangering your life. You have no right to do that." "I don't care. I won't go. I'll die first."

FURNISHED ROOM 

in hospitals in your condition. That means nothing to doctors and nurses. The sicker you are, the more they want to make you well. It's their work in life.” 

Tom had by now struggled into his undershirt and shirt. The tails of the latter hung out ludicrously. "It's no use. You may be right, but I simply can't do it. I'd rather die right here. I mean that."

Without a word the doctor wrote two prescriptions and then turned to Alfred. "At least you understand he is a very sick man. Take him home in a taxi and get him to bed with a hot water bottle at once. Two or three if you can get them. Also a cold pack on his head. He is to take these two medicines alternately, one every hour. 

Begin with this one which acts on the stomach. Be with him as much as you can. He is going to have a hard time." 

He turned to Tom. "And drop all your other treatments. This comes first now. You understand that?" 

Tom nodded.

Two or three minutes later they were in the taxi rolling down West End Avenue. Tom lay back with closed eyes breathing heavily. Alfred's face was set in grim lines as he looked steadily out of the window. He felt he didn't dare look at the horrible mockery of his friend sitting beside him and suffering. Surely some cruel Fate was pursuing Tom, tracking him down at every turn. 

Perhaps this was to be the end, and the hideous "flu" death of which he had heard so much was to round out the strangely groping life, that had dared so much and had done so little. The heavy breathing punctuated his thoughts.

Suddenly there was a gasp and the breathing stopped in a choke. "Tom!" cried Alfred.

131 ridiculous." He began to sing softly. "Nessum maggior dolore. دو 

The chauffeur cast a quick glance about. Alfred leaned forward to speak to him. "Can you manage to drive any faster? My friend has the 'flu' and I'm afraid he's dying." 

The hard face of the driver softened. "Gee, mister, I'll do my best."

The car swung down Tenth Avenue. A hooting whistle and the blaze of lights proclaimed the imminent sailing of one of the beautiful Italian liners. It drew Alfred's mind away from New York and its pressing immediate problems to Tom's dream of living in Italy. How often he and Tom had planned as if they had money, and pored over steamship folders and books of travel together. How beautifully Tom had been able to talk on and on, about the country he never had seen, maybe never would see. Alfred shivered a little with apprehension for the crumpled figure huddled beside him. A low moan came from the parted lips and again the soft, recitative singing of these immortal words of Dante beginning, "Nessum maggior dolore-" 

Like Paolo Tom was now being blown hither and thither on the very winds of Hell in payment for his sin. And yet it was not sin either but, in both cases, the natural pulsing law of life, taking its natural predestined course. Why should man's stupidity and ignorance have brought such dire punishment on the basic impulses of mankind, the natural mating of two bodies? It almost seemed as if triumphant Christian bigotry had planted germs in order to further its doctrine of self-abnegation. 

He shuddered at the horror of the thought. And now poor Tom. No wonder in his half delirium he was singing Paolo's poignant words, "No greater grief than to remember days of joy when misery is at hand."

FURNISHED ROOM 

When they arrived at 50th Street Tom was able to pull himself together long enough to walk unsupported into the house and unlock the doors. But once inside his room he sank limply on the bed and it was a long job for Alfred to get him undressed and under the bed clothes. Once he had him quiet, with a cold towel on his head, Alfred dashed out to fill the prescriptions. How lucky it was that he had gotten his pay that day. It was not much but it had paid the taxi fares and would buy Tom's most pressing immediate necessities. Without doubt there would be an annoying scene with his father and mother over his salary. He had always turned it over so meekly to be saved for him. To be sure his father doubled it every week to build up a bank account for him, but what could be more important than to help Tom out, when there was no one else and he lay at death's door. It wasn't as if his parents needed his pathetic little help; it was being done for his sake. Without doubt they would not understand his turning the money over to Tom. And for his part he was only ashamed that he hadn't done so before. Just so far apart could parents and children drift emotionally.

Within half an hour he was back with the medicines and found Tom in a strange delirium. He would sing softly for a minute or two and then stop abruptly and cry softly, "No, no, you mustn't sing, mustn't sing, mustn't sing." Then he would laugh, a gurgling halfmad laugh that went on and on, but always ended suddenly in a choking sob.

Alfred managed to rouse him to take the first dose of medicine but immediately after he sank back into the half delirium and began to sing softly, "M'appari tutt' amor" from Martha. There was something ghastly about the lilting little melody under such conditions. With tender care Alfred filled the hot water bottle and ar-

FURNISHED ROOM 

133 ranged the bed clothes. Then he soaked the towel again in cold water and placing it on the burning forehead sat down to wait the hour until the next dose.

The weak naked bulb of light made the ghastly red room more than horrible. It seemed such a cruelly appropriate setting for delirium. Belasco himself had never designed with truer inspiration. It was perfect, sordid realism. In this room there had been so much suffering, and sickness, and anxiety, and so little simple joy. It was a room that cried out it had never known pure happiness. There had been orgies here, and drunkenness here, and lewdness, and bawdiness, and immorality, and perversions, inversions, and subversions. But no little child had ever laughed here, and no bride had ever come here, and, probably, no happy young man with his chariot of dreams hitched to a star. One couldn't imagine their coming, and if they did it was unthinkable that they should stay. Only some battered pawn of life like

Tom came here, some log of driftwood that was not wanted because no wise man had seen that it was mahogany in spite of its appearance.

At two-thirty Tom seemed to rouse himself and began to talk quite calmly. The medicines were now active and he was dripping with perspiration. With a deep sigh Alfred saw that a good start had been made toward breaking the fever and Tom was responding well to the treatment even in his weakened condition. "Do you understand me, Tom?" But Tom's clearness of mind was a thing of the moment and before long he sank into worse delirium than before. He rolled and tossed, groaning dully, and finally managed to kick off the bed clothes without realizing it. A horrible feeling of nausea pulled him back into partial consciousness and he staggered to his feet, bent on getting to the bathroom upstairs. His naked sweating body seemed to catch the biting cold air of the room and he shook so he could hardly manage to open the door. The hall was pitch dark and he crept out with trembling limbs, his hand groping blindly before him. 

Some time later Mrs. Crowley found her last bottle of gin was empty and decided to go to bed. She opened her door to get to the bathroom, then closed it again swiftly. A naked man was sitting on the head of the stairs singing and moaning horribly. With a sudden premonition she flung open her door again. The light flooded out into the hall and she saw her surmise had been correct.

She ran quickly and put her arms about the trembling figure but there was no indication that he noticed her

FURNISHED ROOM 

135 presence. "Tom! Tom!" she whispered. "What's the matter with you? Where are your clothes?"
