His eyes opened wildly and he began to sing softly, then broke off at once. "Sh-h-h! Mustn't sing. I mustn't sing."

She led him gently down the stairs and into his room. 

He shivered convulsively but seemed to have no knowledge of what he was doing. She managed to straighten the bed clothes and get him tucked in while he only moaned, "I'm cold! So cold." The light, which she had snapped on on entering the room proclaimed the trouble easily enough and she soon had the hot water bottle by his side and the wet cloths on his forehead. For a moment she studied the two boxes of drugs. It was clear one must be taken every hour and she held him up while he docilely swallowed the powder and took a few sips of water to wash it down. Then he sank almost immediately into a deep, troubled sleep.

All night long Roseanna sat by the bed keeping the tumbled clothes adjusted and preserving the heat generated by the drugs. Only for a moment she left him to get her fur coat and a blanket to wrap about her legs. The drink was soon chilled out of her body as she sat there, and then she realized quite calmly what she was doing, and the chance she was taking but she laughed grimly at the idea. The roughness of her life had left little fears of such a thing as "flu," or pneumonia, or whatever it was.

Just as daylight was creeping in through the grimy windows he suddenly regained half-consciousness and grasped her hand convulsively, crying, "Don't leave me! 

I'm so afraid! Don't leave me!"

FURNISHED ROOM 

She clasped both her hands tightly about his. "Don't worry, Tom, I'll be right here." Her voice broke, but she was conscious of an incredible glow in her heart. 

At eight she awoke from a light doze and after a moment's watching left him sleeping heavily. His forehead was moist now and she knew the first grip of the fever was broken. As she closed his door carefully behind her she saw Mrs. Tillinghast standing in her open door watching with a cynical smile. She beckoned the landlady to follow her upstairs. "Well!" "How much rent does Tom Smith owe you?"

The landlady opened her eyes and then blurted out savagely, "Three weeks back and—” "Forget the and," interrupted Roseanna wearily. "Here's your twelve dollars and let the boy alone for a while. He's at death's door with 'flu' and it's up to you to help pull him through." "What do you mean, up to me? How dare you?” 

Roseanna grabbled her roughly by the arm. "Now see here, you old she-dragon, this is once I'm going to tell you something. If that boy dies I'll have your damned joint condemned by the board of health. Everyone'll move out on you to begin with. Don't think I'm just bluffing either, because I mean it. Now watch your step," and she almost pushed her out of the room. 

Mrs. Tillinghast ran downstairs with only a single frightened backward glance. There was venom in her soul, but, far more important, there was money in her hand. She clenched it tightly. 

Meanwhile, upstairs, Mrs. Crowley slipped out of her coat and stood looking into the mirror over her bureau. 

She studied her face a moment in silence and then burst into raucous laughter. "Roseanna, all your life you've been a damn fool.

FURNISHED ROOM 

137 

You'll probably die one. You've got the soul of a whore who always wants to support a man, and by Christ if it ain't the only decent thing in your whole make-up. 

You'll never even have the guts to tell the bum you saved his life, sure as Hell. And maybe that's to your credit, even while it makes you that much more the damn fool. If you had a soul you'd sell it to save the man who tried to choke you, and who's treated you like a dog. What's the reason? You look a Hell of a mess now and what for? Maybe you'll get the 'flu' and what for?"

She flung herself on the bed in a storm of tears. "And he's such a lousy bum, too."

CHAPTER THIRTEEN 

WHAT is one canza

AT is there one can say adequately to describe the course of influenza with its false hopes and sudden grim reckonings? Under the most favorable circumstances it is dangerous enough and in Tom's case there were none of the commonest amenities of the sick room. Yet, it might have been worse. Even while he did not know it there could be no doubt that Mrs. Crowley's prompt aid on the first night prevented an attack of pneumonia which must necessarily have been fatal. 

And her talk with Mrs. Tillinghast did keep that worthy in her place even though it could scarcely be expected to have aroused any more positive help. At best Mrs. 

Tillinghast's good qualities were purely negative ones and one only remembered her kindly for the things she did not do. 

About eleven o'clock the morning after that first night there came a light tap on the door. Tom was lying awake in a sort of dazed condition in which everything seemed vague and intangible. There was no real pain, only a discomfort and a general lack of being well which he couldn't and didn't want to localize. He had taken some medicine at ten o'clock when he awoke, and the slight effort of rising and walking the step or two to his wash basin had been crushing. Just before the tap came his mind was wandering about the thought that he should have remembered to bring a glass of water and put it on the bureau within reach for use at eleven; but of course he hadn't done it and now the idea of getting up again was devastating. Dully he hoped he might fall 138

FURNISHED ROOM 

139 asleep and so not know when the hour came but that thought seemed to banish sleep from his eyes completely, leaving only an incomprehensible, unappeasable lassitude. 

At the first tap he wasn't sure if he really heard aright, but the second time it was a real rap, unmistakably clear in its meaning. Tom shouted, "Come in," which is to say he tried to shout and actually uttered a feeble call which could only have been heard by a person intent on hearing. 

Linda swung in gaily crying, "Well, have you lost your voice?" 

Tom held out a hand limply. "How are you, Linda?” "For the love of Mike, what's happened to you? You look like the wrath of God." 

He smiled weakly. "And I feel like it. I'm down with the 'flu.' I was delirious most of last night." 

She placed a hand for a moment on his forehead. "Say, you are sick. Got a doctor?" He nodded. "You ought to be in the hospital." Her tone was serious. "I can't go! I won't go to a hospital!" His voice rose in a kind of wail, and then suddenly gasped out. She quietly patted his shoulder as one might an excited Newfoundland dog. "All right, then you won't go." 

She waited a minute while he quieted down. "Now how about medicine?" She took up the two boxes curiously. "When do you take these?" She rattled them in that peculiarly vague, helpless way in which everyone always does rattle a pill box as if the very sound would give some strong indication of its use and purpose. Then quite suddenly she dropped her vague manner and was opening the boxes when Tom's slow answer reached her. "Now-at eleven o'clock-the brown box." "That's fine. I just came in time, didn't I."

FURNISHED ROOM 

Without further ado she threw off her coat and set to work. First she gave him the pills and then made the bed comfortable. She straightened up the bureau and then washed his face carefully. As she went on Tom couldn't keep in his astonishment. "Why, Linda, I feel better already. You're a real worker." 

She turned and faced him with a broad smile. "You see I trained as a nurse-for a while. A lot of us do until the nice young internes see us. Then the game's soon up. 

Ah well, I suppose they can't very well use the maternity ward for the nurses, can they?" She turned and went on puttering about. "Did you have your baby, Linda?" Tom asked. "Baby, say I think you're the baby. Didn't I tell you 

I trained as a nurse? I hope I learned something."

Tom lay back with closed eyes trying to imagine 

Linda all dressed in starched clothes learning to be a nurse and resisting the amorous advances of the young internes, not resisting very successfully, of course. How the hard inelastic routine must have irked her, yet something of it had gotten into her blood as her energetic movements now showed. The whole thing led one to envisage an entirely new Linda. One was so apt to fall into the fallacy of accepting such women as if they were fated to be of the streets and had not arrived there by strangely different courses and were only alike in the grotesque externals of their trade. 

It was strange to be thinking about Linda seriously.

He was sure he had never thought about her or her problems before. It is strange how long we can continue to see a person in a particular way and not visualize him in the ordinary pursuits of life. One sees a waiter as a strange being in black who waits upon tables and appears bringing food from some realm of mystery beyond a pair of swinging doors. We never ask him, "How is

FURNISHED ROOM 

141 your wife," or encourage him to tell about the new baby while waiting for the steak to broil. And it isn't so much that we never do it as that it never occurs to us to do it. 

We forget he is a man. 

And it was so with Linda. Beyond her name and obvious way of life his curiosity had never wandered.

To him she was always simply the girl who had sneaked into his bedroom in the middle of the night and hid under the bed because the cops were after her. As such he had told the story and those who heard had laughed gaily with him. Only Alfred had asked, "Where did she go when she left you?" and he recalled how the question had surprised him. He had answered shortly, "How should I know?" and thought no more about it. Now when she was playing the good Samaritan within reach of his hand, he felt he wanted to know her as something a little better than just "the woman the cops chased under the bed." For instance, what was she doing now to keep her so quiet so long at the bureau behind him. 

He cried, "Linda."

Slowly she came around into his range of vision and a single glance proclaimed the reason for her long silence. She held in both hands the open box containing his other medicines. She held it out toward him and there was a bite in her voice. "I thought you said you had 'flu.'" "I have." In his earnestness he almost sat up. "If you trained as a nurse you can see that." He waved a feeble hand toward the gruesome, yellow-stained box. "I have that, too. But that's unimportant now." He sank back on the pillow.

A strange look came into her eyes as if sight were turned back into herself instead of out into the world. 

The hard cold look died out of them completely and her voice changed. She spoke very softly, more to herself

I never would have dreamed it of you with your high and mighty ways.'

THERE CHAPTER FOURTEEN 

HERE is so little need ever to go into the suffering of an illness like "flu." For days Tom had intermittent spells of delirium, mostly in the evening when Alfred was there to take care of him. Every evening 

Alfred called up the doctor to report his condition in detail and to receive instruction in return. But it seemed there was little enough could be done for the sufferer as the illness had to run its course. 

Roseanna came no more and, in truth, Tom never knew of her night of devotion to him. Now and then 

Linda would bang her way in with a big bag of fruit, but her visits were as irregular as the rest of her life. Indeed she was like a different person at every visit; sometimes gay and bubbling over with good cheer, at other times gloomy and morose, and prone to discuss at great length her immediate entry into a convent. 

As Tom got enough better to lie propped up in bed reading and there was no real pain but only a great lassitude, he began to look forward to her coming because there was no telling in just what mood she would be. But whatever her state of mind, whether she felt like Thaïs before or after her miraculous conversion, she was invariably kindness itself to him and managed to make him much more comfortable.

At first he remonstrated feebly against her ministrations, but she paid no attention and went right on bathing him and fixing things in whatever way she could for his comfort. "Maybe I'll be a nurse again, some day," she laughed as she patted his pillows deftly. 

Then one day when he was able to get some clothes on for the first time and was sitting up on the little 145

FURNISHED ROOM 

straight chair at the window, Anne came. He called "Come right on in" gaily because he had rather been expecting Linda, who had not been around for several days. When he saw who it was he tried to rise, but sank back on the chair limply.

Anne's costume was an ultra-smart tailored affair trimmed with fur. She had been walking in the open air, her cheeks were glowing with color, and her eyes sparkling. To Tom she seemed like a visitor from another world. The thought flashed through his mind again as it did every time he saw her. She was certainly like no one he had ever known, like no one he had ever seen. She held out her hand. Its touch was cool and invigorating. "You see, if you won't answer my letters or my telephone calls I have to come after you.' "Anne, you shouldn't." 

She looked around quickly and then lowered her eyes with a smile. "May I sit down," she began, then finished before he could interrupt, "on the bed." She seated herself and swung back the furs swathing her throat. "Please!"

The single word dropped from his lips after a moment's bewildered hesitation. What was the use of pretence of social graces here in such surroundings. He had always meant that Anne should never see this place but that resolve was of less than no importance now. He vaguely remembered he had received her two or three letters and had put them aside to open later, being too weary for them at the time. He had been called to the telephone too, but had fairly shouted at Mrs. Tillinghast, "I can't go to the phone. Say I'm out." He hadn't even thought or cared who it might be. It hadn't seemed of any great importance when compared with his misery.

One could see patches of clean blue sky, but no sun entered the room at this season of the year. No caressing golden finger ever touched the gloomy red wall-paper even in mockery. Tom climbed back into bed where he lay breathing heavily. He was almost sorry Anne had come. He had been in a kind of half-pleasant stupor, probably caused, at least in part, by the medicines he was taking. He wanted just to sit or lie dully, and the nearer his mind was to a blank the easier it all was. Now if Linda had come he would have laughed at her, outwardly or inwardly depending upon the mood she was in, and of course she would have managed to make him a little more comfortable. She always did. It was her knack. 

But Anne went so much deeper and stirred up all the memories he wanted to banish. She disturbed him even as her mere presence soothed him, she stimulated him even as she mocked him with her farness, mental, physical, emotional. Lately he had told Linda he now knew exactly what he wanted out of life. Just to be well. Now after Anne had been with him for a few minutes he realized how false that glib statement was. He wanted more, far more. Even if he didn't know exactly what,

FURNISHED ROOM 

he knew it was far more than merely to be well. He had been well before; he had had triumphant health; he had laughed at all sickness; and yet he had had only the husks of happiness. Anne made him feel that way. Just being intellectually superior to your surroundings was not enough any more, was not going to be enough in the future, if there ever was any future. He closed his eyes. 

Anne returned followed by a waiter carrying a heavily laden tray. With ill-concealed scorn he placed it on the table and turned to go. Mohammed's example may be followed in so many things even as he intended it should be."

Under Anne's quiet insistence Tom managed to make a good meal and she paid him the true compliment any sick person likes so much of eating right along with him. For a while she stayed sitting on the bed beside him and talking of light, easy things. She told him amusing anecdotes of her musical life in Paris and then slipped gaily to the plays she had seen since they met.

seemed to Tom afterwards as if the loan was the I winch which literally pulled him up on his feet. Then write me down as one 

Who loves a sirloin steak- 

Everyone I showed it to had a good laugh."

The doctor smiled and then explained quietly once more that he must do his best and he promised he would. He walked from the office with a fine buoyant feeling of elation. He had cleaned up his little debt to the doctor. That was something.

But two days later when he returned to the West End Avenue office his treatments were resumed and he realized how far back he had fallen in his race for health. He suffered cruelly, breaking out in an agonized sweat, and when he began bitter complaints the doctor explained that it was only to be expected that the enforced neglect should have developed this aggravated condition. This time there was nothing to laugh about and he finally crept out of the office with the exhausted feeling that perhaps hospitals were good things after all.

157 

Once in the subway he sank into a seat with a groan.

A prim old-maidish woman looked him over from head to foot and then sniffed loudly as she thrust her long nose again between the pages of her magazine. The word "drunk" floated impersonally on the air, snorted up from behind the barricade of the magazine and Tom felt the eyes of two or three others upon him. There were only a few people in this first car which he was always careful to get, but now he wished there were more so there might be someone else to stare at.

He tried staring at a short fat man with a pendulous stomach who sat with knees thrust wide apart so the seat might help support his belly. He remembered a vulgar story about such a man and a mother who came into the car with a little boy. The boy, according to the story, went to the man and said, "My mother says if you move your legs together she can sit down." The man's answer contained the joke and the vulgarity but was funny because of its ludicrously apt repartee. He grinned a little but the man at whom he was staring slept quietly on with his hands on his bulging abdomen and attracted no other stares. They were all directed firmly at himself.

A sense of irritation filled him. He felt he wanted to move across the aisle and sit beside the withered up old maid. He wanted to take her magazine away from her face and say very loudly, "Madam, you are making a terrible mistake. You think I'm drunk, but I'm not. I'm just very sick. I've just come from the doctor and he hurt me terribly and I am suffering right now. I've just gotten over 'flu,' too. I almost died of it. Yes, I knew you would be sorry for me if you really understood. I'm very sick, a very sick man. "It's difficult to explain my trouble to you. Didn't your beaux have it, or father, or brother, or uncle?

FURNISHED ROOM 

I'm sure some of them did or else you come from a very lucky family, and there are not so many families where everyone is lucky. Come, I do want you to understand what I mean. Come on to the front end of the car and 

I'll tell you exactly what I mean. I'm sure it will be interesting to you." 

How perfectly ridiculous to imagine such a scene. Yet somehow the withered old maid had a vinegary look and sneering expression that made him want to cut deep into her musty virginal mind. And the others were probably as badly in need of a good shock. How horrified they would be at the mere mention of his trouble. How they would look in disgust at any flagrant mention of carnal, physical contact and its all too frequent result. Bah!

Yet most of them would go to their favorite lending library and demand the latest "hot" book, and gloat over its lurid salacious pages. Such people sickened him. 

He glanced up and was glad to see the train pulling into soth Street station. He got off quickly. He was sick of the subway. Once out in the open air he stopped watching the scurrying crowds along Broadway and then turned slowly down that street. He would go to see Mrs. Bobbs and change his mind a little. He didn't want to go back to his dingy room. Anywhere else was preferable. And then he might also collect six dollars still owing to him. Now that he didn't need it so much he would probably get it.

It's so funny," cried her playmate clapping her hands. "First she screams at him and calls him names, then he screams at her and calls her names, and then there's a loud smack and a bang. Wait, you'll hear it in a minute." And she ran to the foot of the stairs to listen more intently. Her expression was one of impish delight. "I'm sure it would be much better outside," he began again, but the third little girl now broke in with a singsong voice. "We can't play outside; we have colds." "Of course you have," cried Cynthia scornfully. "You stay up too late. You never go to bed." "We do so! We do so! My mother won't let us stay up after two o'clock. She says we must get our rest.” "That's what I said. That's too late." Cynthia's manner was very scornful. She turned quickly to Tom. "Isn't it, Mr. Smith?" 

He looked for a moment at the little girl who smiled up at him quite sure of his approbation. Her sister, a bare year older, now returned from listening at the foot of the stairs and they caught hands instinctively, as if they were wanting his approval and at the same moment doubtful of obtaining it. Their bodies were gauntly thin and their clothes as insufficient in warmth as in cleanliness. Their hair was exactly alike, a matted, dirty yellow that cried out to be washed and combed and show its soft prettiness. There were smudges of dirt on their cheeks and their pale blue eyes looked tired and heavy.

Cynthia herself sometimes looked tousled, but it was

FURNISHED ROOM 

only a momentary condition while in this case he couldn't help feeling that the smudge on the smaller child's nose had probably been there at least a week. A glance at Cynthia showed her to be clean and neat.

But all three were waiting for an answer and with six earnest eyes upon him it was difficult to know just what to say. Finally: "I am sure, being good little girls, you all do exactly as your mother wishes."

It was a stupid reply and, indeed, had nothing to do with the question, but it seemed to satisfy the children with its broad compliment and the tenseness fell away. The smallest child now took his hand coyly. "Will you play house with us?" "Why of course not!" began Cynthia pedantically.

He cut her short with a squeeze of the hand, then faced the little one gravely. "I'm sure I should love to play but today I'm calling on Cynthia's mother and I haven't time. We'll play some other time." He patted her on the head.

They waved grimy little hands at him as he rode up in the lift with Cynthia who, head in air, was quite scornful of such youthful triflings. Just as they got out of the elevator she couldn't hold in any more. "They are such little babies. I don't see why I play with them." She tossed her auburn curls disdainfully. "I think they are very nice little girls.” "Do you really?" She stopped short in the hall. "I really do." He made it very positive.

For a moment she hesitated, shifting uneasily from one foot to the other, then cried suddenly, "Then I'll go back and play with them," and she ran wildly down the stairs shouting, "Lillian, Jean, I'm coming."

He stood looking after her a moment before ringing Mrs. Bobbs' bell. There was only a strange noise from within, but it indicated clearly enough that someone was

FURNISHED ROOM 

161 in, so he rang again more vigorously. A choked voice cried "Come in," and he swung open the door upon a desolate scene. His eyes opened wide in astonishment.

Mrs. Bobbs sat in her big chair with a Turkish towel over her head sobbing convulsively. Her head and shoulders were invisible but the quivering of her ankles showed the extent of her grief. Indeed, she shook all over in a way that would have been alarming had he not become accustomed to her marvellous possibilities in that way. "Oh, oh, oh," she sobbed wildly. "I wish I was dead! 

I wish I was dead!"

Tom stood perplexed, not knowing just what to do. 

When a lady wishes she were dead in a loud sobbing voice it is very evident she is in no humor to entertain company. But the door had swung to behind him as he stood in open-mouthed astonishment and to retreat was now as difficult as to remain. He remembered in a flash he always had difficulty opening that door even under the best of circumstances, and it was much to be doubted if his skill would improve under present conditions. He remained standing helplessly while the moans and sobs waxed louder and louder. He must do something as he had rung the bell twice and had been told to come in, so for all her seeming obliviousness she must know that someone was in the room. 

At last in desperation he called, "Mrs. Bobbs," very softly and then louder and louder as he began to fear he would never penetrate the mounting barrage of wailing sobs. Then, unexpectedly they ceased, and she cried still behind the towel, "Get out of here, you big beast! I hate you! I hate you! Get out! Get out! Get out!" 

If he had been astonished before he was dumbfounded now. He had always supposed Mrs. Bobbs rather liked him, but while her present reception of him was bewil-

FURNISHED ROOM 

dering, at the same time its meaning was very plain. He fumbled with the various locks while the wails and sobs now fairly rang out in the little room. He tried the handle but had only succeeded in locking himself more hopelessly in. At the sound of the rattle of the knob the voice behind him began again. "That's right, you big bum. Sneak off like the coward you are. You can't face me. And after all I've done for you. Oh! oh! oh! Your own mother couldn't have done more. I hate you. Go away! Go away!"

In desperation he walked up to Mrs. Bobbs and put his hand on her shoulder softly. She stopped crying almost at once and whimpered, "Oh, I knew you couldn't leave me like that. You dear boy, don't look at me. I know I'm a sight." She whisked off the towel about her head.

For a second they stood looking at each other in silence. Tom felt how glorious it would be if rooms were only equipped like stages and he could disappear as startlingly as Bela Lugosi did in Dracula. He opened his mouth to speak, to explain, to apologize, but no words came out.

But his embarrassment had made no allowances for the lady's theatrical powers of recuperation. With a sudden queenly gesture Mrs. Bobbs flung the towel into the bedroom and said gaily, "Hello, Mr. Smith. How long have you been here?" "Just a minute." He managed to squeeze out the words.

She reached for a comb on the table beside her and began to comb out her violently golden hair, patting in pins and sweeping it into place with superb nonchalance as if she were surrounded by mirrors rather than without the assistance of any. "Was I emoting all over the place?" She laughed heartily.

She nodded portentously. "I tried to get it away from him but he pushed me down on the couch. He was clever enough for that.”

She sighed deeply, then went on with satisfaction. "Oh it was all very dramatic, just like a play. I wish you could have been here to see it. You would have enjoyed it. Finally he flung me on the couch and banged out. I started to cry where I lay until I remembered I was on the new pillow I had just finished making. I almost ruined it before I realized." 

He clucked his sympathy and glancing about spied the pillow in question. There could be no doubt about its violent pink newness. It was one of those atrocities that are made by loving hands and are beyond the wildest dreams of the most daring commercial manufacturer. "It would be a shame to have ruined it," he commented gently with appropriate pathos in his voice. 

She looked immensely pleased. "Wouldn't it?" "And so he didn't come back?" "No, worse luck, because I thought of a few things to call him I hadn't thought of before. For one instant he thought he couldn't keep down the gurgling laughter which threatened to choke him at the thought of Mrs. Bobbs in his room. Even were she able to get inside the door, which was doubtful enough, she would have stuck between the bed and the side wall.

Mrs. Bobbs looked off into space as if looking back into another world when things were so very different. 

Then she spoke very softly, "Maybe I could get back on the stage again." Her eyes closed lightly as if to shut in the delicious remembrances of days gone by. "What did you do on the stage?" he asked. "I was very good in my day, Mr. Smith. I was a tightrope walker." She simpered professionally. 

For one instant Tom felt he must shout with joy. 

What a glorious picture to imagine this colossal, almost bed-ridden woman of about four hundred pounds dancing leaf-like on a thin, taut wire. He needed all the horrible memories he could muster at short notice to keep his laughter under control, and he was afraid his face

proclaimed his inward struggle. But the woman was oblivious to all but her glorious memories. She ran on romantically, "And I used to wear a little lace dress and tights. You could have carried my whole costume in a pocket. I was called Sybilla the Elf."

It was too much. With a swift gesture he knocked his hat under the table and dove out of sight after it, cramming his handkerchief into his mouth. Sybilla the Elf was too much at last. He stayed under the table so long she called out, "What's the matter, did you lose something?" 

He came up with a scarlet face. "My hat fell under the table." He glanced about till he saw the clock. "Oh

He was laughing, mother, as if he would break in two." "I don't remember anything funny, Cynthia. You must be mistaken. I only told him about Siegfried." Her face wrinkled up and a tear glistened in her eye. “Siegfried's gone and will never come back." 

Cynthia patted her mother's fat cheek solicitously. "Don't cry, mother darling, maybe he'll come back."

Her mother shook her head in a vague way which might mean either a positive or a negative, but the child seemed to find it quite satisfactory. Then Mrs. Bobbs' eyes closed slowly and with parted smiling lips she slept softly. For a few minutes Cynthia sat studying her with loving eyes, then tiptoed out of the room, closing the door very softly after her. Maybe she'd be able to hear the funny story later. Mr. Smith had been laughing and he had looked so sad when he arrived. People were always nicer when they laughed.

CHAPTER SIXTEEN OWARD the end of January Tom got a job. Silly 

TOWARDS he rejoiced in it as a sign of better things to come, since his mind was always on the lookout for any change in his fortunes. The hundred dollars loaned him by Anne's father had carried him along well and he knew a security that was infinitely restful, but finally it was gone. On two occasions Mr. Dale drew him aside when he was at their house and insisted on his accepting more, but Tom was firm in his refusal and he doggedly continued his search for employment of some kind, any kind. There was nothing for Mr. Dale to do, but accept his decision and Tom could not help feeling he respected him the more for it. 

Yet the hundred dollars did have a lasting effect. He had his clothes mended as well as possible and pressed. 

He bought shoes and a hat. The hat was always a gay reminder of Mrs. Bobbs and Sybilla the Elf because he had only had it a day or so on that occasion when it had given him an excuse to crawl under the table and hide his ill-controlled laughter. He also got underwear, socks, handkerchiefs, and even a light overcoat at a pawnshop. 

For the first time in years he began to taste the joys of looking at least neat and clean.

The virus entered his soul. He was impressed with his own ability to do such common things as pay rent when due, have his shoes shined, get his hair cut, all the ordinary things of life which meant nothing to most other men, but were strange enough to him. And as he began to feel more and more the need for the common 170

FURNISHED ROOM 

171 amenities of life, so he realized more and more the necessity of getting them. He was losing his power to scorn what he lacked. 

And then he got his job. He was sitting in Battery

Park at noon one day, when a chap came along whom he had known a year or so before quite casually. Now 

Tim Daly welcomed him as an old friend. After a minute or two they were talking about hard times and living conditions. Tom glanced at the office coat visible under Tim's overcoat which he had flung open to do honor to the bright sunshine and clean generous air. 

Tim caught his glance, hesitated a moment, and then- "Say, did you say you wanted any kind of a job, do anything?" 

Tom nodded vigorously. "I certainly did.' "Then come on back to the office with me. Maybe I can get you taken on as a runner." He got up briskly. "A runner?" Tom queried dubiously. "That's what they call 'em but it doesn't mean anything. You see I'm in a broker's office and every office has to have what they call 'runners' to deliver stock and collect checks and things like that. It's a regular kids' job. I know because I was one for the longest year of my life, but if you want anything," he waved a hand in a mock Jewish gesture, "well, it's something. It pays almost nothing, though," he added in explanation. 

But Tom had heard enough. He accepted eagerly and went back to the office of Faulkner Brothers with Tim.

It was on the tenth floor of a dingy building on Broadway whose side windows looked out on a church- Trinity, with its graveyard-and therefore was counted one of the finest locations of the Wall Street district. From the front windows one could almost look down into Wall Street and therefore it didn't matter that the signal bells on the elevators wouldn't work, ac-

FURNISHED ROOM 

and that one had to shout at the operators as the cars flew past the old-fashioned grill-work. As they rode up 

Tim told him an amusing story about a man who had a bad cold and couldn't speak above a whisper. So all he could do was to ring the bell. which didn't ring. 

Finally he stood so long waiting hopelessly and trying vainly to make himself heard that one of the special police of the building went up and arrested him for loitering.

Evidently it was a standard story, for the elevator man held the car at the tenth floor to hear the end of it and then everyone guffawed loudly. Tim was well pleased with himself. Once inside the office he introduced Tom to a Mr. Dennison who was very short and sleek with a mouse-like manner. He had an overly large head and wore a large grey hat with a tiny red feather in the side. The hat gave the effect of being glued on his head. He listened while Tim explained in a peculiar way. "Tom's a good guy, Mr. Dennison, but he hasn't any reference or anything, 'cause he ain't never worked much. He's one of these artistic intellectual guys what can't make a living at it, ain't you, Tom?" Tom was forced to agree and as Dennison looked up he thought he caught a twinkle in his eye. "I thought" (with Tim 

Daly the word became almost taught) "that you could use him as a runner maybe. He's as slow as molasses in a snowstorm but most of 'em is worse, says I.” 

He grinned, appreciative of his own humor. Mr. Dennison smiled. "All right, Tim, that was a noble recommendation. As to runners, we haven't had a good one since you went into the office. I'll see what I can do for your friend."

Tim waved gaily and went into the office, well satis-

FURNISHED ROOM 

173 fied with having "done a pal a good turn," as he expressed it. 

Mr. Dennison turned to Tom. "Do you really need a job badly? This is only a boy's job or," he added softly, "for some old man. You aren't either, are you?" "Hardly; but I do need something, and I'll do my best at whatever I undertake."

The man nodded. "Somehow I think you will. It's only fourteen dollars a week, you know." "That's a great deal more than nothing." "Very well, then, let's try it. Do you want to start right away, even if it is Friday, or else wait until Monday morning to begin the week fresh?" He smiled a little at Tom's vigorous answer. "All right, then. There's no time like the present."

They passed into a dark gloomy room that seemed scarcely more than a big closet. At one end was a cage and along the other side were ground-glass windows, through which a faint grey glimmer trickled into the place from the office on which these dim orifices opened. 

Over the windows were signs reading, "Three Ways," "Deliveries," "Checks," "Demands," etc. Beneath the windows ran a long bench on which sat two old men and seven or eight young ones. The old men were smoking battered pipes and the young ones cigarettes. A big bluish bulb cast that ghastly glare over the place which is supposed to equal, if not improve upon daylight. 

There were no windows into the open air and the place was so stuffy after the bright clean air outside that he gasped a little for breath. The place positively reeked of tobacco. Mr. Dennison smiled. "It is pretty bad until you get used to it, but I'll see you get out most of the time today so you can get

FURNISHED ROOM 

the swing of things and not be cooped up here on a beautiful day like this. I'm in all the time." He indicated the cage and at the same time called out of it a tall young man with a mass of bright red hair whom he introduced as John Honan. "He'll explain things,” he said. Then he walked out again.

The two young men shook hands cordially. Tom felt immediately that he was going to like John Honan with his flaming red hair and wide smile. For the love of holy Saint Patrick what do you want to come to work in this hole for. If I had your size I'd be on the cops.' 

He spoke in a quaint high Irish voice. They sat down on a vacant end of the bench but in so small a place every word was overheard. One of the old men pulled his pipe from his mouth and drawled in a rich brogue, "There ye go, John Honan, pizening the young man's mind before he's fair got his feet inside of the door. 

Sure there's worse places than this to work in. Where did you work last, John?"

A chuckle greeted the sally but the red-headed chap was no whit disturbed. "Just for that, O'Halloran, it'll be up to you to break the man in and show him how to appreciate the place. Mr. Smith, may I present Seamus

O'Halloran, the pride of County Kildaire, or Kilkenny, or Killarney, or some of those 'killing' counties, no one knows which."

There was a general laugh but the old Irishman shook hands with dignity. Then he continued, "And look at the head of hair of him as jokes at the Irish. Now I ask you, Mr. Smith, did you ever hear of a name like

During the general laugh they went out and stood at the bank of elevators. The old Irishman shouted "Down" in a voice of thunder until a car finally stopped. Once out in the street Tom inquired, “What was the last joke about 60 Beaver Street?” "It's just the furthest place we have to go and then it isn't far unless you're hurrying to get home at night.

Sure it's better to be out in the fresh air than in that pig-pen."

Tom breathed deeply as they walked along. Certainly his present companion did not take the word "runner" any more seriously than Tim had done. Meanwhile 

O'Halloran kept chuckling to himself, "So Honan is a 

Chinee name. That'll keep him quiet for a while. I'd hate to be named after a city in China."

The rest of the day Tom went around with O'Halloran on various errands while the old Irishman explained the various things to be done in his quaint old way, with many fantastic circumlocutions. They delivered stocks and collected checks for them, the larger of which had to be certified. "Sure, since the crash in the market they want nearly every check to be certified. 

They don't trust nobody at all. Some of the firms that failed were thought to be as good as banks. Faith ye can't tell anything about anything these days.” He shook his head dolefully.

China, John? or maybe your mother was Irish and so ye came honestly by your map." Once inspiration rose to "And did you ever try taking in laundry, John," but

It was all rather amusing and at the end of the day Tom rode home on the Sixth Avenue elevated with a feeling of elation in spite of his tiredness. As he had come out into the hall Mr. Dennison had stopped to talk with him. "Well, how do you like it?" "I rather think I do. I haven't had a regular job in so long it's all fascinatingly new to me.' 

"Then you'll be here tomorrow morning?" he asked rather as if surprised at such a decision. "Certainly. That is if I'm wanted."

The little man rested a hand reassuringly on his arm. "As long as this poor job is any good to you you're welcome to it. I saw this afternoon you're going to be a good man for us to have. We have to depend so much on scatter-brained kids who don't care what happens as long as they get home early. O'Halloran says you're a fine man." He smiled a little. "He even went on to say that you were a gentleman and what was the world coming to when a gentleman had to take a job like this. 

You got a good reputation in a few hours.'

"Fine and dandy, Tim," he had replied heartily and then grinned quietly to himself. They had never in their lives called each other by their first names and it

FURNISHED ROOM 

177 was clear Tim was going out of his way to show they were close friends. And it was very obvious that Tim himself was very popular with everyone. As Tom remembered back it had always been clear Tim was the proverbial "good skate who'd give you his shirt.”

Now, riding home on the "L," he was conscious of having had a job to do and having done it well. He was tired, very tired; but it was a kind of healthy, end-ofthe-day tiredness which differed from his usual sick exhaustion. Even supposing the difference to be largely mental it was none the less poignant on that account. 

His good humor was still upon him when he reached his room just in time to see Mrs. Tillinghast coming out. 

She smiled her acidulous smile, but he no longer resented it since he knew now it was the only one she had. 

Since he had cleared up his arrears of rent and was now paying regularly in advance she had become almost cordial in her manner. "I was just leaving a telephone message in your room from Mr. Murray. He says he will be here at eight, unless you are going out. In which case telephone him." "Thank you, Mrs. Tillinghast, I'll be here." "And there's a letter and a note left by hand." He pulled off his clothes, shaved carefully, and then went up the stairs to the bathroom where he lay soaking for a long time in a tub of hot water. 187 "And suppose I say you are deserving of all the good in the world?" She was very earnest.

Anne glanced up on the platform and could make out a shadowy figure but couldn't be sure it was he. The train rumbled in, stood panting for a moment, and then pulled out, chugging heavily. A strange smile passed over the girl's face. "Yes, mother, very happy. Goodnight."

Once in her room Anne flung up her window and leaned out into the night. A few snowflakes were falling fitfully and the stars were being blotted out by a heavy cloud. The spell of clear weather had broken finally. Above her she could hear Nora humming: 

Erin, the tear and the smile in thine eye 

Melts like the rainbow that fades in the sky.

She closed the window very softly.

CHAPTER SEVENTEEN 

W morning after a 

EN Tom got up about seven o'clock Monday morning after a night of restless tossing he was unashamedly glad that he had work to do which must of necessity make him think less about himself. It would have been too terrible had he been condemned to stay in his room and continue to live over the torments of the night. For it was certainly the worst night he had ever known. His body was all hot as if with high fever and all night long he tumbled and tossed about the bed in a succession of loathsome orgiastic nightmares. His dreams were so vivid and so gruesomely passionate that he shuddered at the memory of them. Now it was as if his limbs were weighted down with lead and soiled with a thousand uncleannesses. He fairly dragged himself up to the bathroom and scrubbed himself thoroughly with strong soap wishing he could so wash away even memory. 

Then he shaved, put on fresh clothes and went in search of breakfast. The long spell of crisp sunny weather was over and the city had wrapped itself in a cold grey fog. He shivered a little in his thin overcoat but at the same instant he was grateful for even such a garment. And he knew his chill was as much mental as physical. He went into a restaurant on the Sixth Avenue corner and ate two plates of very hot oatmeal rapidly. He felt that several cups of strong black coffee would help him most, but that was one of the things most strictly forbidden to him. It seemed at the moment 189

FURNISHED ROOM 

as if everything he wanted most in life were forbidden to him. 

Down in the runners' room at the broker's office he was greeted with full cordiality. Everyone there always seemed in a radiant good humor and it was one of the things which pleased him most. This morning they were teasing one of the older men whom everyone called Mike. He was a very obvious butt for cheap witticisms, but he seemed to enjoy it rather than show any resentment. In every group there is usually at least one person who is quite willing to be ludicrous in order to remain the center of conversation. The childishness of the joking, its very immaturity, helped to take his mind off the cruel remembrances of the night before.

It had been such a fine day, too, with hope springing round him on every side and everyone kind and interested in him. Mr. Dale's offer undoubtedly meant the biggest thing that had come his way in years and probably a real solution of his difficulties. And then in a single wave of passion he had thrown it all away. He had always thought Anne cared a little for him in her impulsive youthful way and of late he had come to believe he had roused her deeper feelings. There was something in the way she looked at him and in the tone of her voice when she spoke which proclaimed her interest in him and in his well-being. He had known, too, that in her turn she had come to occupy more and more of his thoughts until she stood between him and everything he tried to do or think like a delicate fragrant shadow. It had become terribly important just to be with her and, when he was away, to look forward to the next time he would be with her.

That had come to worry him because it made him think of things which he had always supposed were not for him and could not be for him. He had always ridi-

FURNISHED ROOM 

191 culed all family life and the commonplace tendernesses between two happily married people. He had always laughed at anything but the whirlwind of a tremendous passion that lifted you to the very mountain peaks of ecstasy and as inevitably flung you down breathless and spent, at the end. His jokes about quiet simple domesticity had always made everyone laugh.

Yet of late he had been falling into the way of simplicity at the Dales' house and when he told Mr. Dale that the happiest days he knew were spent there, he meant every word of it. He had taken to dreaming of himself and Anne together in the gay impossible way that young men will and he had found the dreams temptingly sweet to linger upon. 

Now they were gone. His mad kisses in the car had certainly driven her away from him spiritually even while she had risen to the occasion nobly and had tried to forgive him before he broke away and ran for the train. His dreams of her had always been quiet and peaceful and she had always been his wife in them rather than his object of passionate desire. His dream had been to love her quietly and tenderly rather than passionately; he had dreamed of peace with her rather than ecstasy. 

His lust had killed all that. In his horrible diseased condition he had lusted after the only lovely pure girl he had known, or at least after the only one he had ever known who had impressed these beautiful qualities upon him. He, who felt guilty of soiling something virginal when he so much as shook hands with her, had actually kissed her and crushed her body to his exactly as he might some woman picked up for a few hours' amusement. Not since the never-to-be-forgotten night with Roseanna had he flamed up so madly. He had hoped that devastating side of his nature was dead; dead of the

FURNISHED ROOM 

very consequences of its own being, something which the fire of disease had burned away, something never to lift its flaming head again.

Alas for such hopes. Last night his dreams had been too revolting to recall. Even in his dreams he had polluted that which he held sacred, which he must hold sacred even had no social stigma attached to him. After such a night he could never face her again. He had crushed the fine flower of their intimacy and nothing could be gained by trying pathetically to gather up the petals to weep over them. That part of his life was closed. She might forgive his kiss but he could not forgive or forget his dreams. 

Just before three o'clock he wrote a little note to

Mr. Dale and asked one of the boys who was going to the bank to deliver it. It said simply: 

Dear Mr. Dale: 

I am sorry I won't be able to come see you today as you so kindly asked me to do. After thinking it over, I find I would not be justified in accepting any further kindnesses from you. I am too much in your debt already. Sincerely, Thomas Smith

The boy promised to deliver it in person and then wandered out whistling. Tom sat back on the bench gloomily. It was all very well to do what you were morally obligated to do, but sometimes it was very hard. 

Conversation buzzed about him unheeded.

It was a few minutes before eight that night when Tom was ushered into the doctor's office. "I'm afraid I'm rather early," he explained. "That's quite all right." The doctor smiled grimly. Tom explained fully while the doctor listened with growing irritation. Finally he burst out, "You'll have to give up that job right away. It's absolutely criminal for you to be walking about so much even if you don't run, as you say you don't. I know how difficult things are for you and if you can get some position where you sit down in comfort I suppose you must take it. But you can't go on with this other kind of work." "And suppose I have to?" Tom demanded.

For a moment the doctor was silent, then he spread

FURNISHED ROOM 

197 his hands deprecatingly. "In that case if you must you must, and take the consequences."

A little later, Tom told him simply about his horrible dreams the night before. The doctor listened carefully, then asked, "Is that the first time it has happened like that?" "Yes. I have been bothered, of course, as every young man is, but I have never known anything so terrible as that. It is awful to remember." He shivered. "I'm afraid you will be bothered some at this stage of the treatment. But didn't you have some particular excitement to bring it on?"

In a burst of confidence Tom explained his growing love for Anne and then the scene at the station. The medical man listened patiently. Finally Tom blurted out, "And it's too horrible that I should fall in love at last when I am in this condition. I always used to ridicule people who loved each other simply and quietly and wanted a little home together. Now I'm getting paid back for my jokes." "My boy, you must not get bitter over the way life runs. You still insist on viewing your experience as something unique. It isn't that at all. So many men only realize the value of a home life when they get in your condition. It is almost part of the ailment. You learn to appreciate women in a newer, finer way; you learn through suffering. I mean just that. It happens so often and it is for the best. I believe in the change of ideas which so aften comes. But remember there must be no sex excitement as that means trouble, serious trouble. Your affection must be deep-and right."

For the next few minutes he went on talking and explaining while Tom listened numbly. Now that he had lost Anne it seemed so futile to talk about his feelings

FURNISHED ROOM 

toward her. But he tried to crush down his gloomy thoughts and asked, "And you really mean it when you say that even in my condition I can think of getting married?" His earnestness was almost grim. "Certainly." The doctor smiled. "I say you can get married. You can think of it and plan for it if you please. If all goes well one day I can take you by the hand and say, 'Now get married and God bless you.'

I've told you before that with proper attention and care the cure is absolute. I would give you permission to marry my own sister or daughter if I had one. Does that finally set your doubts at rest? You should remember we have talked these things over before and while I am always willing to set your mind at rest, you should remember better than you do." "I'm sorry, doctor. I'll try not to forget this time."

But there were moments all during the week when he was apt to forget anything except his condition. 

There was no doubt but the standing and walking were exhausting him and he quickly learned to take advantage of every moment he might sit and rest. On Friday he felt in an almost fainting condition and Mr. Dennison noticed his drawn face and haggard eyes. But when questioned he laughed it off as just feeling a little "under the weather." 

Dennison smiled. "Anyway, I think I'll excuse you for the rest of the day. You've been very faithful and worked hard. I'll see you tomorrow morning."

Thankfully he walked slowly out of the building. It was another bright sunny day with a strange touch of warmth in the air such as New York sometimes experiences in mid-winter. Tom strolled into Trinity churchyard to sit on one of the benches in the sun. People were there taking advantage of the bright warmth and only

FURNISHED ROOM 

199 at the far end was there a bench with room for him. A girl was sitting there looking across Broadway with the sun warming her back. Tom walked slowly up with half-shut eyes until he was almost up to the bench. 

Then he started forward with a little cry. "Anne."

She turned to face him and her eyes blinked into the glare. For a moment which seemed to him hours of agony she didn't know him; then she held out her hand quickly. "Why, Tom, this is a surprise. Come around here where I can see you."

He stepped over the bench and sat down beside her. 

There was a strange tightness in his throat and a silly jingle kept running in his head about little Miss Muffett. 

Two lines hummed in his ears: 

Along came a spider 

And sat down beside her ... 

As the silence continued she laughed shortly. "You look very glum. What are you thinking about?"

Moved by impulse he told her and quoted the childish rhyme. She laughed again, more easily this time. "What a silly goop you are. And also, you see Miss 

Muffett is not frightened away."

He smiled deprecatingly but continued his silence. 

Finally she sighed deeply. "Tom, why are you so. Now, you won't say anything or make any conversation so you are forcing me to ask you questions. Do you want me to do that?" "I don't know. I don't know what to say." "Very well, then. Where were you last Monday between three and three-thirty in the afternoon?" "Working." "Yes, yes," she cried impatiently, "but that really

FURNISHED ROOM 

doesn't answer the question at all. Why didn't you come to father's office? I was there myself," she added softly, "waiting for you." "I was afraid you would be." "Afraid I would be," she gasped a little uncertainly. 

Then after a moment she smiled indulgently. "So that's the explanation. You thought I'd run tell papa that you kissed me and that he'd be waiting to accuse you of assault and battery or something equally terrible. So you were afraid?" "That isn't fair, Anne," he cried. "Anyhow I don't know. Maybe I was afraid. I don't know. You must see 

I can't explain." There was a wild appeal in his voice. 

She changed the subject abruptly. "You look very bad today. Are you working too hard? Or is it the touch of spring in the air?” 

He shrugged his shoulders. "I guess I'm not used to work, that's all. It isn't anything important. I suppose 

I'm just tired. I've been let off for the rest of the day." 

She rose quietly. "Then you can go with me now to see father. I was down here to run in and see him. He'll be glad to see you too." 

He remained seated on the bench. "I can't talk to him, Anne. I can't talk to anyone. Don't you see that." "I almost believe you," she answered grimly. "You are acting in a way I certainly can't understand. Are you by chance trying to be deliberately rude to me?" "Oh God, Anne, don't you know better than that? 

Have I made myself that much misunderstood?" "Almost, Tom. I'm trying to understand better and you won't help me. You won't come to see father now. 

Will you come out on Sunday?” "I can't, Anne, I have an engagement." "Will you have dinner with me tonight and we'll go to a concert or a play?"

FURNISHED ROOM 

201 "Please don't ask me, Anne. I'm so terribly tired I can't, I really can't." He looked at her piteously. "All right." She held out her hand. "Goodbye, Tom."

He caught it convulsively in both of his. "Please don't say it like that. It doesn't sound right.” "Very well, then. But you must acknowledge you are hard to please today."

She turned and walked away slowly. He stood watching her as she passed out through the open gates and started down Wall Street. He stood numbly realizing he had played the part of the perfect fool and with no excuse that anyone but himself could possibly see. It was not clear even to himself why he had behaved so stupidly. And then at the end to be so terribly caught up. Why had he not broken his Sunday appointment with Sylvia when it was so much more important that he be with Anne.

As for the other invitation, it was true that he was ghastly tired, but even so he could have forced himself, and he would have enjoyed a quiet dinner and a beautiful evening of music. Instead he had merely sat back refusing and refusing with curt stupidity. Was it possible the trouble was beginning to affect his brain in some way? With a shudder he pushed the thought from him. That was too horrible altogether, and that way lay madness indeed. It was nothing so bad as that and therefore it was the more inexcusable. He was simply an idiot.

With weak steps he crossed the churchyard and passed through the little gate to Trinity Place. There was nothing to do now but go home and lie down. In sleep one might forget stupidity. But the memory of Anne's face as she walked away tortured him. She had walked away so slowly it would have been possible to have run after and to have made some amends for his crudeness.

FURNISHED ROOM 

Or he might have said "I want to tell you what is the matter. I want to explain everything.' And once he had told her it would be ended one way or the other. 

It was a way out.

He pushed away the thought with an actual physical gesture. He must not let that solution of the problem tempt him even for a moment. That certainly was not fair to any woman of refinement much less a delicateminded girl like Anne. Even if in her ignorance of the true meaning of social disease she thrust it aside and said, "We'll forget about it," could she or anyone else so placed really do so. He didn't dare take such a chance with his happiness. While that secret remained a secret there was always hope. He dared not kill hope.

Gently, with trembling fingers, he unfastened her garment and then kissed her firm white breasts while his hands explored her velvet flesh. First gently, then with full flaming passion, his burning lips caressed her body, while she still clung to him murmuring, "Tom, oh, my Tom," in faint gasps.

FURNISHED ROOM 

211 forearms, her torso gleaming white where the garment fell back. She opened her eyes to look at him, then, "Tom-take me,-show me-love."

He walked across the room lightly and into the delicately scented bedroom he had never seen. As he carried her, a golden slipper fell from her naked foot with a light plop on the heavy rug. Its golden color lay on the dark background like a splash of imprisoned sunlight.

Darkness had come before Tom walked out into the street again. A thin cold rain was falling drearily. It bore with it a wet blanket of fog and seemed on the point of turning into snow momentarily. The street was deserted save for a single taxi which slogged through the wet dreariness as if despairing of ever attracting a profitable fare. Tom turned up his coat collar, pulled down the brim of his hat to protect his face from the slanting rain, and trudged along toward the park. It seemed he could just drag one foot after the other, but he knew that it was spiritual rather than physical weakness which held him in its grip. Once inside the park he sank down on the first bench he came to and buried his face in his hands.

It was such a little while ago that he had wakened with a start to find himself in Sylvia's bed, with the girl trustfully sleeping in the circle of his arms. Perhaps the faint swish of the rain against the window pane had wakened him, he couldn't be sure, but the sight of its grey coldness did clear his head in an instant, and make him realize clearly what he had done.

Or perhaps it only made him realize clearly that he must get out of the perfumed, silken room if he wanted to think clearly. Gently he withdrew his body from her clinging embrace; carefully he laid her head back on

FURNISHED ROOM 

the pillow. As he lifted her from his arm the tendrils of dark hair seemed to cling to his naked shoulders as if they too had rested happily there. Then lightly_he stepped from the bed and bending over, covered her tenderly. Her eyes opened for a second, while her lips murmured his name, then closed voluptuously.

He waited for a moment until her breathing was regular again, then began to climb into his clothes as quickly as the necessity for absolute silence permitted. 

A great bowl of roses on the dressing table seemed to weight down the air with their fragrance and drug his mind so he couldn't think clearly. Outside the rain splashed down drearily, but out there was clearheadedness and a chance to think. 

Carefully he sneaked into the living room, closing the door quietly behind him. Out there everything was a little saner. The dishes were still on the table, as if patiently waiting to be taken away. The piece of music they had started but never finished stood invitingly on the piano. He crossed over to glance at it. It was a ballad called "If Love Were Mine." How like Sylvia to have wanted him to sing that. She had probably bought it for the occasion. He had never sung it but he remembered having heard Mario Chamlee sing it years ago. The end still was clear where he sang triumphantly, "Now love is mine," and rose to the high note on the word love with a swooping glissando. Chamlee always did that and it tortured every singer's soul, even though his audiences applauded deliriously. At any rate it was a ringing virile song.

He rubbed his eyes and put on his overcoat and hat. He must get out quickly before Sylvia awoke. For one instant he stooped to pick up a gold slipper and put it on the big couch. Then he crossed the room, went through the little hall, and let himself out of the apart-

FURNISHED ROOM 

213 ment. The door clicked loudly and for a moment he leaned against it waiting to hear Sylvia's voice. What should he do if he did hear it and she should come running to the door to bring him back? What could he do if the door were suddenly to fling open and she were to be there with tousled hair and sleep-kissed eyes, with the faint glow of the hall lamp already lighted, caressing the sensuous curves of her naked body; lighting dimly the glorious peach-like color of her bare flesh? 

Would he have strength enough then to want to think clearly out in the cold rain-soaked darkness, or indeed care if he ever thought clearly again when so much rapture lay in muddled thinking,-in merely closing his eyes to the grim horror of thinking?

But the door remained shut and after a full minute of breathless waiting he tore himself away and dragged down the stairs wearily. A cruel lassitude weighted down his limbs, yet he felt he couldn't wait for the little elevator to come up. It was easier to walk down three flights of stairs. And there was something symbolic and final about it too. Step by step he was leaving mad folly behind and going down, down, to face reality on the grim streets with no bowls of roses and no bottles of costly perfume to drug his senses. Outside was only a biting winter rain in whose touch was no caress and a chill winter wind in whose voice was no flattery. 

Sitting on the bench he faced the situation squarely.

He, a man in the worst stages of a disease, had seduced an innocent girl who for some romantic reason had become desperately fond of him. And the word innocent was here no general term to classify all women not exactly promiscuous. Sylvia was a virgin. Somehow he had always felt that such was the case for all her free and easy ways. She was typical of so many modern girls who enter the glamorous world of the theatre, who

FURNISHED ROOM 

speak and act so carelessly, who exhibit their bodies with perfect indifference to public comment, but who remain ultimately pure. That was Sylvia. She was no fool. She probably knew perfectly well that to give herself to Bob Landers meant almost immediate stardom and yet she scorned the idea. But she had given herself to him, completely, triumphantly; and what was he? There was no use trying to hide the facts from himself. He was a nobody in the completest sense of the word, a vagabond who sang with a romantic sob in his voice which had touched some chord in her sentimental heart. She had been happy he should possess her. It had been that simple. 

And there was no excuse for him. He couldn't say she had arranged the meeting for the special purpose of arousing his passion. Suppose it were true, it was entirely beyond the question. Were he in the pride of full health and vigor, then there might have been some excuse. He would have had to be an iceberg to have withstood her flaming charms, once he had roused her passion. He had begun, indeed, by paying her the clever compliments which successful actresses feel to be due them, but soon it had slipped beyond his control. He had done nothing to fight back. His passion had risen like a wave sweeping him off his feet and he had gone flaming with the tide of emotion, heedless of consequences. All the nights of passionate dreams and the agonies of restraint had risen in him and he had crushed her with his thwarted virility.

The ecstasy of it still pulsed in his blood, even now while he sat frozen with horror at his deed. His body flamed with the memory of her responsive passion even as his thoughts raced back over his long months of agony and his mind screamed over and over like a windtossed gull in a storm, "You have given it to her! You

FURNISHED ROOM 

215 have diseased a virgin! You have ruined a young, beautiful life to satisfy an hour's animalism.” 

With a hoarse cry of anguish he started to his feet glaring about wildly. He must do something about it. 

A taxi drew up slowly and he hailed it, running to meet it. The driver looked surprised but said nothing as he climbed in dripping with rain. In a choked voice he gave Dr. Krassin's address. Arrived there he rang the bell three or four times in quick succession. The stupid maid opened the door and he pushed past crying, "Tell the doctor I must see him at once. It is terribly important. 

Is he in?" 

She nodded quickly, obviously frightened by his brusque manner so different from his usual rather elaborate courtesy, and then backed away leaving him to hang up his coat in the hall and go sit in the waiting room. He sank into a deep chair and bit his nails impatiently unsoothed by the gentle ticking of the old grandfather clock he had always admired. It seemed interminable hours though it was really less than five minutes before the door opened behind him and the doctor's suave voice called, "Come in, Mr. Smith. What has happened now?”
