"That is no excuse!" the doctor cried sharply. “You have suffered no worse than hundreds of thousands of others who did not take your course to relief. And you have aggravated your own condition. You know that?" "That doesn't matter. That doesn't matter, now. I only want to know for her sake. What can I do? Must 

I go to her and tell her? Will it do any good if she goes to a doctor at once?" 

He shook his head slowly. "I do not think you should go to her. You might intend to tell her and instead only be guilty again. Such things have happened." "No! God knows I am not that bad."

The doctor rose slowly and faced Tom, who was standing with his hands clenched at his sides. "I mean to accuse you of nothing. I am merely telling you how horrible life can be when we lose our intellectual control. You are horrified at what a stupid ignorant peasant will do, and yet you, a man of culture and refinement, have done the same, and without his excuse of ignorance. You want to know what to do? What can I

FURNISHED ROOM 

217 tell you? Whoever she is, she is in the hands of God.

For you to tell her anything so ghastly might drive her to desperation, to suicide. You can only sit back and wait. If you believe in prayer, there is always that. Now 

I think you might as well go. There is nothing more 

I can say."

Dumbly Tom stumbled out into the entrance hall. 

The doctor helped him on with his overcoat. As he let him out of the door he laid a hand for an instant on his arm and his voice was gentle once more. "As for yourself, don't do anything foolish. Get home directly and get to bed. Another attack of 'flu' would be fatal now. Remember whatever comes you can help the girl most by acting sane."

Tom never knew how he got home but when he entered the hall of his house the landlady popped out of her door and called to him, simperingly. "Oh, you're just too late for a telephone call. A Miss 

Blake called and said you should get in touch with her." 

He managed to stifle a groan. "Thank you, Mrs. 

Tillinghast. But I'm soaking wet and going to bed. I don't want another attack of the 'flu.' I'll see Miss Blake tomorrow." "Can I get you anything, Mr. Smith?" "No thank you, I just need to get warm and go to sleep." 

He closed the door behind him. He dared not wonder what Sylvia must be thinking. He dragged off his wet clothes and crept shivering into bed. The rain sloshed drearily against the window and ran streakily down the long, unwashed panes. Upstairs someone kept pounding intermittently on the radiator crying for more steam.

In the next room someone was playing a bad radio under full power and through horrible shrieks of static a nasal voice drawled, "I'm just a vagabond lover."

ON 

CHAPTER NINETEEN 

N Monday evening Tom returned home feeling tired but much more contented in mind. Viewed in the calmness of a new day there was clearly nothing to do but sit back and wait, and, as far as possible, keep an eye on Sylvia. A strange hope now filled him that she would escape punishment for her indiscretion and would never know how desperate her plight might have been right at the beginning of her career. Certainly the doctor was right and he could say nothing to her to warn her. But at the same time he must keep in touch with her every day to be prepared to help in the event of his hope's being confounded.

That part of his programme he found solved for him by the presence of a note from Sylvia waiting for him. 

Dearest Tom! 

Weren't you the bad boy to run away like that and not come back? I cried myself to sleep, but today I only know I want to see you no matter how bad you are. Here are two tickets for tonight. Come backstage and see me after the show. I can't wait till I see you again. Your Sylvia 

P.S. Bring anyone you want but not that tall girl I saw you with at Sardi's. I'm afraid of her.

Tom smiled faintly at the postscript. She was still such a child. Indeed, that was her charm. As such she had quite naturally resented Anne's poise and quiet beauty. Alas, there was no fear of his bringing Anne anywhere. That lovely dream was shattered hopelessly by 218

FURNISHED ROOM 

219 his stupidity. Perhaps his very seduction of Sylvia had been only an attempt to buoy up his courage when he realized he had lost the most wonderful thing he had ever come near to possessing.

And now he had tangled himself with Sylvia whom he did not love and whom ultimately he could never consider as more than a delightful child. Now that he had sensed a touch of quiet beautiful home life, even the flaming exotic charm of the girl could only tempt him for a moment. That moment had been and if there was no serious consequence it was all for the best. If only he could be sure there were no consequences. He must wait days and days to see. But Sylvia and her troublesome beauty must always have troubled him with her wild appeal to his own passionate nature had things not come to pass as they had. Now he would lust after her no more. For the last time in his life, he hoped, he had come through a wild promiscuous passion and found it unsatisfying. For a few days more the memory of her would pulse in his blood and then it would be gone forever.

He called Alfred on the phone and arranged to meet him at the door of the theatre. When they were safely seated, well down front, Alfred grinned. "My boy, these seats need a lot of explaining. Come, confess and tell me what's up."

Tom waved him aside with some remark about not being silly and soon the performance began. About half way through the first act Sylvia did a solo number and at the end the audience was fairly shouting its appreciation. Tom could not help flushing as she took bow after bow upon finishing the last half of the dance the second time. She was revelling so joyously, so completely in her triumph. Finally she walked right into the footlights and kissed her hand to him. There was no mistaking

FURNISHED ROOM 

the gesture and he waved in response. A buzz of conversation hummed around him as the show went on. 

Alfred chuckled. "So that's it. Now we are flirting with the stars. What will we be next, I wonder? How long has this been going on?" "Don't be silly. She's a lovely girl. You're going to meet her after the performance. Remember, stick to me." 

So when it was over they went round to the stage door and were ushered into Sylvia's dressing room. She flung her arms about Tom's neck crying, "Oh, but 

I'm glad to see you!" Then she greeted Alfred warmly as he was introduced saying, "I'm always glad to meet 

Tom's friends." She looked very sweet in a dark red velvet robe.

A minute later the manager came in with Bob Landers and there were introductions. Bob was evidently in a vile temper, but keeping himself under control. 

The manager kept his eye on Tom especially when Sylvia refused Landers' generous and general invitation to supper and said she was tired and must go straight home. 

Tom looked into her eyes and asked softly. "How do you feel, Sylvia? Quite all right?" "Much better than that, silly, I never felt so good in my life. Are you going to take me home?" "I can't, Sylvia, I've got to get to bed," and as he saw her face fall he added, "I'm not long over the 'flu.' 

Her smile returned. "I forgot that, you poor boy. 

You must be careful, very careful. Will you come to see me here tomorrow night?" Her eyes glowed darkly.

Tom promised and in a minute or two took his departure with Alfred in tow. Sylvia turned to Landers.

FURNISHED ROOM 

221 "Bob, may I change my mind about supper and accept your invitation?" She smiled coyly.

He lifted one of her hands and touched it lightly with his lips. "Darling, you may always change your mind -in my favor."

The men went out while Sylvia dressed. The manager said, "Who's the big guy? She seems stuck on him. 

You know she kissed her hand to him after her number in the first act?" 

Landers nodded slowly. "She's always been crazy about him. Meyers wanted to use him in this show and 

I blocked it. Maybe I was wrong. I think the less she sees him the crazier she gets about him." "Yeah, I remember now. What do we do, be nice to him?" He looked up quizzically. 

Landers nodded again. "Give him the house if he wants it. I got a new idea and it can't be any worse than the other was."

So night after night Tom dropped into Sylvia's dressing room to ask how she felt and night after night his hopes rose higher as she said she felt wonderful and her only worry seemed to be why he wouldn't go home with her and wouldn't even kiss her as she desired. "I do believe you don't love me," she cried. 

He patted her hand gently. "I wouldn't dare to aspire to love you. I am only an errand boy and you are a Broadway celebrity. You must realize that."

She climbed on his knee. She seemed more like a child than ever in the abbreviated costume in which she did her second act dance. She twined her arms about his neck. "Why are you so silly, Tom? When I gave myself to you the other day didn't it tell you how much I love you? Can I say it in any other way to make you believe me? I was so happy when you held me in your arms

FURNISHED ROOM 

and my heart just melted out of my body and into yours. Every night I stretch out my arms to hold you closer to me and you're not there. I want you so much, all the time." "And how do you feel now?" he asked again.

A puzzled frown wrinkled her brow even as she brushed his hair lightly. "Why do you always ask me that?" 

He faltered a bit. "Well, Sylvia, you're not a little girl any more. You're a woman now," he finished lamely. "Oh, Tom, as if I didn't know. No man can ever understand what a change comes into a woman's life. 

How wonderful life can be. I never dreamed it before." 

Then she lowered her voice to a whisper. "Do you mean 

I may have a baby?" "God forbid," he cried hoarsely. "Oh, don't say that. Some time I want a little baby, yours and mine, Tom." She clung to him at the risk of smearing her whole make-up.

But before he could speak there was a sharp rap on the door and a voice cried, "Two minutes, Miss Blake." 

She jumped to her feet, once more an obedient servant of the theatre, and swiftly repaired the damages to her appearance. "Quick, Tom, do I look all right?" "More than lovely, Sylvia." 

With a flashing smile she slipped out of the door to answer her call. Tom walked slowly out into the night.

The glorious miracle had happened and Sylvia had been spared. What she had escaped was something she must never know. Now there was the problem of breaking with her without putting her into the shameful position of seeing herself as merely the plaything of an idle hour.

For if he were not careful it must look like that to anyone who did not know the whole story. In his

FURNISHED ROOM 

223 anxiety about Sylvia's health he had not stopped to gauge how completely she might be absorbed by her foolish passion. There must be some way out. And the best hope lay in the theory that she was really in love with life and passion and the thrill of physical contacts and so might shift to someone else all her devotion to him. Would Landers do?

A few evenings later Tom came out of Sylvia's dressing room just before the beginning of the second act and crossed the back stage toward the stage door. He glanced about him with apparent carelessness and as if heedless of the comments his passing elicited from the various groups scattered about. A sudden smile of satisfaction crossed his face as he saw Landers approaching. 

He was expecting him since Sylvia had turned him away from her dressing room in response to a whispered appeal to say she was busy talking to Tom and would see him later. He had hoped to stir him up and had evidently succeeded. "See here, I want to talk to you." Landers' voice was tight and hard. He was evidently controlling his temper. 'And I want to talk to you, too, so everything seems to be all right. Where shall we go?" cc Landers led the way in silence through the little door leading into the orchestra of the theatre and then along the side aisle and up a flight of stairs to a richly furnished office. Tom noticed the house was packed with the usual enthusiastic crowd, and there were many standees. With a succession of such successes to his credit 

Tom didn't wonder at the opulence of the office furnishings. Landers made himself quite at home and offered a cigar silently. Tom smiled his refusal before accepting a chair. "I suppose you still don't drink? But you don't mind if I do, of course." The sarcasm was thinly veiled.

FURNISHED ROOM 

"I wouldn't if I were you." 

His quiet tone startled Landers like a shot and he slapped shut the drawer he had been opening, a glimpse into which revealed an assortment of bottles. "What the hell-" he began, but Tom continued easily, —Because I want to talk to you. About Sylvia.”

Landers swung back in his chair. "Now we're getting somewhere," he announced grimly. "It seems we've got the same thing on our chests. Shoot first if you want to. It's about time we had a show-down." 

Tom leaned forward with his hands on his knees. "I want to ask you a simple question. What are your intentions toward Sylvia?" 

Landers leaped to his feet as if shot out of a gun and charged toward him. Tom caught him by both wrists and held steadily. "Not quite that way, Landers. You've done many damned fool things in your life, probably, but that would be the worst. That goes especially if Sylvia means anything to you. Now answer my question, please.' 

Something in the low steady tone of voice brought the man to his senses. He answered almost in a whisper. "I mean to marry her the minute she'll have me. I'll do anything in the world for her."

Tom dropped his wrists and sat down easily. "That's what I hoped you'd say. Now play the game for just one more minute with me. You really do love her, don't you; not like the other girls you've had." "Yes." The single word carried conviction entirely. "And you're in a condition to marry a decent girl. 

You know what I mean. I know the money you have and all you can do for her career. I mean are youwell?" "Yes." Again the monosyllable dropped and rang true.

FURNISHED ROOM 

225 "Then you shall have her," he laughed shortly. "Doesn't it sound ridiculous and melodramatic, but someone has to step in before you two fools botch your lives. It's a new rôle for me. I think she really loves you and it's up to you to show her how you feel tonight. You've got to use every ounce of passion you've ever had for her and sweep her off her feet. You've sat back and waited too long, because you thought she was different from every girl you've ever known and therefore you must act different. Oh, I grant you it's a wild speech but this time I know. "Now listen to me. Sylvia is expecting me at her apartment at twelve-thirty tonight. Now don't fly off the handle again; it won't help any. You're going to go in my place and tell her you met me and I said I couldn't come because I had another date. Remember it is date, not appointment or engagement. If you need details you might add the young lady was tall and slender with light brown hair and grey eyes." He paused a moment as a wave of memory swept over him. 

Landers' eyes opened wide. "It sounds like the girl with you at Sardi's last fall. Sylvia saw her." "That's the one. There is no meeting, of course, but you'll know what to say. Tell her I'm a dirty unappreciative rat with any decorations you may find appropriate. But don't lay it on too thick. Then go in and win. Make her feel how a passionate man can love a girl when he's really roused. After all she wants a man, not a mopping stick."

For half a minute there was a dead silence in the room. Tom sat leaning forward with his eyes on Landers and the latter sat staring at the fine Oriental weaving of the rug. Slowly his eyes raised and there was a strange light in them that would have been hard to classify.

FURNISHED ROOM 

"Maybe you're right," he said slowly. "Anyhow you're a strange man. I never could make you out. What are you doing this for? What's the game?"

Sylvia to be happy. Because she wants passion, success, money, all the things you can give her. And then, you see, I really do love the other girl. Maybe that makes a difference." 

Landers held out his hand. "I hope you win her." "Thanks."

They passed out through the front of the theatre. 

Tom turned west toward Eighth Avenue. He wanted to walk for a few minutes, but not along Broadway. 

Tonight he didn't want the pushing, good-natured, after-theatre crowd. He didn't want to speculate as to which theatre would first flaunt Sylvia's name in electric lights.

The boys nowadays would take the shoes off his feet while he was walking his beat. How about it, Tom?" 

He agreed with a grin. He hadn't heard such a ridiculous discussion off the stage in years and they had quite lost sight of the fact that they still knew nothing about what he was doing in Wall Street. TOM 

CHAPTER TWENTY 

OM lay in bed looking at his alarm clock which pointed to one minute to seven. It was rather funny how he set the alarm every night for seven o'clock and yet it never had rung. He always woke up quite clear-headed a minute or two before the hour. He sat up in bed and pressed down the little knob which cut off the bell, and as he did so there was a sharp rap on the door. In answer to his command the door swung open and Landers walked in. "Hello," cried Tom, "This is a surprise.' وو Landers shook hands and Tom saw his eyes were sparkling and he looked years younger than the man he had been last night. "Did I wake you up?" "No, indeed. I woke up just before you rapped. Seven o'clock is my hour. Hard working man, you know."

Landers sat on the edge of the bed and put an arm around his shoulder. "Tom, I've just come from Sylvia, now. I'm so happy I can't breathe ordinary air. We are going for the marriage license this morning." "I'm very glad, Bob. I thought I knew all along." 

Landers' voice shook. "Tom, I owe it all to you. I thought I was mad last night when I forced my way in and rushed her off her feet before she had time to cry about you. She fought me off for a long time but 

I made her come to me at last, and this morning, a little while ago, she said she'd marry me." "I hope you'll both be happy." "But how she hates you, now. She said you ditched her and I don't know what else. That's the rotten part of it all. Now that I know what a great guy you are, she says you're a bum. Ain't it funny?" "She's right, Bob, there's no doubt about that. Women 230

FURNISHED ROOM 

231 are usually right about such things when they're very mad. They seem to see clearer that way." He got out of bed and started to dress quickly. Landers shivered a little. "I should think you'd freeze dressing in here." "I suppose I'm used to it. I've learned to do a quick job of it." He grinned and fixed his razor. 

Landers glanced about him. "So this is where you've been living. This is a terrible place. You can't go on living here any more. We'll find you something else today." "It's all I can afford and I really can't afford this.” 

The man was silent a moment. "See here. Don't go to work today. Meyers is casting a new musical show soon and I'll send you to him today. To Hell with whatever this job is you have now. I'll make Meyers give you a good part and he won't mind because he liked you anyway when you sang for him. I'll stake you till the show opens. What do you say?"

Tom drew the razor down his cheek with a steady hand. "Thanks ever so much, but I've made up my mind to stick to business. I'd be afraid to try to live by my voice. There are too many slips, many too many.' 

"You haven't forgiven me for making Meyers drop you for that other show, have you?" "It isn't a case for forgiving. At the time it caused me to suffer terribly, to go hungry, half starved for weeks and weeks. I hated you at the time as much as 

I've ever hated anyone in my life. I accepted meals and loans from you because I was starving and because I hated you so much." 

For a moment he was silent while he shaved his upper lip with quick light strokes. Then he went on. "I don't know why I tell you all this except that I have always wanted to tell you. Then through my sufferings I found a new hope and a new ideal, and right now I think you

FURNISHED ROOM 

did me a favor that day. I probably shouldn't have been able to keep up with the work for reasons I needn't go into. I've been enjoying rotten health, thank you," and he grinned. "I've decided finally I want a job, I want to work and be free of all the emotional glamour." He finished by dousing his head in cold water and then began to towel vigorously.

Landers sat still until he had finished. "I don't know whether I'm glad or sorry about the way you feel. You make it hard for me to help you and I want to help you. I needn't say I'm sorry about before. You know 

I am. Now what can I do to help you? I'm afraid I haven't much influence in Wall Street."

Tom held out his hand which the other grasped warmly. "Now there we are. That makes us even. If you really want to do something very badly you can buy my breakfast since this is pay day and like most unmarried men I never seem to be able to arrange for breakfast on pay day.” 

They left the house two or three minutes later.

As the days went on, Tom found that by regulating his life very carefully the walking was not too much of a strain and he even began to show definite signs of improvement in his physical condition. He told Dr. 

Krassin as much as he thought necessary of the affair with Sylvia. The doctor shook his head sadly. دو "You see how lucky you and she were that time, but remember it would be too much to ask again. "Never fear. I have learned my lesson this time, I hope, once and for all.” 

Then one day he saw in all the papers that the rising young dancer, Sylvia Blake, had married the well known play-boy, Robert Z. Landers. The bride said that they would delay their honeymoon until the end of the run

FURNISHED ROOM 

233 of the musical comedy in which the talented dancer was now appearing. Seral papers went on further to say that it was quite easy for the wise ones to predict now that the fall would find a new star's name blazing along Broadway in very large letters. One paper went still further and said that in a private interview in her suite at the Hotel Elysée, Miss Blake repudiated the rumor that the summer might find her in Hollywood. "I wouldn't like just being a picture to my audiences.

I feel I need the personal contact of the stage in order to put forth my best efforts." Her picture accompanied the interview.

Tom folded the paper carefully and put it out of sight in the chair next to him. John Honan ran a hand through his mop of red hair and grinned across the luncheon table at him. "I see you were reading about that dancer catching one of the rich boys. Say how do they ever do it?” "I don't know," Tom smiled. "Maybe she deserved the best there was. They sometimes do, you know." "Yeah? Well I'd like to believe it. Maybe this Jane was some friend of yours, just one of the little playmates," and he laughed mockingly. "Oh, no, but I've seen her dance. She does that well."

While John ran off on some other subject Tom's mind wandered off to Sylvia. How happy she must be now, with a passionate lover at her command and a whole world of triumphs suddenly opened up to her. It would be like Landers to install her at once in a hotel of exactly the right sort like the Elysée. He could see Sylvia sitting with her feet drawn up under her explaining why she felt she had rather be on the stage than in the moving pictures. She would fascinate the reporter without doubt and he would quote her generously. After only one season in New York Sylvia was a real success.

When he got home that night he found a letter from 

Landers and a small package. Hopened the letter first. 

It said: 

Dear Tom: 

I am happier than I ever hope to be or have any right to be, and I know to whom I owe it. Since you won't take something more substantial I am sending you a watch to replace your 

Ingersoll. And one thing more. If you're ever in trouble you know where to come and if you're too damned proud, well, the watch is hockable at any pawnshop. Someone said “Time is money." 

Bob Landers

He opened the package carefully and drew out a very thin, plain-looking watch of beautiful design. The name Pettig-Phillippe told the whole story. It was worth hundreds of dollars. He slipped it into his pocket with a smile. He never need go hungry again. It was the most poignant sign yet of the better times to be. 

And it was perhaps the pressure of the watch against his body that gave him confidence and faith in himself and drove him into Mr. Dale's bank one day and made him ask for that gentleman. He was directed to the seventh floor. Once there he again asked to see Mr. Dale.

The guard, a strapping young fellow with a smug uniform and a revolver slung nonchalantly about his waist, was very doubtful if Mr. Dale could be seen, but after looking Tom over carefully he did call an office boy.

The latter was quite sure Mr. Dale would see no one except by appointment but he was also shrewd and prided himself on a long memory. He frowned a second in thought. "Did you say your name was Tom Smith?" 

Tom nodded. "And didn't you have an appointment one afternoon a few weeks ago?"

FURNISHED ROOM 

235 

Tom smiled and said "Yes." "Then you wait and I'll see what I can do."

Tom opined and the other nodded, his dignity now quite lost. "That's the truth. But a job's a job these days and you take what you can get. What do you do?" "Run stock for Faulkner Brothers just now."

The guard straightened up again and sniffed scornfully. "That ain't a job at all. It's for kids." 

Tom shrugged his shoulders. "You're right, but you also said these days you take what you can get. That was right, too."

Before the guard had time to expand his opinions the office boy was at the mahogany rail again, holding the gate open respectfully. His manner was deferential. "Will you come right in, Mr. Smith. Mr. Dale will be delighted to see you at once."

FURNISHED ROOM 

Now sit down and let's see what we can do about things."

As Tom sank gratefully into a wide chair the office boy reached the guard who was scratching his head. "Gee, he got right in to see the old man.' "Sure, I knew he would," cried the office boy wisely. "Me, I always remember names. It comes in handy sometimes." "Say, he's only a runner in a broker's house. He told me so. That's only a kid's job." "Keep your shirt on. What does it matter what he does now. Remember the day I told you the old man's daughter was cryin' in the office. Well that's the day this guy didn't come and that's the reason I figgers this is the guy she was cryin' for. And what dames cries for they gets." "Gee, I can't imagine anybody cryin' over him." "Sure you can't and that's why you'll never get along, Mike. You ain't got no imagination." 

That night after dinner Mr. Dale spoke to Anne. "What is the matter with my darling these days? You seem so tired and listless and I haven't heard you laugh in ever so long." "It must be the warmer days," burst in Mrs. Dale quickly. "There is a touch of false spring in the air that seems to strip me of energy." Her tone denied her words. 

Mr. Dale smiled at his wife but turned again to 

Anne. "And do you find it the same?" "No, father. I'm quite all right and feel perfectly well. Do you want me to play something for you? I know I've neglected you in that way lately." "That's no matter, child. Tonight I think I should entertain. Now what shall I talk about?" "I know," cried Mrs. Dale mockingly. "Tell us

FURNISHED ROOM 

237 something about the bank. You're sure to anyway." "Am I such a banking bore then?" "No, indeed." She reached out and patted her husband on the arm. "The fault is with me that I don't appreciate your words of wisdom like a financial editor of the Sun would, or someone like that. And John, you know you do love to talk shop even though you won't admit it." "Very well, Olive, then I will admit it, and since you think I do it well I guess I had better talk banking to entertain Anne. Should I tell you about an interesting experience I had today, Anne?" "Yes, father." She smiled faintly. "Hmm! That sounds much more obedient than enthusiastic but I must take such feeble encouragement as I get." He grinned slyly, then settled down comfortably in his chair. "Well, I had a very interesting talk with a young man who came to me today looking for a position. Of course, I don't usually see men looking for work; we have a department that handles that most efficiently-at least I think so. But I did have an idle quarter of an hour and I said to myself, 'Now see here, John Dale, maybe if you talk to this man in the right way you'll learn something about the difficulties a young man has locating himself during these hard times.' You see I have begun to realize that, shut up in an almost air-proof office on the seventh floor of a building where one is never out of sight of several uniformed guards, it is quite possible that I am losing contact with the-er-man in the street, and especially the young man who is trying to place himself in these most difficult times. Now that is a real problem-but I said that before, didn't I, Anne?" "Yes, father," she replied dully, and continued to look out of the window by which she sat. Mrs. Dale,

FURNISHED ROOM 

with quietly folded hands watched her husband with a puzzled frown between her eyes. He looked at her blandly and continued with still more marked pomposity. "When the young man came in I saw he wasn't such a young man at all; that is I would not call him a young man, though the term is naturally open to many interpretations. If one really means a young man to be one who is not old, that is one idea. Or else one may use the word merely meaning one who is too young to be called simply a man. It is a most interesting point to discuss, don't you think so, Olive?” "Yes, John, but do get on with your story. I've never known you to be so long-winded." "Olive, I distinctly resent the word long-winded. I am merely trying for niceties of distinction, a true search for the 'mot juste' which is always justified, or at least I think so. At any rate the man was a fine upstanding fellow of about thirty and he told me a great deal about himself and his difficulties in getting employment that supplied, shall we call it 'a living wage.' He was quite talented, I think I should have sensed that even if he had not explained so carefully, but his talents are of the sort which are most difficult to market successfully and so he was 'up against it.' I believe those were his exact words. Anne, dear, are you listening?" "Yes, father." The words seemed to come from outside in the garden where she was looking. "All right then, because I didn't want you to miss any of this interesting story. It is such a great problem we have to face now of the young man who is educated culturally and spiritually beyond his earning capacity. 

Well, to be very brief I became interested in the young man and made up my mind to really do something for him. Our talk lasted well over an hour and at the end

FURNISHED ROOM 

239 I think I sent him away very happy. I told him to report Monday morning for work in our bond department."

He settled deeper into his chair and cut a cigar carefully. "I felt he had a real broadness of vision, real mental scope, in spite of having had no experience, and I feel sure he will ultimately be a real asset to our organization. I felt that keenly enough so that I decided to take a long chance and start him at a fair salary immediately so he can adjust himself to his new conditions. I went so far even as to loan him a little money for clothes and things so he can start in fresh and clean on Monday morning as if it were a new life, as I hope it will be for him. I feel I have not made a mistake in this young man." He puffed his cigar confidently, and with vast content.

Mrs. Dale stared. "Well it certainly is a strange story. 

John Dale, I can't imagine you doing such a thing.” "It is rather funny but wait until you see the chap and then you'll like him too. You see I've invited him for Sunday dinner and I'm sure he'll be very entertaining. I don't think he gets out very often and it's good for him." 

Mrs. Dale gasped. "For Sunday dinner, John? Anne, will you talk to your father; I'm past words." "What does it matter, mother?" Anne smiled wearily. "If father wants to play Santa Claus, let him play."

Mr. Dale looked hurt though he could scarcely keep the twinkle out of his eyes. "Anne, I'm surprised at you. 

You haven't even asked his name.' 

"I'm really not interested, father. You must forgive me if I'm not, but there it is." "Dear me, what strange women," he mocked, "I never heard of such a lack of curiosity." 

Mrs. Dale tapped her foot impatiently. "John, you

FURNISHED ROOM 

are certainly in a quaint humor tonight. All right, tell us then. What is the name of the new Shining Light?" "Tom Smith." He spoke the two words quietly. 

Anne jumped to her feet, her face white with emotion. "Father! what are you saying?" "Anne, darling, I'm trying to explain to you that 

Tom Smith came to see me today; that he is coming to work for me Monday morning; that he is coming here for Sunday dinner." 

For a second the girl stood quivering, then fell sobbing to her knees with her face buried in her hands on the seat of the chair from which she had just risen. Mrs. 

Dale rushed across to her and kneeling beside her took her tenderly into her arms. She turned to her husband reproachfully. "John, how could you be so cruel. I should have realized all that foolish talk was leading up to something. Why couldn't you tell us right out?” "Olive, I wanted to find out something in my own way. I wanted to be sure how Anne felt. I am sure now. 

I'm completely satisfied." He crossed the room and raised his wife and daughter gently. Then he held out his arms to Anne. "And you you do understand I didn't just mean to torture you? I mean something so much more than I've said. I mean I want my girl and Tom Smith to be happy together. Can I say any more?" 

The girl let her father hold her in his arms. "Father, nothing in the world could mean any more to me than that. Now tell me everything." "There isn't much to say, Anne. I tried to do what you wanted to do and he wouldn't let me. I offered him an income in Europe to study singing and he replied he wanted to sing only for pleasure. I offered him an income so he might settle in Oxford or Florence and write as he talked of doing when he first came here, and he

FURNISHED ROOM 

241 said no, he felt he wanted to write only for pleasure. "Then he went on to explain his change of mind toward all business and, indeed, toward all life. He said he wanted a settled income so he could marry and have the peaceful quiet life he had come to yearn for. Oh, he was very clear and very simple about it. So I offered him a post in Shanghai, China, with a big salary attached and he said he no longer wanted the far places but just quiet home life. To test him I said I couldn't help him in New York for some time to come and now 

I am going to give you his exact answer to that. He said, "Then I must continue as I am because I can't leave New York. My whole life now consists in trying to get one thing and that thing is here.'" "Is that what he said, father?" "Exactly. And I said, 'May I know what that is?' 

He said, 'Some day when I have proved myself, I hope to ask you for your daughter's hand in marriage.' Just as formal as that he was.' 

Anne's eyes were sparkling. "And what did you say to that?" 

He kissed her forehead gently. "I think I remember my words. I said, 'When Anne and you come to me hand in hand and say you love each other, I shall be happy to call you my son.' I forget what he said after that. There were a great many words, but finally he's coming to dinner Sunday, and he'll probably remember most of his fine words and even have plenty to add to them. Now is my girl happy?" "Yes, yes, yes," and she burst into a flood of tears. 

Mrs. Dale took her away from her father. "Come with me, darling, you've finally puzzled your father. 

When will men ever learn that when a woman cries she's perfectly happy." And mother and daughter wept together.

CHAPTER TWENTY-ONE 

'HAT same evening Tom took Alfred to dinner and THA same one the theatre. It was a strange sensation to be able to entertain anyone even in the most modest way and he savored it to the utmost. After the play they returned to Tom's room to talk. "What's the matter, Al, you seem terribly depressed. 

You don't seem overjoyed at my good fortune at all." 

Alfred smiled feebly. "Maybe it's this ghastly red room. Of course, you're going to get away at once." 

Tom faced him seriously. "Strangely enough I'm not going to leave here. When I go away I want it to be into an entirely new life. Then I want to forget this room and everything connected with it as if it had never been. In this room I have suffered and out of that suffering was born the new ideal. I am being unbelievably lucky now in being allowed to stand up and strive for my new ideal. But as long as I am sick, tainted with the loose, foolish life of the past, I shall stay here where that foolishness reached its culmination. I suppose it sounds idiotic but that's the way I feel about it. I shall leave this dreary place like a butterfly leaves a cocoon and in no other way." 

Alfred sighed. "And then there is the bigger thing, too. You were offered a chance to study music abroad, and you threw it away. Tom, think of Paris and Munich and Dresden and Prague and Rome and all you're giving up. You're limiting your life here to slaving in an office 242

FURNISHED ROOM 

243 when you might be roaming the world singing. Why do you deliberately choose a little narrow life when you might have a big spacious one?" "You don't know Anne." Tom smiled with quiet assurance. "I only want a simple life with her. I mean just that. I don't want any of the glamour you predict, even if I thought I should be so very successful, which there is every reason to doubt." "But, Tom, why are you so blind? Anne is a musician. You tell me she almost lives for her music. Don't you see you must mean more to her as a musician than as anything else?" "That's a very clever argument, Al, but it won't do. 

I've tried it on myself. If Anne loves me as I hope she does, she loves me for the petty, worthless thing which is me, and not because I sob so gracefully on a top Bflat. I've had a taste of that love and I know how little it means in the end. Our music will mean more to us since it is not professionalized, and I am not such a fool as to think I am robbing the world of anything. In 

Anne's case it might have been different. Hers is a great talent and she has a great soul that would have made every sacrifice demanded of her art. But that can't be and she is resigned. No, I want a regular job, a regular home, a regular sweet kind wife to welcome me, a regular wife with whom I can go quietly down through the years. Just that." "You're lost, Tom. I hoped for such fine things from you. You faced the world so bravely with your head held so high in the face of its indifference. You had the spark of glorious life in you that was an inspiration to others, to me. You laughed at the storms and flung your scorn up to the stars. You were the reincarnation of some gay troubadour of the past. Now you're a bank clerk. You want a little home in the suburbs.

FURNISHED ROOM 

You want to go see your mother-in-law every Thursday night. You want to catch the 8:15 every morning after a breakfast spent discussing why your coffee isn't as good as it used to be. You want to have a little bridge party every alternate Saturday night and play golf in the afternoon." He ended up almost on a snort. "Tom, don't you see what you are headed for?" "Yes, Al, and I like it. I want peace, quiet, real love, security, all the things I've never had. I don't want to hurl any more questions at the stars or greet any more sunrises from a park bench. I want a home, a good bed to sleep in, good food to eat, happy friends to enjoy my home with me. Sometimes I'm afraid I've always wanted these things, that all the swashbuckling romanticists really want them, deep down in their hearts, that they only mock them because they feel them to be unattainable. Some day you'll change, too." "Never, Tom, never, never, never. I wouldn't exchange a single dream for all you've mentioned." 

And so they separated for the night but it was soon clear to Alfred that Tom meant every word he said.

As the weeks went by he settled deeper and deeper into his work and his devotion to Anne. And as the months went by and warm weather came Mr. and Mrs. Dale came to look upon Tom as already their son whose only strange idea that persisted was that he would not leave his gruesome room and move into more comfortable quarters. 

One night when he was riding with Anne she laughed and said, "Your dingy red room must have some strange attraction for you. Surely you don't need to live there now." "Yes, I do. I shan't leave there until I step into a new life." She stopped the car and the darkness drew them together.

FURNISHED ROOM 

245 "But isn't this a new life, now? I wish somehow I were a magician and could wipe all memory of the past from your mind. Sometimes when we're alone I see your face suddenly change and I know some black memory has come out of the past to haunt you. Would it make it any easier for you if you told me? You have no need to fear sharing it with me. Nothing could change my love for you. I think you know that." 

He bent over tenderly and kissed her hand resting on the steering wheel of the car. "Yes, Anne, but it is really nothing. And don't ever want to make me forget the past. Because I suffered then I can be happy now, very, very happy, Anne. By a new life I mean when you are my wife. I want that to change everything inwardly and outwardly."

One evening in June Tom paid his last visit to Dr. 

Krassin. The doctor shook hands at parting. "My boy, you have suffered and come out triumphantly. Now you are absolutely, unconditionally well.

Let no one ever try to tell you otherwise. I have given you every test known to science and you are safe. Now get married and be happy." "And you still say I did right in never explaining my condition even to Mr. Dale?" "Exactly right. From now on you are to forget it has ever been." He held out his hand. "And there is no danger, not even for the children?” "Absolutely none. I would welcome you into my own family. I can't say any more, can I?" 

In August Tom and Anne were quietly married at her home. Shortly before the wedding Tom took Alfred to the Dale's house. "Anne, I want you to meet the only friend out of the past I shall ever present to you. I'm not sure I don't owe him my life or at least all my chance of happiness."

FURNISHED ROOM 

Anne shook hands. "So you're the man who took care of Tom when he had ‘flu’? I've heard such wonderful things about you. Won't you be our friend now even as you've been just Tom's friend in the past?" 

Alfred bowed deeply. "I hope I shall be so lucky."

Alfred was the best man and there was no other friend of Tom's. And Tom suddenly realized how many friends Anne had whom he had not met because they had been neglected for him. He mentioned it to her. "Dearest, you have many black memories, so you say, and they can't come. I have so many, shall we say, white memories and they all have to come. But ultimately there is just you and me and mother and father and your Alfred. Do you think he'll let us take possession of him like that?" 

Tom laughed. "He's a very romantic soul, Anne. I've told you I disappointed him in not being another Villon." "But Villon was hanged, wasn't he?" "Oh, that means nothing to Al. If I could sing a better song or write a better story he'd wish the same for me. Or for himself. He has a big enough soul for that.” 

She shuddered playfully. "You positively terrify me.

Does he go about with some gruesome 'claim of the ideal' like the horrible young man in the Wild Duck?" "No, no, nothing like that. He's a mirthful pessimist. 

When we come back we'll win him over with songs. 

They're his greatest weakness." 

She clapped her hands crying, "Music hath charms,” and Tom smiled in reply as some guests came up to claim them.

Among the wedding presents was a huge chest of silverware which drew everyone's eyes. Anne opened it and there was just a card which said,

FURNISHED ROOM 

247 

Dear Tom and Anne: 

May you both be as happy as we are. Bob. 

Anne exclaimed at the beauty of the gift. "Why, who is Bob? I'm sure I don't know him." "Anne, he is only something out of the past. Like me he has found happiness which he didn't deserve, and through a twist of circumstances he feels I helped him win that happiness. Also when I was very low in spirits he gave me a watch, you know, that very beautiful one you have always admired and been curious about. When 

I finally summoned up courage to go to see your father after I had been so very stupid I held that watch in my hand as if I were holding a kind of talisman. He only caught your name from the papers but you may be sure his wishes are sincere. And last of all he is rich enough to give sumptuous presents."

Some hours later they stood in the Grand Central Station in the midst of the crowd waiting for the Montreal Express. They were going to cross Canada on their wedding trip and then go down to California for a short visit with one of Anne's old aunts who lived in Pasadena. They were alone now in the midst of the scurrying throng. Anne, for all her life of independence, clung to Tom. He stood pale and distracted with gloomy features and troubled eyes. Suddenly he cried: "Anne, I must leave you with the porter a second. 

I have to make a telephone call." 

Before she could answer he plunged into the crowd and was gone. She stood on tip-toe trying to see him but he vanished as if the crowd had swallowed him up.

When he reached the phone booth he rang a number hastily. A smooth voice came over the wire.

FURNISHED ROOM 

"Yes." Even at sound of the voice his tension seemed to relax. "Dr. Krassin, I had to call you again. I have to make sure. I was married today, as I wrote you I would be.

I am in Grand Central Station now on the way to Montreal on my honeymoon. Doctor, I am almost frantic. 

Should I go through with this? Am I really fit to live with a decent girl?" "My dear boy, after all my explanations you still don't understand. You are absolutely well. There is no lingering hidden ailment. It is gone, all gone as if it had never been. Am I making myself clear this time? 

I would give my daughter or sister to you in marriage with perfect assurance. Did you go to Dr. Tate as I suggested?" "Yes. He said exactly the same." "As I told you, and if you were a doctor, my boy, you would know no one doubts that man's word. 

Now go along and be happy. Write me when you can."

Slowly he left the booth and wended his way through the crowd. Anne greeted him with puzzled eyes. "Is anything the matter, Tom?" 

He kissed her full on the lips with all the fervor of his warm nature. "Everything is all right, Anne,—now. 

It only remains to be happy." 

And smiling into each other's eyes they passed through the gates to the waiting train. 

At the very moment Anne and Tom stepped on the train, Mrs. Tillinghast flung open the door of the red room to show a slim young man what she had vacant. 

He flung down two bags and looked about grimly. "Is this all you have?" "Yes, and it's a fine room too. The last young man

FURNISHED ROOM 

249 kept it until today when he was married. He married a very rich girl, too." She nodded primly.

The slim young man shrugged his shoulders. "Well, some people have luck. I don't know anybody here and it's damn lonely. Wish I was back in Detroit." "Well, you're not," snapped Mrs. Tillinghast, "so do you want the room or don't you?" "All right, I'll take it." He paid the money out of a thin, shabby wallet. 

Mrs. Tillinghast took it greedily. "And I suppose your name is Smith too?" Her tone was at its most acidulous. 

The slim young man grinned. "No, I'm Jones. John Jones." "I thought so." She sniffed her way out.

The man took off his coat, shirt, collar and tie and sat in his undershirt. Then he kicked off his shoes and lay down to read a paper he had with him. It was not ten minutes later when there was a knock on the door.

He jumped up to open it and a young woman in a kimono staggered in crying, "Hello there, hello, Tom. Got a drink?"

The slim young man looked her over slowly from her bare feet and slippers to her tousled hair and half-shut eyes. Then he grinned slowly. "Sit down, baby. You don't look like you needed a drink. You've had plenty." He pronounced it "plenny."

She sat down heavily on the edge of the bed. "No plenty. Never had plenty. Who are you anyway?” "Johnny Jones. Detroit's my town. Who're you?" "That don't matter a damn. The question is, have you got a drink?" She grinned vacuously. 

He ran his tongue quickly over his dry lips and his eyes kept fastened on her. "Sure I have. That is if you ain't in no hurry to go."

FURNISHED ROOM 

He knelt down to open one of the bags and she rumpled his hair clumsily. "I ain't never in no hurry when there's a drink around. What you got?" "Scotch. Just bought it in case." 

She held out her hand gaily. "Boy, are you as welcome as the flowers of Spring. Welcome to our city." She waved both arms to indicate spacious welcome.

He handed her the bottle and she took a deep swig while he sat down beside her running his eyes over her body. He laughed shortly. "Maybe I'll be glad I came. 

I wasn't sure I was going to like New York." "Oh, you'll like it. It's a great place. Gee, but it's hot in here." She picked up his paper and fanned. "Why not be comfortable then?" he said softly and began to slip her kimono up over her shoulders. The fading daylight gleamed on her white flesh. He kissed her neck and rubbed his cheek along her breast with a deep sigh. She pushed him away drunkenly but let her single garment fall further open. She felt his hand on her thigh. “Gee, but you're a fast worker. Say, Big Boy, let's have a drink first."

He handed her the bottle and as she swung back her head in a deep drink he eased her back upon the pillows where he had been lying. The discarded paper crumpled under her body. 

Outside a high boyish voice sang loudly: 

East side, west side, All around the town 

THE END
